"There's the Longshore tomorrow," said Aske. "And you'll be on it with your hands in your pockets, or you'll not be on it at all." He stood up. "You did three minutes and eight seconds with a cracked rib. I've written that on the card as well. I thought you'd want it there."

---

There were eleven fours still to run.

Kiva had not thought about that. She sat on the quay with her back against the lockkeeper's hut and a blanket round her that somebody had put there, and her boots off, and her feet in Anwen's lap being rubbed by Anwen's hands, which were nearly as cold as her feet. She looked at the chamber. The lockkeeper had shut his shutters. The water was going out through the tail sluices into the basin, slowly, with a sound like a bath draining. The Board's men in grey were standing on the stair waiting for it to go down, with a dry sailcloth folded over their arms.

"Eleven," said Anwen. "And the last of them's the Ropewalk."

"I know."

"Halden. The Tarn. Old Four. Twenty-Eight."

"I know who they are."

"I'm saying it so I'll stop thinking it." Anwen rubbed her foot. "Three and eight is good. It's very good. The best before us was three and twenty-seven."

"Three and eight is a time," said Kiva. "Somebody'll beat it."

The fourteenth run went. It was a four Kiva did not know, in Westerley's sea blue, and they took the stair and came in at three and twenty-six. The fifteenth was Holloway in dark red. They took the ladder with a rope and were clean and quick, three and twenty-one, and the quay made its warm low sound.

The sixteenth was Team Twenty-Eight.

Kiva watched Dray go down the stair onto the chamber floor on two feet, without a stick. She had not seen her run since the autumn. She did not run now. She walked to the chalk line at the tail with Marten and the others and stood there with her face like a shut door, and when the bell went she ran.

She ran well. She ran with a limp, slight, on the left, and nobody who had not seen her in a chair in the ninth month would have known it was a limp. Twenty-Eight took the stair with a litter and a good strong Hold under the cloth. At the spar Dray cut both lashings with one stroke of her edge, the line going taut and parting together, and Kiva heard Jude, beside her on the quay, say *oh* under his breath.

"Three and nineteen," said the marshal.

Dray came up the stair afterward and walked past the lockkeeper's hut. She looked down at Kiva sitting against it in a blanket with her bare feet in Anwen's lap. She did not stop.

"Your captain's got a cracked rib," she said, going past. "The whole quay heard it."

"He has."

"Good climb," said Dray, and went on.

Old Four went at three and sixteen, on the stair, very solid. Brask carried the Purser across his shoulders the whole length of the chamber through water to his waist and never once put him down, and Old Four's Hold walked a wall beside him the whole way to keep the spray off the painted face. They were clean. They were fast. They were eight seconds slower than three minutes and eight.

"Eight seconds," said Jude.

He had sat up. He was sitting on the quay beside the Purser, who nobody had yet taken away, with his left arm held against his chest in his right hand.

"Your arm," said Kiva.

"It's frosted. It was frosted already." Jude did not look at it. "It's not worse. It's just mine. I'd want it said I leaned on it once, for a loop, and I'd do it again." He looked at the chamber. "Eight seconds. That's Brask. Brask's the strongest carry in Meridian."

The Board's men spread a dry sailcloth. They lashed the spar. The water went out.

"*The Tarn,*" said the Clerk. "*First four.*"

Trewin went down the stair as he did everything, without hurry.

He stood at the chalk line with his four in brown and buff behind him and his hat in his hand, and he gave his hat to the marshal to hold, and nodded to him, slowly, as though nodding were a thing that had to be done properly. The marshal took the hat. Somebody on the quay laughed, not unkindly.

The bell went.

Kiva had not seen the Tarn carry before. She had seen them in a lane, a square that came down a pen without any of its corners changing place. They came down the chamber in that square now, and at the Purser they did a thing that she had not expected. They did not lift the cloth. Their Hold put her ward under the near edge and their blade put the flat of his edge on the far edge, the leaded hem, pressing it down into the water, so that when the ward lifted, the cloth folded on itself and did not slide. It was the same thing Kiva had done with her boots. It was slower, because two people were doing it. It was cleaner.

"They watched you," said Anwen.

"They'd have done it anyway."

"They watched you," said Anwen again, "and they did it with a blade instead of a door."

Trewin took the spar off the Purser's shins with his still door planted, and set it down, and then he did a thing that made the whole quay go quiet. He bent and picked up the Purser by himself and put him across his shoulders, the way a shepherd carries a sheep, with an arm and a leg in each hand. Then he turned round and walked the fifty paces back down the chamber to the tail stair, through water that was over his knees, at a walk.

He did not run. He did not need to. Kiva watched his feet. He planted on every step he took.

She had not seen anybody do that. She did not think anybody could. A still door is a slow thing; it goes down and holds, and you do not walk with it any more than you walk with a post. Trewin walked with it. He put his foot down, and planted, and the water broke round his boot as round a post, and he shut and lifted the other foot, and planted. It was slow. It was so slow that the quay began to murmur. And not once did the Purser's face come within a foot of the water, because the water could not move Trewin an inch.

At the stair he went up the steps the same way, a step and a plant, a step and a plant.

"That's what you did," said Jude, in a low voice. "On the ladder. Only he's doing it on the floor."

"He's doing it on fifty paces," said Kiva. "And a stair. And nobody's saying *set* to him."

The Tarn's four came along the quay behind him at a run. Trewin went the last twenty paces to the mark at his walk, and laid the Purser down on it, quite gently, on his back, and straightened up.

"*Three minutes and twelve,*" said the marshal. "*Clean.*"

The quay roared. It was the first time it had roared all morning. Kiva saw people on the lockkeeper's roof stamping. She saw a woman in brown and buff weeping openly by the hut. And she saw Trewin take his hat back from the marshal and put it on, and look along the quay, slowly, until he found her sitting against the hut in her blanket. He nodded to her once.

She nodded back.

"Four seconds," said Anwen. Her hands had stopped on Kiva's foot. "He was four seconds off. Carrying it on the floor."

"He was safer than we were."

"He was safer than anybody's ever been." Anwen looked at Trewin standing by the mark. "He didn't need a word. He never moves when he doesn't mean to."

---

Halden License went second to last.

Kiva watched Kellan go down the stair in his black and gold, and stand on the chalk line, and not look up. Pettit declared. The bell went.

Halden took the ladder. They did not take a rope. Hesketh's ward went under the cloth's near edge, and Kellan, Kiva saw, put his edge flat on the far edge, as the Tarn had, pressing the lead down. They had watched too. Pettit took the spar. And then Halden did a thing that was neither Seven's nor the Ropewalk's. Pettit and Yarrow went up the ladder side by side, one on each edge of the iron rungs, with the Purser between them in the Board's sling, one loop over Pettit's shoulder and one over Yarrow's. They climbed together, rung for rung, with Kellan below them on the rungs calling *left, right, left,* and the Purser came up the wall between them like a sack between two porters on a stair.

It was quick. It was very quick. Nobody planted anything. Nobody needed to.

"Three and ten," said the marshal. "Clean."

"Two seconds," said Jude.

He had got up. He was standing by the hut with his arm held against his chest and his face set. Kiva had never seen him look like that. He looked like a man watching a pot that he had been told would not boil.

"Two seconds," he said again. "And the Ropewalk."

The Ropewalk's first four went last.

They had watched every run. Kiva had seen them watching from the end of the rope, Carrow with her arms folded and her quick blunt face giving nothing away, and Treen beside her with her eyes on the chamber the whole morning, never once looking at the crowd. Tolver had been talking to people in the crowd. Lyle had been standing by herself.

They went down the stair. Carrow declared, in a voice that carried up the walls. "*Carrow, edge and stance, laid. Lyle, ward and stance, laid. Tolver, stance. Treen, ward.*"

The bell went.

They were very fast. Kiva had known that they would be. They ran the chamber in a body, and at the Purser Lyle laid. Her ward went under the cloth and her feet into the floor together, in one breath, and the cloth went up and over and stayed there, because a laid ward does not move. Tolver took the spar off the shins with his stance, laid. Carrow's edge cut both lashings. Treen had the rope over her shoulder and was at the ladder before the cloth had stopped moving, and up it, light and fast, like a cat up a curtain.

"Twenty," said the man on the lip with the watch.

It was six seconds faster than Seven. Kiva heard Anwen's breath go in.

At the foot of the ladder, Carrow and Lyle and Tolver had the Purser up against the wall, and Tolver was tying the rope round his chest under the arms with quick big hands, and Carrow was holding the Purser's head.

The water was at the ladder's foot. It was coming up. The lockkeeper had turned his crank at the minute, as he had turned it at the minute for twenty-three fours before them, and the spouts were full, and the water came down the chamber and pooled at the head end where the ladder was, and it was at the Ropewalk's knees.

Tolver tied the knot. He tied it twice. He was being careful, because a knot that slips on a wall is twenty seconds.

The Purser's head lolled forward.

Carrow had it in her hands. She had been holding it up. But she was holding it with nothing open, because she was laid, and to lift anything she had to walk, and to walk she had to unlay. She had done that. She had shut both doors to hold the head. And the Purser was heavy, and wet, and folding at the neck, and his chin went down onto his chest. His painted face went down into the brown water to the eyebrows and came up again.

"*Face,*" said the marshal.

It was flat. It was the only flat thing in the whole roar.

Carrow did not look up. She did not stop. She lifted the head again and held it, and Tolver said *up,* and Treen and their blade hauled from the top, and the Purser went up the north wall of the Inner Lock on the end of a rope, steady and fast, with Tolver laid at the bottom keeping him off the stone. It was beautiful. It was the fastest thing anybody had done all morning.

They ran him to the mark.

"*Two minutes and fifty-nine seconds,*" said the marshal. "*Ten for the face. Three minutes and nine.*"

---

It was very quiet for a moment.

Then the Clerk came along the quay in her grey coat, and stood by the mark, and the quiet went quieter.

"The Carry," she said. "The second board. Twenty-four fours run. No four struck." She did not look at a paper. "First. Team Seven, Meridian. Three minutes and eight. Second. The Ropewalk School, first four. Three minutes and nine. Third. Halden License, Meridian. Three minutes and ten. Fourth. The Tarn, first four. Three minutes and twelve. Fifth. Old Four, Meridian. Three minutes and sixteen. Sixth. Team Twenty-Eight, Meridian. Three minutes and nineteen." She paused. "The rest will be posted at the tent at six, with the standings on two boards."

She turned away. Then she turned back.

"There is a protest," she said, "from Old Four, Meridian. Lodged at the mark by its captain, Brask, at eleven minutes past twelve."

The quay murmured. Kiva saw Perrin, on his bollard, go very still.

"The protest is that the carry of Team Seven planted a still door in a wall and not in a floor," said the Clerk. "Its captain cites the rule. A still door plants in the floor, and does not plant in a square." She did not raise her voice. "I have read the rule. It says a still door does not plant in a square. There are no squares on a ladder. The protest is dismissed." She looked along the quay. "Captain Brask, I thank you for the reading. It was close. It was not close enough."

There was a laugh. It went round the quay and grew.

Kiva looked for Brask. He was standing by the stair with Old Four round him, a big mild young man who read people as other people read the labels on jars, and he did not laugh. He did not look angry either. He looked across the quay at Perrin on his bollard for a long moment, and then he lifted one hand, flat, and held it there, the way a referee holds up a hand for a call that was fair. Then he turned away.

"He taught me to say thank you to the ground," said Perrin, very low. "In the junior lane. When I was thirteen."

He did not lift his hand back. He sat on his bollard with Aske's linen round his chest and the whole quay looking at him, and his face had gone a strange colour, neither pale nor red. Kiva had never seen it before.

"First," said Anwen. "Kiva. First."

"I heard."

"By a second. By one second, off the Ropewalk." Anwen let go of her foot at last and put both hands over her face, and took them away again. "On a ladder. With his rib."

Jude had sat down again on the quay. He had the book out on his knee and he was writing, very slowly, with the pencil in his right hand and his left arm held against his chest.

"*Tuesday the 15th,*" he said aloud, as he wrote. "*The Carry, the Inner Lock. First, three minutes and eight. Cade, twenty-two rungs, twenty-two hundredths, and a rib. Pryce, nine, for the cloth. Talley, four, for two lashings. Rowan on the far edge and at the foot, her own thimble.*" He looked at it. "*Thirty-five. Held, three and a half.*"

He shut the book.

"I'd want it said," he said, to nobody in particular, "that I was at the top. That I saw his face come up over the lip." He was quiet a moment. "I'll not write what it looked like. It's not that kind of book."

---

The Mercury's man got to Perrin before anybody could stop him.

He was a young man with ink on his cuffs and a notebook already open. He came pushing through the people round the bollard and stood in front of Perrin and said, in a quick coast voice, "Mr. Cade. The Mercury. How did you think of it, sir? The rungs. Nobody's ever planted a ladder."

Perrin looked at him.

"I sat on the lip," he said. "Last night. For an hour."

"And it came to you?"

"No. I worked it out." Perrin's voice was hoarse. "A rung's iron. It's in granite. The granite's in the ground."

The young man wrote it down. He wrote it with his mouth moving, the way Pru read. "*The granite's in the ground,*" he said. "That's very good. That's a line. And the girl at the foot, sir, calling? That was the catcher? Rowan?"

"That was the word," said Perrin. "The ladder was mine."

The young man looked up from his book. He looked at Perrin's face, and then at Kiva in her blanket against the hut, and something in his own face changed, as if he had found something more interesting than what he had come for.

"Yes, sir," he said. "Of course. *The ladder was mine.*" He wrote that down too. "And how does it feel, sir? To beat the Ropewalk on their own quay? People will be talking about it for years. They're talking about it now." He jerked his head at the crowd. "Half the low town's down here. They came to see the Ropewalk. They'll go home talking about you."

Perrin did not answer.

The young man waited, and then, when he saw that no answer was coming, he touched his hat and went away into the crowd with his notebook, to find somebody who would talk.

Kiva saw them then. She had not been looking. Now she saw how many people were looking at Perrin.

They were all along the quay. They were Saltmere green, and Holloway red, and Westerley blue, and the buff of the Tarn, and Meridian. They were looking at him the way people look at a man who has done a thing that they wish they had done. Some of them were smiling. Some of them were not. A boy in green by the stair said something to the girl beside him, low, and the girl laughed, and they both looked at Perrin, and the girl stopped laughing. By the hut, one of Old Four was saying to another, not quite low enough, *he'll not stand tomorrow, any road. Not with that.*

Perrin heard it. Kiva saw him hear it.

He sat on his bollard with his hands on his knees. He did not look at anybody. He looked at the Purser, lying on the mark with his painted face to the sky and the Board's sling round his chest.

"Kiva," he said.

She got up, with the blanket round her, and went and stood beside the bollard. Her bare feet were on the cold granite.

"I wanted a thing with only my name on it," said Perrin, very low. "All winter. I told you so, out loud, at home. I minded that it was always you."

"I know."

"Now it's got only my name on it." He was still looking at the Purser. "And everybody on this quay's looking at it. And half of them want it." He breathed in, carefully, as far as he could go, and out. "I thought it would feel like having something."

"What does it feel like?"

He thought about it for a long time. He was honest about things. He always had been, even when it cost him, even in the autumn when he had stood in a yard and said *I mind* out loud.

"Like being looked at," he said. "That's all. Like being looked at by people who'd like to be where I am, and think I'm lucky, and don't know about the rib or the lip or the hour." He looked up at her at last. "That's what it was like for you. Wasn't it. All year."

Kiva did not answer at once.

She thought of the gate at home in the autumn, and the Pit, and the Old Yard, and the white arm. She thought of every floor she had stood on since the spring with people looking at her, and of a boy who had stood up in front of his own four and said *I mind,* out loud, and had been Perrin.

"Yes," she said. "It's that."

"It's not better," said Perrin. "Being envied. I thought it would be better than the other thing. It isn't."

"No."

"It's not worse either." He looked back at the Purser. "It's just the other side of the same thing. I'd not have known that. I'd have gone on thinking that you had something I hadn't." He was quiet. "I'd like it known that I know now."

"It's known," said Kiva.

Aske came back through the crowd with a cup of tea in each hand. He gave one to Perrin and one to Kiva, and looked at them both.

"Drink that," he said. "Neither of you is to be looked at by anybody for at least a quarter of an hour. I'll stand here and see to it."

He stood in front of the bollard with his bag in his hand and his back to the quay, a narrow grey man who never raised his voice, and for a quarter of an hour nobody came near them at all.

---

## CHAPTER — manuscript/chapter-28.md

# Chapter 28 — Kiva: The Second Plant

Aske wrote the card at the long table in the loft at two o'clock, with Perrin sitting across from him in a clean shirt and the four of them standing round.

He wrote it in his small square hand, slowly, as he wrote everything that mattered. Then he turned it round on the table so that Perrin could read it, and then so that the rest of them could.

*Cade, P. The seventh rib on the left, cracked across, not through; the sixth and eighth sprung as before. Tuesday the 15th, at the Inner Lock, under load. No plant. No lift. Nothing carried heavier than a cup. Strapped, to be seen morning and night. A fortnight at the least. W. Aske.*

"A fortnight," said Perrin.

"At the least." Aske capped his pen. "I've written *at the least* so that nobody can come to me on the thirtieth and say I promised."

"The Lane's Thursday."

"The Lane's Thursday, and you'll be at the hurdle with your hands in your pockets, declaring." Aske did not raise his voice. He never did. "You may captain. You may talk. You may stand on the hard tomorrow on the Longshore and watch your four with a glass, if the Board will have you there, and I think it will. You may not go onto the mud. If you go onto the mud and something goes wrong, you'll be one more thing on the mud that somebody has to carry, and you'll be the heaviest."

Perrin looked at the card for a long time.

"I knew," he said. "On the lip. Last night. I said the price. Rowan said it."

"Then you knew," said Aske. "That's the best I can say for it. You knew, and you paid, and you came first." He got up. "I'll see it again at eight."

He went down the room to his own bed. The four of them stood at the table and looked at the card.

"Finch stands," said Perrin.

He said it before anybody else could say anything. He said it as he had said things at the marshal's box, flat and clear.

Pru was by the stove with her cord in her lap. She had been winding it since they came in, and she stopped.

"I'm the reserve," she said. "It's on the entry. I stood the Bench. I stood the Tarn on Saturday." She went red. "I'll stand. I'd like to stand. I want to say that before anybody's careful with me."

"Nobody's being careful with you," said Perrin. "There's nobody else." He put his hands flat on the table, either side of the card. "Rowan, catcher. Finch, catcher. Pryce, Hold. Talley, Kill. That's the four on the mud. I captain from the hard."

"Two catchers," said Anwen. "And no still door."

"No still door."

"On the Longshore." Anwen had her hands in her sleeves. "Where the ground's mud, and the water's never still, and you've told us since Coombe that the far edge wants weight on it." She looked at him steadily. "What's behind it? Two catchers and no plant. I'm asking because somebody will."

"Rowan's a still door."

"Rowan's whatever you declare her. One door." Anwen did not look at Kiva. "If you declare her stance, she's our plant, and she can put her boots on a far edge as she did this morning, and we've got weight. That's what a still door is on the Longshore. Coombe said so. On a moving thing, weight on the far edge."

"I know what Coombe said."

"Then what's behind it?"

Perrin did not answer at once. He looked at his own hands on the table.

"I'm going to declare her Ember," he said.

There was a silence. Jude, at the end of the table, put down the book.

"Ember," said Anwen.

"Ember." Perrin did not look up. "Declared, single, by her captain's voice, off her own thimble. It's on her card. She can light it and nothing else, on that board, all day." He drew a breath, carefully. "Which means she's not our plant. She can't put her boots on anything. She's a catcher with a carry's door, and a still door shut in her that she's not let open."

"Why?"

"Because if somebody goes under a skin tomorrow," said Perrin, "there's one thing I know of on this coast that takes it off them without folding whoever holds it. Coombe said it. She said she'd seen mixed fours pass a load hand to hand on the Longshore, badly, two people grabbing at once and hoping, and twice it killed somebody. She said she'd never seen it called." He looked up at last. "It's three people. Finch folds. Rowan carries. Somebody plants. We've two of them standing tomorrow, and the third was me, and the third's cracked."

Anwen was quiet.

"I'm not saying it'll be wanted," said Perrin. "I'm saying if it's wanted, and we haven't got it, I'll have spent the day on the hard with my hands in my pockets knowing I could have had it declared and didn't. I'd sooner lose the weight on a far edge than that." He put his hand to his side, without noticing. "That's what's behind it. It's a thing that might not happen. I'm declaring for it so that if it does, we're not grabbing."

"And the plant?"

"I've been thinking about the plant since Sunday," said Perrin. "I asked for a second plant on Sunday, at eight, at this table. You all heard me. I said if my ribs went on the Bench or the Carry, there'd be no method." He almost smiled. "They went on the Carry."

"Trewin," said Jude.

"Trewin." Perrin nodded at the door. "He did it once on a yard, a count late. That's once more than anybody else who isn't us."

Kiva had not said anything. She found that they were all looking at her.

"It's mine to say?" she said.

"It's your door," said Perrin. "I declare it. You light it. I'd not declare a door of yours you didn't want lit." He looked at her straight. "Is it yours to say?"

She thought about it. She thought about the far edge of a grey sailcloth under her boots that morning, and the lead in it pulling, and how good it had felt to be the ground. She thought about the Longshore at midnight with Penhale's purse pole under her two fingers, and a fold coming up out of black water on the far side of a skin like a page turning. She thought about Coombe on the Low Lane with her rope in her hands. *It's the thing she does that she can't do, done by three who can.*

"It's yours to say," she said. "You're captain. But I'd say it too, if you asked me. Ember."

Perrin let out a breath.

"Then it's Ember," he said. "Talley, write it."

Jude wrote it. "*Tuesday. For the Hunt: Rowan, Ember, declared. Finch stands for Cade. Cade captains from the hard, on his healer's card.*" He looked up. "Pryce?"

Anwen took her hands out of her sleeves.

"I wanted it asked," she said. "I've asked it. I'll stand with it." She looked at Perrin. "I want it said that I'd still sooner have a plant on the far edge than a method in a pocket. But you're captain, and it's a good reason, and I've not got a better one."

"That's fair," said Perrin.

"It's fair," said Anwen. "I'd not say it if it weren't."

---

The board went up at the tent at six, under the lamp, with the seal on it, and the whole of the Measure came down the boards in the dusk to read it.

Kiva went with Jude. Perrin had been told by Aske to lie on his bed until eight, and was lying on it, under protest, reading the Mercury's afternoon sheet with his name in it upside down so that he would not have to look at it the right way up. Anwen had gone up Tar Lane by herself again. Pru had gone to sleep sitting up on her bed with her boots on.

There were two sheets on the pole. The first was the Carry, top to bottom, twenty-four lines, with Seven at the top of it in the clerk's careful hand. *1. Team Seven, Meridian. Three minutes and eight seconds; clean.* Kiva read it twice. She had seen it written before, in her head, on a quay. It was different on a pole.

The second sheet was the standings.

*After two boards. Marked by place, twenty-four to the first and one to the last, and added.*

*1. Halden License, Meridian. Forty-six.*

*2. The Ropewalk School, first four. Forty-five.*

*3. Team Seven, Meridian. Forty-three.*

*4. Old Four, Meridian. Forty-one.*

*4. Holloway, first four. Forty-one.*

*6. The Tarn, first four. Thirty-eight.*

Kiva read it three times.

"Third," said Jude. He said it very quietly. "Kiva. Third. Of twenty-four."

"After two boards."

"After two boards." He scratched his jaw. "We were seeded twelfth. On Saturday. We were the exact middle of the whole board, and Cade said it was honest and he'd take honest." He looked at the pole. "I'd want it said that this is honest as well."

"It is."

"I know it is. I'm saying it." He was quiet for a moment. "Six on the Bench, and first on the Carry. And tomorrow's the Hunt, and the Hunt's the one that's alive."

People were looking at them. Kiva felt it. It was not like the looking on the quay that morning, which had all been at Perrin. This was a quieter kind. It went past her to the pole and came back to her, and to Jude in his Seven colours with his book under his good arm, and went away again. A girl in green said, low, to the boy beside her, *that's the drained jacket, that's the ladder,* and the boy said, *that's the one who doesn't stand at the Bench.*

Under the standings there was a third sheet. It was newer. Kiva had to step closer to read it in the lamplight.

*The Hunt. Wednesday the 16th, on the Longshore, from nine. Twelve beats, two fours to a beat, one officer to a beat, by the Board's draw.*

She read down it. *Gannet Creek: Old Four; Westerley, first four. Officer, Sorn, of Graywater.* She read it again; Rhea had a beat. *Brine Reach: Team Twenty-Eight; Holloway, second four.* And then two lines lower.

*Mallow Creek: Team Seven; the Tarn, first four. Officer, Yeo, of the Saltmere Company.*

*Skerry Pill: Halden License; the Ropewalk School, first four. Officer, Bray, of the Saltmere Company. A lodger marked in the pill's mouth by the Company at the evening low; the fours on the beat have been told.*

Jude had read it too. Kiva heard him draw breath.

"The Tarn," he said. "On our beat."

"Forty paces off, likely. Two fours to a creek."

"Trewin." Jude looked at her. "Cade asked for him on Sunday. Before there was a draw. Before anybody knew."

"I know."

"That's luck," said Jude. "I'd want it said that I know it's luck. That we didn't do anything to get it." He looked at the line again. "And a lodger on Skerry. With the Ropewalk. Laid."

"*The fours on the beat have been told,*" said Kiva.

"Everybody's been telling them since the gate," said Jude. "Coombe told them in the second month, before she ever met them. Pryce told Lyle a fortnight ago, up Tar Lane. The Company's told them." He shut the book under his arm. "I'd not know what else anybody could tell them."

---

Kellan came up the loft stair at a quarter past six and stood at the end of Kiva's bed.

He did not sit down. He was in his black and gold, with his coat over his arm, and he had the look he had when he had been doing a sum that would not come out.

"Skerry Pill," he said.

"I saw."

"Bray's beat. With the Ropewalk. And a lodger in the mouth." He looked down the room at the south end, where Pettit was sitting on his bed with Yarrow and Hesketh, talking low. "Pettit's been to Bray. She says the lodger's lying in the mud at the pill's mouth where the Company left it, and it's not moved since five, and they'll not kill it before the Hunt, because it's the Board's beat and the Board wants it as it lies." He shifted his coat. "She says the Ropewalk's been told. Carrow said, *we've been told.* That's all she said."

"Then they've been told."

"They've been told." Kellan looked at her. "I'm not saying anything's going to happen. I'm doing a sum. A lodger goes to a laid door. Coombe showed that. The Ropewalk lays everything they've got; they laid a lock this morning. A pill's mouth on a neap ebb with the wind where it is—" He stopped. "I'm on the same beat. I'm a blade. I've watched one in Coombe's cage. I've never seen one loose."

"Jude cut one. In the Pit. Through the cord on stone."

"I know. I read his line." Kellan was quiet for a moment. "Pettit says Halden's work is Halden's work, and the Ropewalk's is theirs, and Bray's is Bray's, and that's the rule on any beat, and he's right." He put his coat on. "I'd expect nothing else of him. I'm telling you because I wanted to tell somebody who'd not tell me I was being foolish."

"You're not being foolish."

"No," said Kellan. "That's what I was afraid of."

He went down the room to his own bed. He did not look back.

Anwen came up the stair at twenty-five past six, as Kiva was going down it.

She had been up Tar Lane. Kiva could tell by her boots, which had tar on the heels, and by her face, which had the closed careful look it wore when she had said a thing she had made up her mind to say. She stopped two steps above Kiva on the narrow stair and put her hand on the rail.

"I went to the Ropewalk," she said.

"I know. Jude said."

"I saw the board first. Skerry Pill." Anwen looked down the stairwell, at nothing. "Lyle was in the long shed with the little ones, like before. I told her what the board said. She'd read it. They'd all read it. Bray told them at five."

"What did she say?"

"She said Carrow says a laid boom's the only boom on this coast that holds. She said Mace's walls let a third of it under at Gannet Creek; she'd heard that from the Company. Hers don't let anything under, because her feet are in the mud under them." Anwen's hand tightened on the rail. "She said Wenna's night was in the dark, on the flood, with a crew that didn't know. This is daylight, on the ebb, on a Board beat, with Bray, and everybody's been told. She said Treen will be watching, and if Treen calls *run,* she'll walk."

"Then she'll walk."

"She said she would." Anwen was quiet for a moment. "I've said it twice now. Once a fortnight ago and once tonight. I'll not say it a third time. A third time's not telling somebody. It's minding their business for them." She looked at Kiva at last. "I want it said that I know the difference. I've only lately learned it."

"It's said."

"Good." Anwen went on up the stair past her. At the top she stopped. "She's teaching a boy of eleven to lay. He's very bad at it. She's very patient." And she went in.

---

The Tarn's four lodged over a cooper's on Quay Street, in a long room that smelled of new oak and wet ash hoops, and Trewin came down to the yard at half past six to meet them with his hat in his hands.

Perrin had come. Aske had let him go on condition that he walked, and that he walked slowly, and that Kiva walked beside him and stopped him if he tried to carry so much as a door handle. So he had walked down Quay Street at a pace that made a woman with a pram overtake them twice, and now he stood in the cooper's yard among stacks of barrel staves with his hand pressed to his side.

"I heard it," said Trewin. "From the stair. On the eleventh." He nodded at Perrin's side, slowly. "I'm sorry. It was a very good climb."

"Yours was better," said Perrin. "You planted on the floor. Every step. For fifty paces."

"Mine was slower." Trewin turned his hat round in his hands. "I'd thought of it on Sunday. Coming down the Long Quay from your yard, saying *nobody.* I thought, if he can plant on a word that isn't his, I can plant on a step I've only half taken." He looked at the cobbles. "I'd not have thought of it without the yard. I wanted you to know."

Perrin did not say anything for a moment. Kiva saw him go the strange colour again, the one from the quay.

"We're on your beat tomorrow," he said at last.

"I saw the board. Mallow Creek." Trewin nodded. "Yeo's. She's very good, they say."

"I'm not standing," said Perrin. "I'm on the hard with my hands in my pockets. Finch stands. Rowan's declared Ember."

Trewin looked at him, and then at Kiva, and then at Pru, who had come too and was standing by the staves with her cord at her belt. He was slow, but he was not stupid. Kiva watched him understand.

"For the pass," he said.

"For the pass. If it's wanted." Perrin made himself say it. "If anybody goes under anything, on either half of that creek, and the officer says. We've a catch and a carry. We've no plant." He held out his hand, flat, as Trewin had held out his own on Sunday morning, at the top of the loft stair. "I'm asking you to be the third. If it's wanted. Which I hope it won't be."

Trewin did not take the hand. He looked at it for a long time, with his gatepost face quite still. Then he looked past it, at the yard gate, where a tall plaited girl of fifteen was standing in the brown and buff of the Tarn, listening.

"Bridie," he said. "Come here."

The girl came. She had her two plaits pinned up round her head, and her sleeves rolled to the elbow, and Kiva knew her. She was the reserve who had unpacked the Tarn's box at the gate on the first day, and laid out a stone jar, and said *plain* to the clerk, proudly. Her grandfather had kept the chandlery at Tarnfoot for fifty years. The jar had been cut with soot. Kiva had watched her chin begin to shake.

"Hebden," said Trewin. "Bridie Hebden. Our reserve. She's catching tomorrow." He said it slowly, as he said everything. "Our catcher turned her ankle on the stair this morning, coming up after me. It's twice the size it should be. She can't stand on the Longshore."

"I can catch," said Bridie. Her voice was quick and northern and very determined. "I've caught all winter. On the yard at Tarnfoot. I've never caught on a coast."

"Nobody here has," said Pru, by the staves, and went scarlet.

Trewin turned back to Perrin.

"She's my catcher tomorrow," he said. "She's fifteen. If you're asking me to be ready to plant a thing that Rowan carries across our beat, then I'm asking you something back." He nodded at the girl, quite gently. "If it's her. If she's the one who's gone under. You'll not stop to ask me whether I'm ready."

"No," said Perrin.

"Then I'm the third." Trewin took the hand at last. He shook it slowly and properly, once, as he nodded. "If the officer says. And if it's wanted, which I hope it won't be, I'll not wait to see her land."

---

They did it dry, in the cooper's yard, among the staves.

They lit nothing. Aske had said one go, on Sunday, and that had been the go; and there was a Hunt in the morning, and nobody's door was to be spent on a cooper's cobbles the night before. So they did it with words and hands and feet.

Pru stood at one end of the yard with her hands held low at her hip, palms up, as though her ward were under something. Kiva stood beside her. Trewin stood eight paces off, by a stack of staves, with his feet square.

"I'll say it twice," said Kiva. "Once when Finch lets go, from where I'm standing. Once when I'm in the air, before I land. You open on the end of the second one. Not when you see me land. Not when you feel it come. On the word."

"I know. I've had it said."

"I'm saying it again. You'll want to wait. Your whole body will want to wait. It did on Sunday." She looked at him across the yard. "Don't."

Trewin nodded, slowly.

"*Nobody,*" said Kiva, short, with the swallow.

Pru's hands opened. Kiva went across the yard at a walk, eight paces, with her right fist closed as if something rode along it. Three paces from Trewin she said it again.

"*Nobody.*"

Trewin opened his hands.

He opened them a count late. Kiva saw it. He had waited to see her stop.

"Again," she said.

They did it again. And again. On the fourth time he opened half a count late. On the seventh, a quarter. On the tenth, he opened on the end of the word, and Kiva saw him do it, and saw his face as he did it. It was white. It was the face of a man who has just stepped off a wall into the dark because somebody he hardly knows has told him there is a floor.

"That's it," she said. "That's the word."

"It's horrible," said Trewin, quite politely.

"It is. It gets less horrible."

"Does it?"

"No," said Pru, from the other end of the yard. "It gets more usual. That's not the same."

Trewin looked at her, and then he laughed. It was the first time Kiva had ever heard him laugh. It was very deep and slow, like a cart going over a bridge.

They did it ten times more. Bridie stood by the yard gate and watched every one, with her arms folded and her plaits pinned up and her eyes going from Pru's hands to Kiva's fist to Trewin's feet and back again.

When they had done, and Trewin had gone to fetch a lamp, Bridie came across the cobbles to Kiva.

"What happens in the gap?" she said. "When nobody's got it. When she's let go and you've not got it, and you've let go and he's not got it. What happens to the thing?"

Kiva thought about it. Nobody had ever asked her that. They had asked her how long the gap was, and what it cost, and whether it was a stack. Nobody had asked her what happened to the thing.

"Nothing," she said. "For a quarter of a count, nothing. It hangs. It doesn't belong to anybody, so it doesn't do anything to anybody." She looked at her own right fist. "That's the whole point of it. A thing that wants to fold onto whoever holds it can't fold onto nobody."

Bridie looked at her fist too.

"Our book says you pass a load hand to hand," she said. "Two people holding it at once. So it's never dropped."

"That's the book."

"It sounds safer."

"It sounds safer," said Kiva. "Two people holding a thing at once is two doors on one thing, agreeing. There's a cord on the Longshore that goes to that like a dog to a bone. Our Marshal had one in a cage. It went to two people on a sack." She stopped. She did not want to frighten a girl of fifteen the night before a Hunt. "I'm not saying your book's wrong. I'm saying nobody's is right about everything."

Bridie considered that with her chin up.

"My grandad used to say that," she said. "About his scales." Then her chin did a small thing, not quite a shake. "He said nobody's scales were right about everything, and that's why you weigh twice." She went back to the gate.

The customs house clock struck the three-quarters, a long way off down Quay Street. Kiva took her left hand out of her pocket and laid the first two fingers on the nearest barrel stave where it stood on end against the wall, lightly, without leaning.

*It keeps time.*

She wrote it in the back of the book by the light from the cooper's window. *Tuesday the 15th. A quarter to seven. A stave in a cooper's yard on Quay Street. Yes.*

---

They walked back along Quay Street in the dark, slowly, at Perrin's pace.

The low town was going to bed. The gratings rattled in the gutters on the count, four and four, and in the windows above the street the lamps went out one by one, yellow, ordinary lamps, oil and wick, nothing like the white ones on the Long Quay. A man was singing in a public house. A dog barked at them from a doorway and stopped when it saw how slowly they were going.

Pru had gone ahead. Kiva walked beside Perrin with her hands in her coat.

"I've declared you onto a door you'll not use," said Perrin. "And Finch onto the mud. And Trewin onto a thing he's done once on a yard and twenty times dry tonight, for a girl of fifteen who's never caught on a coast." He walked a few paces. "And I'll be on the hard with my hands in my pockets watching. If it goes wrong, it's mine. All of it. My declaring."

"That's captain."

"I know it's captain." He breathed, carefully. "I didn't want to be captain. You know that. I minded it so much I couldn't sleep. And then I found out it meant you got to decide things." He was quiet. "It means you get to decide things for people. That's the other half. Nobody tells you that half."

"You've been told it now."

"I've told myself. It's not the same as being told." Perrin almost laughed, and stopped, and put his hand to his side. "Ow."

They walked on.

At the corner where Quay Street came out onto the Long Quay, the white lamps began. They stood on their fluted posts all the way along the water to the Counting House, without a flicker, and their light lay on the black harbour in long still bars. Behind them the basin was coming in on the flood, quiet, and the gratings had stopped rattling for a moment.

Jude was standing at the end of the quay by the first lamp, looking at the sea.

He had his left arm in its linen again. He was standing quite still, with his good hand on the bollard and his face toward the Mole and the dark beyond it, where the sea was breathing, every count at once. Kiva and Perrin stopped beside him. He did not look round.

"Three and a half," he said. "In the book. And the four's quarter of whatever's clean, tomorrow." He was quiet for a moment. "I've been standing here since the board. I've been thinking about what it's for."

"The ash?"

"The ash. The Hunt. Tomorrow." He did not look round. "Yeo said it's always the same thing. A kitchen in the low town. Keeping the sea out." He lifted his good hand off the bollard and put it back. "We'll go out tomorrow on the Longshore and kill what's there, and comb it, and nearly all of it will be red, because everything is now. And the red can't go in a pump house. It can't go anywhere. It'll sit in a bag with a tag on it for evidence." He was quiet. "So what's it for? The Hunt. If what we bring in can't keep anybody's kitchen dry."

Perrin did not answer. Kiva did not know the answer.

"I'd want it said that I don't know," said Jude. "That I've been looking at the sea for half an hour and I don't know. And that I'm going out on it tomorrow anyway." He turned his head at last. "That's all. I'll go to bed now. I'd want it said I thought about it."

"It's said," said Kiva.

He went away along the quay toward the chandlery, limping a little, under the white lamps. Perrin watched him go.

"He'll stay," said Perrin. "Here. When it's done. I'd put money on it."

"Yeo hasn't asked him."

"No. She's watching." Perrin looked at the black water. "That's how they do it on the coast, I think. They watch you till they're sure, and then they ask once."

He turned and went slowly up the quay toward the loft, with his hand pressed to his side. Kiva stood a moment longer by the bollard where Jude had stood.

She took her left hand out of her pocket. She did not lay the two fingers on anything. She looked at them, in the white light: the first two, which bent and gripped and told her when a thing was pushing, and told her when a thing kept time, and on the coast, at the slack, told her nothing at all.

Tomorrow they would be on the Longshore. She would have them on a purse pole again, as she had had them on Penhale's pole in the dark at Gannet Creek, with the flood going inland and the town's count under it. She would know the slack before anybody else on the beat. She would know when it ended.

It was not much. It was two words, once, to the person who needed them.

She put the hand back in her pocket and went up to bed.

---

## CHAPTER — manuscript/chapter-29.md

# Chapter 29 — Kiva: The Longshore

"Warm," said Aske. "Three mornings."

It was six on Wednesday, and the loft was dark except for the candle on the sill by the north window, and the window was grey. Aske held the hand for two minutes by his watch, as he always did, and then for one more, and then he let it go and wrote.

*Warm at six on Wednesday the 16th. Three mornings. Thursday, if wanted, and the price said first.*

He looked at the line.

"Today's your Ember," he said. "Declared, single, all day, off your own thimble. It's not your hand. It's not a crossing. There's no gap in you anywhere today, and I'd like there not to be." He capped his pen. "If anybody on that mud asks you for a second door, you'll say no, and if they ask twice you'll send them to me."

"Nobody will ask."

"Somebody always asks," said Aske. "On a beach, with the tide coming. I've been on beaches." He put the card in his bag. "I'll be at the Board's tent on the causeway with the other healers, a quarter of a mile from anywhere. I'll be no use to anybody till they're carried to me. Remember that."

He went to Perrin's bed. Perrin was sitting on it in the half dark with his shirt off and Jude winding the linen round him again, tight, and Perrin's face set at the hinge of the jaw.

"Morning and night," said Aske, and put his hands flat on the linen and listened. "As before. Don't breathe deep. Don't laugh. Don't, above all, lift anything." He took his hands away. "You may hold a glass. A small one."

---

The Longshore began where the Mole ended.

Kiva had walked out along it once before, at two in the morning with Jude and a lantern, and then it had been black flats and a pale line of sea. Now, at half past seven, it was grey. The causeway ran out from the end of the Mole across the flats, an old raised track of stone wide enough for a cart, and on either side of it there was nothing but mud and sky. The mud was grey and wet and shining, and cut everywhere with creeks, some as narrow as a ditch and some as wide as a street, all running seaward full of brown water. The tide was going out. It had been going out since six and would go on going out till noon. Far off, at the edge of everything, there was a line of white where the sea was.

The wind was in the south-west, off the sea. It came across the flats in gusts, cold, smelling of salt and weed, and when it came it pushed the water in the creeks back a little against the ebb, so that the brown surface wrinkled and stood up in little ridges and then lay down again.

"*Gusty,*" said Jude, at the causeway's edge. He had his arm back in its linen, and he was looking at the creeks as other people look at a crowd, to see which way it would go. "The bosun said it'd be gusty. He said a gusty ebb is a bad ebb, because the water doesn't know which way it's going."

The Board had put its tent at the landward end of the causeway, where it left the Mole. It was the same grey tent from the timber pond, carried out on a cart. There was a comb table in front of it, and a row of clerks, and every four in the Measure stood in a queue along the causeway and laid out its tins to be combed empty. Behind the tent there was a second, smaller tent, with a red cross painted on its flap and a row of stretchers outside it. Aske was already there, with his bag, talking to a Company healer in green.

And along the top of the Mole, all the way back to the town, there were people.

They stood along its parapet in a dark line against the sky, as far as Kiva could see, the low town and the high town both, in shawls and caps and coats, with children on shoulders and glasses to their eyes. There was a boat in the harbour mouth with the Mercury's flag on it, and a man in its bow with a long glass on a stand. There were more people on the causeway itself, roped off at the tent, a hundred, two hundred.

"They've come to see the worst of it," said Orla.

She had come out on the Circuit's cart with Aske and the clerks, and she sat on the cart's tailboard with her stick across her knees. She had not been asked. Nobody had expected her not to come.

"The worst?"

"The Hunt's the only board you can lose somebody on," said Orla. "They know that. They've known it longer than we have. That's why they come." She looked out at the flats. "There's nobody in the low town who hasn't got somebody in the Company, or had."

Coombe came along the causeway at a quarter to eight in her grey Board coat, with the rope in her hands.

Kiva had not seen her since the second month. She looked exactly the same. She walked as if the causeway were a quay she was counting heads on, slowly, and her pale eyes went over the fours in their colours one after another without stopping on any of them. She stopped at the comb table. The Clerk was there, in her own grey coat with the gold chain, and they did not greet each other. They stood side by side, two women in the Board's grey, and the Clerk read the rules.

"The Hunt," said the Clerk. "The third board. Twelve beats on the Longshore, two fours to a beat, one officer to each, by the Board's draw. A four works on its officer's word and on nothing else. Nothing is lit without it. Nothing is cut without it. A four does not go onto the mud below a form on running water. A person down on the mud is the officer's crew's; the fours stand off. Students close no tear." She did not look at a paper. "Every bag is combed at the gate. A four is marked on its quarter of its clean yield, after the Board's half and the officer's company's quarter, and less a dram on the mark for every remark on its officer's slate. The Hunt closes at three, or when the flood is over the flats, whichever comes first."

She stepped back. It was Coombe's turn.

Coombe did not step forward. She stood where she was by the comb table with her rope, and she began to tie a knot in it, and while she tied it she spoke.

"Who has your far edge?" she said.

Nobody answered. It was so sudden that nobody knew whom she had asked.

"Every catcher on this causeway," said Coombe. "In order of the draw. Say who has your far edge. Say it aloud, so that your officer hears it, and your four, and I do." She did not look up from her knot. "Gannet Creek. Old Four."

There was a moment. Then Old Four's catcher, a thin boy Kiva did not know, said, "The corner-hand, ma'am. On the far side. By the book."

"By the book," said Coombe. "Westerley."

"The corner-hand, ma'am."

"Brine Reach."

It went down the causeway, four after four. *The corner-hand. The corner-hand, ma'am. By the book.* Once a Holloway catcher said *me,* and Coombe said *and who has your heel,* and the catcher said *nobody, ma'am,* and Coombe said *say that to your officer, not to me.* Twenty-Eight's catcher was Marten, who said *the corner-hand* and then, after a moment, *and Dray, if she's free,* and Dray's face did what it always did, which was nothing.

"Mallow Creek," said Coombe. "Team Seven."

Kiva had known it would come. She had known what she would say since Coombe opened her mouth.

"Finch, ma'am," she said. "The second. She kneels at my other side and has the far edge, and says it once."

Coombe's hands stopped on the rope.

"The second," she said. "A place."

"A place, ma'am. Today it's Finch."

Coombe looked at her for the first time. It was a long look, the pale steady eyes going over Kiva as they went over a street, and then going on to Pru, who was standing beside her with the cord at her belt and her face going slowly scarlet from the neck up.

"Finch," said Coombe.

"Ma'am," said Pru.

"You'll say it once."

"Once, ma'am. Two words." Pru's voice did not shake. "I've had it from Pike."

Coombe tied her knot. She went on to the next.

"The Tarn."

There was a pause. Kiva turned her head. Bridie Hebden was standing at the front of the Tarn's four in her brown and buff, with her plaits pinned up round her head and a cord at her belt that was too long for her, wound twice round her wrist. Trewin was beside her with his hat in his hands.

"The corner-hand, ma'am," said Bridie. Her quick northern voice was very clear. "By the book."

"By the book," said Coombe.

She did not say anything else. She tied another knot. Kiva watched her hands, and saw them stop on the rope for the space of a breath, and then go on.

"Skerry Pill. Halden License."

"The corner-hand, ma'am," said Yarrow, for Halden.

"The Ropewalk."

"Me," said Treen.

She said it at once, small and dark, with her sleeves rolled to the elbow and her quick eyes on Coombe's face. "I have the far edge, ma'am. And the call. Run and lay. I always have."

"And who's watching the lodger in the pill's mouth?"

"Me, ma'am."

"That's two places," said Coombe.

"I've two eyes, ma'am."

"So had a man of mine," said Coombe. She did not say it unkindly. She said it as a fact about the tide. Then she went on down the causeway to the next beat, and the next, until every catcher in the Measure had said it aloud, and then she stopped at the end of the line and looked back along the whole length of it, with her rope in her hands.

"My page is with the Board's printer," she said. "It's been there since the second month. It's not the book. I wrote it, and I'd do what it says, and it's not the book." She looked along them all. "Your officer's slate is the book today. Your officer's word. I'll be on this causeway. I'll not be on your beat unless you're in trouble, and if you're in trouble, I'll be late. A quarter of a mile is a long way on mud." She coiled the rope. "That's said once. I'll not say it again."

She went away to the Board's tent and did not come out.

---

Perrin declared at the comb table at eight, in his flat clear voice, with his coat buttoned over the linen.

"*Team Seven. Rowan, Ember, declared, by her captain's voice. Finch, ward, catcher, for Cade. Pryce, ward. Talley, edge. Cade stands down, on his healer's card, and captains from the hard.*"

A murmur went along the causeway. It was not the warm sound. It was the sound of people telling each other what they had heard. *Ember. Not stance. Ember.* Kiva heard somebody in green say *what's she want an Ember for on a beach,* and somebody else say *they've no plant, they've got no plant at all.*

The clerk at the comb table wrote it down. He combed Seven's tins, all six, one after another, and they were empty, and he said so.

"Mallow Creek," he said. "Yeo's. Out past the second groyne and right along the spur. You'll see the Company's flag."

---

Yeo was waiting at the end of the spur with her notebook shut.

The spur was an old cart track of stone that went off the causeway at a right angle, a quarter of a mile out, and ran along the east bank of Mallow Creek toward the sea. The creek ran beside it, thirty paces wide, brown and fast, going out. On the west side there was nothing but mud and marsh grass to the edge of sight. On the east side there was the spur, raised a yard above the mud, and on the spur there was a little group of people in green, and a flag on a pole, and two punts drawn up on the stone.

Kiva saw the stows before she saw anybody's face.

There were two of them, sixty paces apart along the creek. The nearer one, upstream, was Seven's. The further one, downstream toward the sea, was the Tarn's. At a distance they looked like two pairs of grey posts standing in the brown water, eight paces apart across the channel, with something slung between them. Closer, she saw what it was.

It was a mouth.

Between the tops of the two posts, and down into the water, there hung the open end of a long grey skin, as a net hangs between two poles across a river. Behind the mouth the skin went away downstream with the water, under the surface, like a sleeve, or like the long bag of a windsock blown out by a wind. It was ten paces long, perhaps twelve, narrowing all the way, and closed at its far end. The ebb poured in at the mouth. It came out through the sides of the sleeve, all along its length, in a frost of white plates that broke up on the surface and went away downstream toward the sea. And the cold came off it. Kiva could feel it on her face at fifty paces, against the wind.

"Stow," said a voice beside her. "That's what we call them. After the old stow-nets my grandad used to set in the Reach. He'd stake a net across the tide with its mouth open, and the tide would fish it for him."

It was Penhale.

He was standing on the spur in his thigh boots and his canvas smock that had been black once, with his red beard blowing sideways in the wind. He was grinning at her. Kiva had not seen him since the dawn at Gannet Creek, when she had walked away along the causeway in the dark without looking back.

"Miss Rowan," he said. "Second to Pike. You'll not remember me."

"I held your pole," said Kiva. "In the dark. For an hour."

"So you did. Your two fingers on my ash pole, and the girl taking your wrist every quarter, and nobody telling me what you were doing." His grin got wider. "Pike told me afterward. A gauge, he said. Two fingers that tell you nothing, and they told you the slack." He shook his head. "I've been a catcher on this coast since I was fifteen, and my dad before me, and I never had a gauge but my eyes."

"I'm not a second today," said Kiva. "I'm Seven's catcher."

"I know you are. I heard your captain at the table." He jerked his head toward Yeo. "I'm the officer's reserve. I stand on this spur and do nothing, and if anybody goes down on the mud, I go and get them. That's the rule. You stand off, and I go." He looked at the creek. "I've been officer's reserve on four Hunts. Never gone onto the mud yet. It's very dull, and I'm paid for it, and my mother likes it."

Yeo had turned round. She came along the spur toward them, tall and grey and brisk, with her notebook in her hand.

"Seven," she said. "And the Tarn." The Tarn's four had come along the spur behind them, in a square, without any of its corners changing place. "I'm Yeo. You'll have heard of me, some of you." She looked at Jude, once, and away. "This is my beat. This is my crew. Penhale, reserve. Ivey, catcher to the closing crew." A short broad woman in green nodded from beside the punts. "The closing crew, four, under their own officer, with their own licence. They'll close the tears when the forms are down. Your forms. Two stows. Upper's Seven's, lower's the Tarn's. Under each, in the creek bed between the posts, there's a tear. The Company marked them at the evening low. Nobody's touched them."

She opened her notebook. She did not read from it. She held it open in her hand as Rhea held her slate.

"Before," she said. "Two stows on Mallow Creek, working the ebb. Tears in the bed under each. Nothing lit within the beat but what I say. The ebb runs till the slack, and the slack's at the bottom of the tide, which by the gate's table is twenty-one minutes past twelve. On the flats it'll be earlier, and long. It's the neap; the neap slack on this creek runs forty minutes or near enough." She shut the book. "You'll cut in the slack. Not before. Each stow on two posts; cut both and the stow's dead and the skin lies down. You'll set your purses under the posts at the end of the ebb, when the channel's low enough to stand in. Penhale will show you. Anything shed before then, I'll say what's done with it." She looked along them. "Questions."

"Ma'am," said Trewin. "The lower stow. It's in deeper water."

"It's in the bend. The channel's deeper on the outside. You'll want the punt for the far post." Yeo nodded at it. "My boatman's yours when you want him. Anything else."

Nobody said anything.

"Then that's said," said Yeo. "You're on the Board's ground and the Company's beat, and you're on my word. I'm glad to have you." She glanced at Perrin, standing at the back with his coat buttoned to the throat and his hand pressed to his side. "Captain. You'll stand with me, on the spur. You'll have the glass."

"Ma'am," said Perrin.

---

For two hours they watched the stows work the ebb.

It was the longest two hours Kiva could remember. She sat on the edge of the spur with her legs over the mud and her book on her knee, and wrote, and watched, and did not write anything that she had not seen. Pru sat beside her. Anwen stood a little way off with her hands in her sleeves, watching the creek. Jude walked up and down the spur, limping, with his left arm in its linen and his eyes on the posts.

The stow was always moving.

From a distance it had looked still, two posts and a hanging mouth. From the spur Kiva could see that it never stopped. The posts leaned with the ebb, both of them, a little, the way the kiddle's posts had leaned at Gannet Creek. The mouth bellied in the current. The long grey sleeve behind it stirred under the water like a living thing breathing, filling and slackening, and all along its sides the white plates came out and went away. When a gust came off the sea and pushed the water back, the sleeve slackened and the posts came upright an inch. When the gust passed and the ebb ran again, they leaned again, and the sleeve filled.

"It's like a thing drinking," said Pru, very low. "It's drinking the creek."

"It's working," said Penhale, from behind them. He had come and sat down on the spur too, with his boots hanging over the mud. "That's what we say. It's working the ebb. When it turns, it'll swing round on its posts like a vane on a church, and work the flood the other way. Its mouth to the sea and its tail inland." He sniffed. "They've only been on the flats since the winter. Before that it was kiddles. Now it's kiddles and stows, and Lord knows what by the summer."

"How do you know when it'll swing?" said Kiva.

"When the tide turns. Same as everything." Penhale looked at her sideways. "You'd know before me. With your fingers."

At half past ten, Pru said, "There's something under the mouth."

Kiva looked.

Under the surface of the brown water, between the two posts of Seven's stow, where the mouth went down into the creek, there was a darker place. It was not the stow. It lay in the bed of the creek, under the mouth, a long dark shape like the shadow of a log, and the water went round it in a little ruffle.

"Tear," said Penhale. "That's what's feeding it. The Company found it at the evening low. Under each stow, in the bed. Like a tap." He was quiet a moment. "Ivey's crew'll close that. Not you."

"Not us," said Kiva.

---

At ten minutes to eleven, a gust came off the sea that was bigger than the others.

Kiva saw it come. She saw it on the flats first, far out, a darkness going over the shining mud like the shadow of a cloud, very fast, coming toward them. She saw it reach the mouth of Mallow Creek, and the brown water in the creek stand up in ridges, and the ridges come up the creek against the ebb, toward the Tarn's stow and past it, toward theirs.

The Tarn's stow slackened and leaned and came back. Theirs did not.

Theirs had been leaning further than the Tarn's all morning; the upper creek was narrower and the ebb ran harder in it. When the gust hit, the water in the narrow creek did not slacken. It stopped dead against the gust and stood up, and then, as the gust went on, it came back. It came back up the creek in a long low lift, a surge, the wrong way, inland, against the ebb, and it went into the stow's mouth from behind.

The sleeve bellied out the wrong way. It bellied up out of the water, grey and streaming, and the two posts leaned hard inland.

And the top of the near post came off.

It came off on its own. Nobody had cut it. It cracked like a branch going in frost, and a yard of it fell into the creek on the downstream side, and it was a heap of grey ash as it fell. It hit the water and did not sink. It spread.

It spread on the brown water like milk going into tea, grey and fast, and the ebb, which had come back now that the gust had passed, took it and carried it downstream. Toward the Tarn's half. Toward the sea.

"*Pryce,*" said Yeo, from the spur. "*Flat.*"

Anwen was already moving.

She went down off the spur onto the mud of the east bank, in two strides, and her wall came up at her hip and went out over the water. It did not stand. She laid it down. She laid it flat on the brown surface, a hand deep, as Mace's line had laid six walls on the black water of Gannet Creek in the dark. It went out across the creek from the east bank like a boom of logs, silver, nearly to the west bank, and the grey fan came down the creek and met it and stopped.

Most of it stopped. It piled against the silver in a grey crust, steaming.

But some of it went under. Kiva saw it go. It went down under the edge of the wall, where the wall lay only a hand deep in water that was six feet deep, and it came up on the far side, thinner, and went on downstream toward the Tarn's stow.

"*Down a hand,*" said Kiva.

She did not know she was going to say it. It was Mace's. It was what Mace had said to Stroud and Tibb at Gannet Creek, and she had heard it from the hard, and it had gone into her somewhere and stayed.

Anwen heard it. Kiva saw her heels go into the mud. She saw the silver of the wall go down into the brown water, deeper, a hand, two. The grey stopped going under. It lay against the silver, steaming, and the ebb pushed on it, and Anwen leaned on her heels on the east bank with her teeth set and held it.

"*Net,*" said Yeo.

Ivey was already in the punt. The boatman pushed off. They came along the edge of the silver with a long-handled net, like a shrimper's net, and Ivey scooped. What came up was grey sludge, steaming, and she tipped it into a tin in the punt's bottom, and scooped again, and again, until the water against Anwen's wall was brown.

"*Lift,*" said Yeo.

Anwen lifted. The wall came up off the water and shut. She stood on the mud with her arms hanging and her face white.

"That's the creek's," said Yeo. She had her notebook open and was writing in it, standing, fast. "*A post shed a rung uncut on a surge against the ebb, before the slack. Held in part on a wall laid flat on the water by the Hold of Team Seven, on the officer's word. Netted. A part lost downstream.* That remark's the creek's, not yours." She looked down at Anwen. "Pryce. Who taught you to lay a wall on water?"

"Doran Mace, ma'am. Graywater. At Gannet Creek, in the dark, I watched him."

"You watched him once."

"Once, ma'am."

"And you did it." Yeo wrote something else. "And somebody told you down a hand."

"Rowan, ma'am."

Yeo looked at Kiva. Then she looked at the stow, which stood with one post whole and one cut short, a yard of it gone, leaning with the ebb again as if nothing had happened. The sleeve had gone back under the water. The white plates came out of its sides.

"It's still working," said Penhale, quite quietly. "On one and a half."

"The near post's still standing," said Yeo. "Short. It'll hold the mouth. You'll cut what's left of it at the slack, with the other." She shut her notebook. "That rung would have gone straight down the creek into the Tarn's stow, and through it, and out to sea. I'd sooner have it in a tin."

"It's wet," said Jude. He had come down to the edge of the spur and was looking at the tin in the punt. "It's been in the water. On the ebb."

"It's been in the water on the ebb," said Yeo. "It'll comb red. Everything that's been wet on a running tide since the autumn has combed red. We don't know why, and it's not mine to say." She looked at him. "It's still not in the sea."

Jude looked at the tin a long moment. Then he nodded, slowly.

Kiva was looking at the stow.

"Pru," she said. "Did you see it lean? Before the gust hit?"

Pru had been watching too. She was frowning.

"It leaned before," she said. "I think. The near post. It went inland a hair before the water stood up. As if it heard it coming."

"That's what I saw."

"Lord," said Penhale. He had heard. He was looking at the stow with his red beard blowing and his face gone quite still. "Lord. Things like that don't hear."

"I don't know that it heard," said Kiva. "I know it leaned first."

She wrote it down, on the left, where the things went that she had seen. *The upper stow leaned before the gust reached it. Finch saw it too.* She did not write anything on the right.

---

At half past eleven the channel was low enough to stand in.

The ebb had taken the creek down. It ran now in a narrow brown thread through the bottom of a great grey channel of mud, ten feet deep from the top of the banks, and the two stows stood in it with their posts up out of the mud like the stakes of a fence. Their long sleeves lay half out of the water now, grey, streaming, still working what was left of the ebb, still letting the white plates out of their sides. The tears in the bed under them were dark places in the mud, each with the thread of water ruffling round it.

"Purses," said Yeo. "Penhale. Show them."

Penhale went down into the channel first, in his thigh boots, to the knee in the mud, and Kiva and Pru went after him in theirs.

The mud was cold. It was cold through the boots at once, and it sucked at every step, so that she had to pull each foot out of it with a noise like a cork. Pru beside her went down to the shin and said something under her breath that she would not have said in front of Aske.

"You'll get used to it," said Penhale. "Or you won't. My brother never did. He went to sea instead." He was carrying a purse over his shoulder, a grey canvas sheet laced to an iron ring as wide as a cartwheel, with a drawstring threaded through eyes all round its edge. "That's why I'm a catcher and he's in a boat. He couldn't stand the mud. Said it got in his soul."

He laid the purse down in the mud under the near post of Seven's stow, a yard downstream of the dark place of the tear, open, flat, with the iron ring pressed into the mud all round so that it would not lift. The drawstring's two ends he brought together and knotted to a line, and the line he carried up the channel's side to the top of the bank, to an ash pole that one of Yeo's men had driven into the mud there.

"Under the post," he said. "Straight under. At the slack the rung comes straight down. No flood, no ebb. It falls like a stone into still water and lands in the purse, and you haul, and the drawstring closes, and it's shut." He showed Pru how to take the line in her fists. "One fathom. Two fathom. Three. That's how you know it's shut; three fathom of line and it stops. My dad counted it so. Everybody on this coast counts it so."

"Fathoms," said Pru.

"It's what a line's measured in. Your arms out, so." Penhale put his arms out wide. "That's a fathom. Three of those and the purse is closed." He grinned at her. "You'd make a coast catcher, miss. I told you at Gannet Creek."

"You did." Pru went red and did not mind it. "I've been thinking about it since."

They set the second purse under the far post. Then Penhale stood in the mud with his hands on his hips and looked at them both.

"There," he said. "That's yours set. I'll go and see to the Tarn's. Their girl's never set a purse in her life, and she's fifteen, and their captain's a lovely slow man who'll take a week to show her." He began to wade off down the channel toward the lower stow. Then he stopped and turned round in the mud. "Miss Rowan."

"Yes."

"What's *nobody?*"

She looked at him.

"I heard the Tarn's captain at the comb table," said Penhale. "Saying it under his breath. *Nobody, nobody.* Like a prayer. And your captain said you'd an Ember declared, on a beach, where nobody needs to carry anything anywhere." He scratched his red beard. "I've been wondering."

Kiva thought about how to say it. She did not want to say it the way it was written in the columns. She wanted to say it the way she would say it to anybody who might need it on a beach.

"It's a way of passing a thing," she said. "A thing that folds onto whoever's holding it. One person catches it on a ward and lets go. For a quarter of a count, nobody's got it. Then I take it, with one door, and carry it, and let go. Nobody again. Then a still door plants it, somewhere away from people." She held up her right fist. "Nobody ever holds it long enough for it to fold. And nobody ever holds it at the same time as anybody else."

Penhale looked at her fist. Then he laughed, loud and cheerful, so that a curlew got up out of the marsh grass on the far bank and went away crying.

"Nobody," he said. "On this coast nobody passes anything to nobody. We hand it, man to man, both hands on it. You'd put half the Company out of a job." He shook his head, still laughing. "I'd like to see it. I'll not, likely. I've been reserve on four Hunts, and never been onto the mud yet."

"I'd sooner you didn't see it," said Kiva. "It's only for when it's wanted."

"Then I'll hope not to." He touched his forehead to her, a little salute, with mud on his fingers. "Coombe's page. The far edge to the second. They say it's at the printer."

"It is."

"We give it the corner-hand. It's in the book. Has been since my dad." He looked down the channel toward the Tarn's stow, where Bridie was standing in the mud beside its posts with a purse over her shoulder, looking at it as if it might bite. "When it's printed, I'll read it. I'm not one for reading. But I'll read that."

He waded off. Kiva watched him go, broad and red-bearded, with the mud to his knees, talking already. She could hear him from fifty paces, low and cheerful, telling Bridie Hebden about his brother who couldn't stand the mud.

---

She went back up the channel's side to the pole on the bank, where the line of the first purse was tied.

It was an ash pole, as thick as her wrist, driven into the mud at the channel's edge. It went down into the mud, and the mud went down to whatever was under the creek, and the creek went out to the sea.

Pru knelt at the second pole, three paces off, with the line in her fists. Anwen was on the bank above with her hands in her sleeves. Jude stood at the channel's lip with his edge unlit and his good hand open at his side, looking at the near post of the stow, the one that had been cut short. On the spur behind them, Yeo stood with her notebook and Perrin stood beside her with a brass glass to his eye.

Kiva took her left hand out of her coat. It was warm. It had been warm three mornings. She laid the first two fingers on the ash pole, at a light touch, above the mud, and did not lean.

*Something's pushing.*

And then, because she held them there, the other thing.

*It keeps time.*

It was the ebb. It was long and slow and uneven, a long lift and a short one, the way the flood had been at Gannet Creek in the dark. Only it was going the other way, outward, toward the sea, and it was fading. Each lift was shorter than the last. And under it, faint, so faint that she was not sure, there was the town. Four and four.

Pru reached over without being asked and took her right wrist.

"Seventy-two," said Pru, after a while, with her eyes on her own watch. "You're calm."

"I'm not calm."

"Your pulse is." Pru let go. "It's the water. Not you."

Kiva kept her fingers on the pole. Down the channel, Penhale had finished with the Tarn's purses and was wading back up toward the spur, still talking. Bridie stood in the mud beside the lower stow's near post with the line of her first purse in her fists, and Trewin stood on the bank above her with his hat on and his hands clasped. The thread of water in the channel's bottom ran seaward, slower now, and slower.

The ebb lifted. It lifted less. It lifted less again.

At twelve minutes past twelve, between one breath and the next, the fingers said nothing.

Not *pushing.* Not *keeps time.* Nothing at all.

Kiva lifted her right hand and held it up, flat, so that Yeo on the spur could see it.

Yeo looked at the creek. Nothing had changed that anybody could see. The thread of brown water lay in its bed. The two stows stood in the mud with their sleeves half out of the water. And then, slowly, all together, the four posts on Mallow Creek came upright out of the mud and stood straight, like the stakes of a fence on a still day, and the sleeves lay down.

"*Slack,*" said Yeo. "Twelve minutes past. Seven. Your stow."

---

## CHAPTER — manuscript/chapter-30.md

# Chapter 30 — Kiva: Slack

Kellan Renn had the sum done before the rung hit the water, and it did not come out.

He was standing in the mud of Skerry Pill at twenty-five past eleven with the line of Halden's second purse in his left hand and a mallet in his right, driving its pole. The pill was narrower than a creek and deeper, a cut in the mud no more than twenty paces across, running seaward between banks of marsh grass. Two forms stood in it. Halden's was a stow, two grey posts and a sleeve, forty paces above the kiddle. The Ropewalk's was a kiddle, seven posts across the channel nearer the sea, leaning on the ebb with the white plates coming out of its skins. And in the pill's mouth, where it opened onto the flats, there was the lodger.

It lay on the mud at the water's edge where the Company had left it. It was grey, and long, longer than a man, as thick as a wrist, and it did not look like anything. It looked like a cable somebody had dropped off a boat. It had not moved since dawn. Bray had said so at the spur, in her voice like a foghorn, and every one of them had looked at it, and then not looked at it, which was harder.

The gust came off the sea at twenty-five past.

Kellan saw it on the flats, a darkness going over the shining mud like the shadow of a cloud. It came up the pill against the ebb, and the brown water stood up in ridges, and the Ropewalk's kiddle leaned hard inland all at once, all seven posts, and the fourth from the north bank cracked at the top.

A yard of it fell. It was a heap of grey ash as it fell. It hit the water and spread.

"*Maud,*" said Carrow. "*Boom.*"
