She said it at once. Kellan saw her say it, and saw that she did not think about it. It was a word she had said a hundred times, on this coast, at this kind of moment, and it came out of her the way *down* comes out of a marshal.

Lyle was on the south bank, twenty paces below the kiddle. She stepped down onto the mud at the water's edge and laid.

Kellan had seen her lay in a lane and at a lock. He had never seen her lay on water. Her ward came up at her hip and went out over the pill, silver, and lay down flat on the brown surface across the whole width of the channel, bank to bank, and her feet went into the mud under it in the same breath. He felt them go. Everybody on the beat felt them go. It was like a post being driven into the ground beside you.

The grey fan came down the pill on the ebb and met the silver and stopped.

All of it stopped. Nothing went under. Graywater's walls had let a third of a rung under at Gannet Creek; Lyle's let nothing under. The grey piled against her laid ward in a long steaming crust from bank to bank, and the ebb leaned on it, and she stood on the mud with her feet in the floor of the pill and her wall in the water and did not move an inch.

It was beautiful. Kellan thought so, and was ashamed afterward that he had stopped to think it.

"*Net,*" said Bray, from the spur.

Then Treen said, "*Run.*"

Kellan turned his head.

Treen was on the north bank, small and dark, with her sleeves rolled to the elbow and her eyes on the pill's mouth. She had not shouted. She had said it the way she said it in a lane, short and flat, for one person.

He looked where she was looking.

The lodger had lifted its tip.

It lay along the mud at the water's edge as it had lain since dawn, grey, dull, like a dropped cable. But its tip was off the mud now. It was up, a hand's breadth, and turned, and pointing. It pointed up the pill. It pointed at the silver lying flat across the water forty paces away, and at the girl standing on the bank behind it with her feet in the mud.

Kellan had watched a lodger's tip turn in Coombe's cage in the second month, from the rail of the Pit, and had thought that he understood it. He had not understood how fast it would be.

The lodger came off the mud into the water. It went up the pill along the south bank, under the surface, the way an eel goes, straight, without any of the bends a snake makes. Kellan could see it as a ripple and a darkness. It was coming at a man's walking pace, and then faster.

"*Run,*" said Treen again. "*Maud. Run.*"

Lyle did not run.

Kellan saw her hear it. He saw her head turn a fraction toward the north bank. And then he saw her look at the grey crust lying against her wall, forty drams of the Board's ash, steaming, with the whole of the ebb leaning on it. If she shut, it went. It went down the pill on the ebb, straight into the mouth, onto the flats, into the sea. It went into the water where the lodger was.

She looked back at the crust. She did not move.

"*Maud,*" said Carrow. "*Run. Shut and run. Leave it.*"

It was too late for that. Kellan knew it was too late even as Carrow said it. The ripple was ten paces from Lyle's boots.

He dropped the mallet.

He did not decide to. He found afterward that he had done the sum, and that the sum had come out, and that he had not waited for it. The sum was that he was a blade, forty paces off, with an Ember door that would light on his right fist in less than a count, and that the lodger was a cord, and a cord can be cut. And there was nobody else on the beat who was a blade and nearer. Halden's sum was something else. He would do Halden's sum later.

"*Renn,*" said Pettit, behind him.

He was already running.

He ran across the mud of the south bank toward Lyle. The mud took his boots to the ankle at every stride, and the ripple in the water was going faster than he was. He saw it reach the bank below Lyle's boots. He saw it come out of the water onto the mud, grey, dripping, and go up over the toe of her left boot, and lie there.

Its tip went into the place where her ankle met the mud.

Kellan saw her wall go dark.

It did not shut. A ward that shuts goes in on itself, folds, closes like a hand. This went out. It went out the way a lamp goes out when the oil is gone: it was silver, and then it was grey, and then it was nothing, and the water lay brown where it had been. The grey crust that had been piled against it went down the pill on the ebb all at once, in a long fan, steaming, past the girl, past the mouth, onto the flats.

Lyle did not fall. She was still standing. Her feet were still in the mud. Her stance was still open; Kellan could feel it, a post driven into the ground. But she was standing quite still with her hands open at her sides, and her face had no expression on it at all.

He reached her.

The lodger lay across the mud from the water's edge to her boot. Its tip was still in the place at her ankle. Its body went back into the pill, grey, as thick as a wrist, lying on the mud in a long curve like a cable dropped off a boat.

Kellan lit.

His Ember came down his right fist. He did not cut toward the girl. He cut down, into the mud, a pace from her boot, through the grey thing lying on it. He cut as Jude had cut one on the coping of a race in the Pit in the second month, by lighting above it and letting the edge come straight down. The edge went through the lodger and into the mud under it, and the mud hissed.

The lodger did nothing. It did not writhe. Its two halves lay on the mud, and went grey, and then greyer, like ash cooling. The tip came out of the place at Lyle's ankle. It fell onto her boot, and lay there, and did not lift again.

Kellan shut.

He stood in the mud a pace from Lyle with his right fist dark and his heart going very fast, faster than it had gone at the Proprietor's table, faster than in any lane. He looked at her face.

"Miss Lyle," he said.

She did not look at him. She was looking at the water where her wall had been.

"It's quiet," she said.

Treen got there first after him, and then Carrow, running with nothing open, and then Tolver, and then Bray, who did not run but walked very fast and seemed to cover the mud as if it were a floor.

Lyle had shut her stance. Kellan felt the post go out of the ground. She stood in the mud on her own feet, with nothing open, and she lifted her right hand to her hip, where a ward comes up, and held it there.

Nothing came up.

She held it there for a long time. Kellan watched her face. It was the square careful face that he had seen at the mast and on her knees in a lane. It did not change.

"Maud," said Carrow.

"There's nothing there." Lyle lowered her hand. "I can't find it. It's like reaching for a step in the dark and it's not there." She put her hand back to her hip. "It's not shut. It's gone."

"Try."

"I am trying." Lyle's voice did not rise. "I'm opening my stance. That's there." Kellan felt the post go into the mud again, and come out. "I'm opening my ward. There's nothing to open." She looked at Carrow at last. "It's quiet. Juniper. It's all quiet. I always said laid was quiet. It isn't. This is."

Carrow did not say anything. Kellan watched her. She stood in the mud with her hands at her sides and her quick blunt face gone very still, and she did not reach for Lyle, and she did not look away from her.

"I called boom," said Carrow.

"You always call boom."

"I called it laid." Carrow's voice was quite steady. "I didn't say laid. I didn't have to. I've never said boom and meant anything else." She was quiet for a moment. "Treen called run."

"I heard her."

"Why didn't you?"

"Forty drams," said Lyle. "And the lodger was in the mouth, and if I'd shut, it would all have gone down onto it." She looked down the pill at the grey fan, which had gone out onto the flats now and was spreading on the shallow water there, steaming, a long way off. "It went anyway. And so did I."

Bray had reached them. She stood over the halves of the lodger on the mud and looked at them, and then at Lyle, and then at Kellan.

"Who gave the word?" she said. Her voice was so deep that Kellan felt it in his chest.

"Nobody, ma'am," said Kellan.

"Nobody." Bray took a notebook out of her coat. It was the same as Yeo's, a Company book, green. She wrote in it standing, quickly. "*The Hold of the Ropewalk's first four laid a wall on the water on her captain's word, to hold a rung shed on a surge. A lodger in the pill's mouth went to it. The catcher called run. The Hold held. Entered at the left ankle. The ward gone; not shut.*" She stopped writing and looked at Lyle. "Gone, Miss Lyle?"

"Gone, ma'am."

Bray wrote it. "*The blade of Halden License left his own work and cut the lodger on the mud, on no officer's word.*" She looked at Kellan. "That's a remark. I'll say whose it is. It's the blade's." She closed the book. "And I'd have given the word. I'd want that read, by whoever reads it. I'd have given it if I'd been a breath quicker, and I wasn't."

"Thank you, ma'am."

"Don't thank me. I've just cost your house a dram on the mark." Bray put the book away. "Get back to your purse. The slack's in three-quarters of an hour, and you've a pole half driven."

---

He went back across the mud to Halden's stow.

Pettit was standing at the purse pole where Kellan had left it, with the mallet in his hand. He had driven the pole himself. The line was tied off. He looked at Kellan coming across the mud with his heavy fair face quite still, the sleepy face that made people underestimate him, and he did not say anything until Kellan was beside him.

"I said your name," said Pettit.

"I heard you."

"I didn't say it to stop you." Pettit gave him the mallet. "I said it because I was going to go myself, and you were nearer, and I wanted you to know somebody was coming after you." He looked down the pill at Lyle, standing in the mud with Carrow and Treen. "That's all. I'd have gone. I'm a still door. I'd have been no use. I'd have gone."

Kellan took the mallet.

"It's a dram on the mark," he said. "Bray's remark. And I left the second line half set. If it's not right at the slack—"

"Then it's not right at the slack, and Yarrow will haul what he can, and we'll be a place lower on a board." Pettit shrugged. He did it slowly, as he did everything. "I can count. I know what a place is worth. I've been counting places all week." He was quiet for a moment. "I'd expect nothing else of you. You know that. That's what you say."

"I say it about other people."

"I know. I've heard you." Pettit almost smiled. "I'm saying it about you."

Kellan did the sum, at last, aloud.

"Forty drams of the Board's ash gone onto the flats, wet, on the ebb. It'll comb red if anybody nets it, and nobody will. A dram off Halden on the mark, mine. A line half set, so maybe a part of our second rung in the pill at the slack, and a remark for that as well, mine. The Ropewalk's whole rung gone, and a remark, theirs." He stopped. "And a door."

"And a door," said Pettit.

"It doesn't go in a sum," said Kellan. "I don't know what column it goes in."

"No," said Pettit. "Nor do I."

He took the line of the second purse in his hands and began, slowly, to check the knot.

---

At twelve minutes past twelve, on Mallow Creek, Kiva's fingers said nothing, and the four posts stood up out of the mud.

"*Slack,*" said Yeo. "Twelve minutes past. Seven. Your stow."

Kiva did not hear any of what had happened on Skerry Pill. She would not hear it until three o'clock, at the gate. On Mallow Creek there was only the slack, and the stow, and the mud.

It was very still. It was stiller than Gannet Creek had been at midnight, because there was daylight on it and she could see how still it was. The thread of water in the channel's bottom lay in its bed like a ribbon, not running either way. The marsh grass on the west bank stood up straight and did not move. The white plates that had been coming out of the stow's sleeve all morning had stopped coming; the last of them lay on the mud where the ebb had left them, melting. And the stow stood in the channel, its two posts upright, one whole and one cut short a yard, with the mouth hanging slack between them and the long grey sleeve lying down on the mud behind, downstream, as flat as a coat dropped on a floor.

"Talley," said Kiva.

Jude went down into the channel.

He went down the bank on his good leg and his bad, carefully, into the mud to the shin, and waded across to the near post, the short one. He stood beside it. It was the height of his own shoulder now, a yard shorter than it had been. He lit his edge on his right fist, short and bright, and held it at his side, and did not lift it.

Kiva was on her knees at the first purse pole on the bank, with the line in her fists and her two fingers resting on the ash pole above them. Pru was on her knees at the second pole, three paces downstream, with her own line. Anwen was on the bank behind them with her hands out of her sleeves. On the spur, Yeo stood with her notebook and Perrin beside her with the glass.

Kiva looked at Jude's heel.

It was in the mud. She could not see a heel in mud; she could see his boot, and the line of his leg above it, and the way his weight sat. She watched it. She watched it come down off the ball of his foot onto the heel, the way it did before he cut. She had been watching him cut since the second month. She knew it as she knew her own name.

"*Not yet,*" she said.

He did not look round. She saw his head go a little on one side, listening.

The weight settled. The knee came straight.

"*Now.*"

The light went level across the channel, and the top of the near post came off.

It fell straight down. There was no ebb to take it and no flood. It fell like a stone into the still shallow water at the post's foot, and the water closed over it with a grey bloom, and it went down onto the purse lying open in the mud.

Kiva hauled.

The line came in through her fists, fast, hand over hand, the way Pru had hauled at Gannet Creek. Under the water the drawstring would be closing the purse round the rung like the neck of a bag. She hauled until the line stopped coming. Then she put her weight back on it and held.

"One fathom," she said. "Two fathom." She did not know she was going to count it. It was Penhale's count, and it had gone into her somewhere and stayed, like Mace's *down a hand.* "Three. Shut. It's shut."

"*Shut,*" said Yeo, from the spur, and wrote.

"*Far edge,*" said Pru.

Kiva had not looked up yet. She was still on her knees with the line in her fists. She looked up now.

The near post was a stump. The mouth of the stow had hung between the near post and the far one; now, on the near side, it had nothing to hang from. It came down. It came down slowly, a grey curtain falling into the slack water. And behind it the sleeve, the long grey sleeve that had been lying down flat on the mud downstream like a dropped coat, moved.

Its far end lifted.

The closed end, the end furthest from the posts, came up off the mud. It came up in a long grey lip, streaming, frosting the mud as it left it. And it bent over, slowly, as a page turns. It was turning toward the nearest line. It was turning toward the first purse's line, and toward the girl on her knees at the end of it.

Kiva let go of the line.

She let go and stepped back on the bank, without turning round, as Rhea had stepped back off a skin in the Corn Exchange yard, as Penhale's Company man had stepped back on the hard at Gannet Creek. The purse was shut. Shut was shut. A shut purse is a sealed bag, on this coast.

The fold came over and came down. It came down on the mud and on the slack line where it lay on the bank, flat, with a wet slap, and lay there steaming, a yard from her knees.

Then it lay still. It went greyer, slowly. It did not lift again.

"*Hold,*" said Yeo. She had stepped to the edge of the spur. She was looking at the fold, and at Kiva on the bank above it, and at Pru on her knees three paces off. "Seven. Who called that?"

"Finch, ma'am," said Kiva. "My second."

Yeo looked at Pru for a long moment.

"Finch," she said. "From where?"

"From here, ma'am." Pru had not moved from her knees. Her face was scarlet and her voice was steady. "I was watching it. Not the post. The far end of the sleeve. I've had it from Pike. The second watches the far edge and says it once." She swallowed. "I said it once."

Yeo wrote. She wrote for some time.

"*The near post cut at twelve and fourteen,*" she said aloud, as she wrote. "*Shut. The mouth fell; the far end of the stow lifted and folded toward the catcher's line. The second called far edge, once, from three paces. The catcher came off the line. The fold lay down on the bank where the catcher had been.*" She stopped. "I've not seen that before. I've seen the far edge come. I've not seen anybody watching for it."

"It's a place, ma'am," said Pru. "Not a person. Coombe says."

"So she does," said Yeo, and went on writing.

The far post came down at twenty-four minutes past.

It was the tall one, whole, standing in the middle of the channel with its half of the mouth hanging from it and the sleeve, now, lying in a heap where it had folded. Jude waded to it through the slack water. Kiva watched his heel.

"*Not yet.*"

His weight settled.

"*Now.*"

The top of the far post came off and went straight down into the still water, onto the second purse. Pru hauled. She hauled hand over hand, scarlet to the ears, with the line coming in through her fists, and leaned back on it when it stopped.

"One fathom," said Pru. "Two. Three. *Shut.*"

And the stow died.

Kiva had never seen a thing die all at once like that. The kiddle at Gannet Creek had been cut three posts at a time, and then four, and had gone to ash in the mud slowly, over the low water, while she was walking away from it in the dark. This went at once. The two stumps went grey and soft and slumped in the mud like candles in a hot room. The mouth went grey. The sleeve, lying in its fold on the bank and across the mud, went grey all along its length, and then thin, and then it was a film of ash lying on the mud like frost on a window.

Only the dark place in the creek bed was left, under where the mouth had been. The tear. The thread of slack water lay round it in a ruffle.

"*Dead,*" said Yeo. "Seven's stow. Twenty-nine minutes past. Two posts, two purses shut, and a rung in the net's tin from the ebb." She shut her notebook. "That's clean work. I'll say so on the slate." She looked down the spur. "Ivey. Your crew. The upper tear's yours."

Ivey came along the spur with the closing crew behind her, four Company men in green and their officer, a short grey man with his sleeves rolled. They went down into the channel past Jude, who was coming out of it on his good leg and his bad with his edge shut and his face white. They knelt in the mud round the dark place in the bed, with their hands out over it. The light under their hands was steady and white.

Kiva sat back on her heels on the bank.

She looked at her hands. They were shaking. The left one, the warm one, with its two fingers that bent and gripped and told her nothing, was shaking as much as the right. She put them both flat on her knees.

"Pru," she said.

"Don't," said Pru, three paces off, still on her knees with the second line slack in her fists. "Don't say anything. I'll go red and I'll not be able to haul anything for an hour."

"I wasn't going to say anything."

"Yes, you were." Pru looked at her sideways. "You were going to say *well called.* I could see it in your face. Don't. I'd sooner you said it to Pike, in a letter, and let him say it to me."

Kiva did not say it. She sat on the bank with her hands flat on her knees and looked at the film of grey ash on the mud where the stow had been, and at the Company crew kneeling round the tear with their hands out and the white light under them.

She put the first two fingers of her left hand back on the ash pole. Lightly. Without leaning.

Nothing.

The slack held. It was twenty-nine minutes past twelve, and the slack had been holding for seventeen minutes, and it would hold for twenty minutes more, near enough, if the wind held. The channel lay quite still. Down at the Tarn's half, sixty paces off, the lower stow stood in the bend with its two posts upright and its sleeve lying flat on the mud behind it.

---

The Tarn cut their near post at twenty-five to one.

Kiva watched it from the bank. Their blade was a big red-haired boy she did not know, and he cut from the mud as Jude had, standing beside the post. Bridie called it. Kiva could hear her from sixty paces, high and quick and northern. *Not yet. Now.* The rung went straight down into the slack water. Bridie hauled.

"*Shut,*" she called. "*One fathom, two, three. Shut.*"

She had Penhale's count. Kiva heard Penhale laugh on the spur, loud and pleased, and say something to Yeo that she could not hear.

The near post was a stump. The mouth fell. Kiva watched the far end of the Tarn's sleeve, lying on the mud downstream of the posts on the west side of the channel, and waited for it to lift.

It did not lift.

It lay on the mud. It lay as flat as Seven's had lain before the cut, flat as a dropped coat, and did not move. The Tarn's corner-hand, a thin boy in brown and buff on the west bank, was standing at the far side of it with the line of their second purse in his fists, and he was looking at the sleeve too, and it lay still.

"It's not folding," said Pru, beside her, very low.

"It's still on a post." Kiva looked at the far post of the lower stow. It stood in the bend, in the deep side of the channel, upright, whole, with its half of the mouth hanging from it. "Ours was cut short. Theirs isn't. It's still got a post to stand on."

"So it doesn't fold."

"So far it doesn't fold."

The Tarn's blade was getting into the punt.

That was the bend. Yeo had said it at nine. The channel was deeper on the outside of the bend, and the far post stood in the deep side, and at the bottom of the slack there was still a yard of water round its foot. A blade could not stand beside it in the mud. He had to be beside it in a boat. The boatman held the punt against the post with his pole. The red-haired boy stood up in it, unsteady, with his edge unlit, and Trewin stood on the east bank above with the punt's line in his two hands, holding it as he held everything.

It took time. A punt is a slow thing in a yard of water and a foot of mud. The boatman pushed it off the bank and it stuck, and he pushed again, and it came free and slid out toward the post and went past it, and he had to bring it back. Kiva watched the clock in her own head. Twenty to one. Eighteen minutes to.

The second purse's line came up on the west side of the channel. It came up from the purse lying open in the mud under the far post, across the mud, and up the west bank to its pole. The lying sleeve lay across that mud. The corner-hand on the west bank had the line at the pole. But the line had caught. Kiva could see it from where she sat; it had caught on something in the mud, half way, and lay in a sharp bend instead of a curve, and if the purse were hauled like that it would not close.

Bridie saw it too. Kiva saw her see it.

She was on the east bank, with the first line slack in her hands. She put the line down. She called something across to the corner-hand, and he shook his head, and called back, and pointed. To clear the line, somebody would have to go down onto the west mud, across the thread, and walk out along the line to where it had caught, and free it. And the line lay across the sleeve.

Bridie looked up the spur at Yeo.

Kiva could not hear what she said. She saw her say it. She saw her point at the sleeve lying on the mud, and at the caught line across it, and say a few words, a question.

She saw Yeo look at the sleeve.

Yeo looked at it for a long breath. Then she answered. Kiva could not hear the words, but she knew what they were. She had heard them before, at a coal wharf in Meridian in the frost, from a man with his heel on a white patch of hem. *A hem that lies down is ground. That's the book.*

Bridie went down onto the west mud.

She went across the thread of slack water in two strides, in her boots, with her plaits pinned up round her head and the long cord wound twice round her wrist. She went out along the caught line. She stepped onto the sleeve where it lay across the mud, flat and grey and still, and walked on it to where the line was caught, and knelt on it, and began to free the line with her fingers.

It held her. It was hard. It did not take a boot. It was ground.

Kiva had her two fingers on the ash pole. They said nothing. The slack held.

"Pru," she said.

"I see her."

"It's still on a post."

"I know." Pru had not moved. "Kiva. I know."

The red-haired boy was beside the far post in the punt. Trewin had the line taut. The boatman leaned on his pole. Yeo had come along the spur to the Tarn's half and stood above them on the stone with her notebook open, and Penhale stood beside her, red-bearded, in his thigh boots, with his hands in the pockets of his smock.

Bridie had freed the line. Kiva saw it come straight in the mud, a curve again. Bridie stood up on the sleeve and turned to come back.

The far post of the lower stow moved.

It was a quarter to one. Kiva knew afterward that it was a quarter to one, or a minute before, because Perrin had the glass and the watch, and he wrote it down. She did not know it then. She knew only that her two fingers were on the ash pole and they said *nothing,* still, *nothing,* the slack holding, the water still, the grass still, the thread lying in its bed like a ribbon. And the far post moved.

It leaned.

It leaned inland, a hair, the way the upper stow's post had leaned at ten to eleven before the gust had reached it. Only there was no gust. There was no flood. The water had not moved. Kiva's fingers on the pole said that the water had not moved, and the grass on the west bank stood up straight, and the thread lay in its bed. And the far post of the Tarn's stow leaned inland on the turn of a tide that had not turned.

And the stow swung.

It swung on the post as a vane swings on a church when the wind comes round. The half of the mouth that still hung from it came round, and the long grey sleeve that had been lying flat on the mud downstream came round after it. It lifted off the mud. It lifted the whole of its length at once, streaming, grey, and swung round on the standing post like a door on its hinge, across the channel, to face the sea that had not yet begun to come in.

Bridie was standing on it.

She went down. She went down on the mud of the west side as the sleeve came up from under her, and the sleeve came round over her. It came up and over in a long grey fold, frosting as it came, and it came down. It came down on the mud on the west side of the channel with Bridie Hebden under it, flat, with a wet sound that Kiva heard from sixty paces.

The corner-hand at the pole had the far edge, by the book. He was hauling. He had the second purse's line in his fists, and he was hauling it, because the line was clear now and the purse was under the post and the post had not been cut, and he did not know what he was hauling. He had his eyes on the line.

"*Far edge,*" said Kiva.

She said it as loud as she had ever said anything. It went across sixty paces of still mud and still water, and the corner-hand did not turn his head, because it was not his name and it was not his voice, and nobody on that half of the creek was listening for it.

"*Down,*" said Yeo, on the spur. "*A person down. Penhale.*"

Penhale went.

He went off the spur and down the east bank into the channel in two strides, the way he had gone into the mud a hundred times. He went across the thread of water in his thigh boots and up onto the west mud. He did not hurry and he did not hesitate. He went to the fold as a man goes to a gate he has opened every day of his life.

He went to the near edge.

That was the book. Kiva knew it as she watched him. She had never read the Company's book, but she knew it, because it was the same as every other book: when a person is under a skin, the catcher goes to the near edge, and gets his ward under it, and lifts, and the person rolls out under the ward. It was a page and a half. It had been the right page for forty years.

Penhale's ward came up at his hip. It was broad and low and grey, a coast catcher's ward, and he knelt and put it under the near edge of the fold where it lay on the mud, and he lifted.

The fold came up. It came up like the cover of a book. Kiva saw Bridie's arm under it, her sleeve, a plait come loose, the brown and buff of her coat. She saw Penhale lean in under the fold with his ward up over his head like a roof, and reach for the arm with his free hand, and take it.

The fold had two ends.

Kiva saw that too late. Everybody saw it too late. It was a sleeve. It had a mouth, at the post, and a closed end, a long way off down its length. The fold that had come down over Bridie was its middle. The closed end was still coming round. It was still swinging on the post, across the channel, slowly, the last of the long grey sleeve, the far end of it, the end that had been lying furthest downstream on the mud all morning while everybody watched the mouth.

It came round over the near edge. It came round over Penhale's back.

It came down.

It came down on top of the fold and on top of the man kneeling under it with his ward over his head and his hand on a girl's arm. It came down flat, grey, frosting, with a sound that Kiva did not hear, because she was on her feet by then and running, and Pru was running beside her, and somebody on the spur was shouting her name.

The far post of the Tarn's stow stood up straight in the channel's bend.

And under her boots, on the mud of the east bank, as she ran, the slack held. Her fingers were not on anything; she did not need them. The marsh grass on the west bank stood up straight and did not stir. The thread of water in the channel's bottom lay in its bed like a ribbon, running neither way. Nothing on Mallow Creek had turned but the stow.

---

## CHAPTER — manuscript/chapter-31.md

# Chapter 31 — Kiva: Nobody

Sixty paces along the east bank of Mallow Creek is a long way in mud.

Kiva ran it. She ran it with Pru beside her and the mud taking her boots to the ankle at every stride, and the whole time she was running she was looking at the grey fold lying on the west mud across the channel, flat, still, with nothing under it that moved. It lay there as a sheet lies over furniture in a shut house. At one edge of it, the edge nearest the thread of water, a man's boot stuck out. It was a thigh boot, black, with mud on it to the knee.

She stopped on the east bank opposite it, at the channel's edge, with the thread of slack water between her and the fold. She stopped because the rule said stop.

*A person down on the mud is the officer's crew's. The fours stand off.*

The officer's reserve was under the fold.

"*Ivey,*" said Yeo, on the spur.

Her voice did not rise. It went up the creek, flat and brisk, to the place sixty paces upstream where Ivey and the closing crew were kneeling in the mud round Seven's tear with their hands out and the white light under them. Kiva saw Ivey's head come up. She saw her look down the creek, and see, and get up off her knees.

She would be a minute. A minute through mud, and then she would be at the fold, and she would do what the book said. She would go to the near edge. She would put her ward under it and lift.

The fold's far end lay on top of the near edge, where it had come down across Penhale's back.

"Ma'am," said Kiva.

Yeo did not look at her. Yeo was looking at the far post of the lower stow, standing whole in the channel's bend, with the red-haired boy of the Tarn standing beside it in the punt and the boatman holding the punt against it with his pole. The boy's edge was unlit. He was looking up at the spur with his face like paper.

"*Cut it,*" said Yeo. "*The far post. Now.*"

"*No,*" said Pru.

She said it before Kiva could. She said it loud, from the bank, scarlet to the hair, to an officer of the Saltmere Company who had not asked her anything.

Yeo turned her head.

"If you cut the post, the skin folds," said Pru. Her voice shook and kept going. "From its far edge. Toward the nearest line. We saw it at Gannet Creek, a cut post's skin, it folds onto whatever's nearest. Ma'am. They're the nearest. They're under it."

Yeo looked at her for one breath.

"*Hold,*" she said to the punt. "*Don't cut.*" The red-haired boy took his fist away from the post as if it were hot.

Then there was nothing anybody could do. Kiva stood on the bank and knew it. The book said lift, and lifting brought the far end over the lifter. The post could not be cut, because the skin would fold tighter. Nobody could go onto the mud but the officer's crew, and the officer's crew was Ivey, sixty paces off in the mud, coming, and Ivey would lift.

"Ma'am."

It was Perrin.

He had come along the spur from Seven's half. He had not run; he could not run. He had walked as fast as a man walks with a cracked rib, with his hand pressed to his side and his face grey, and he stood at Yeo's shoulder now on the stone.

"There's a way," he said. "It's three people. Two of them are on that bank."

Yeo looked at him.

"Finch catches the fold on her ward," said Perrin. "Rowan carries it, with her Ember. It's declared; I declared it at eight. The third plants it. On this bank, away from them. Nobody holds it long enough for it to fold on them." He breathed in, as far as he could. "It's been done on a yard. Twice before Coombe. Once on Sunday with him." He pointed. "He's the third."

Trewin was on the east bank below the spur, with the punt's line in his two hands. He had not let go of it. He was looking at the fold across the channel with his gatepost face gone quite white, at the place where a plait of brown hair had come loose from under the grey and lay on the mud.

"I'll plant," said Trewin. He did not look away from the plait. "Ma'am. If you say."

Yeo looked at the fold. She looked at Ivey, forty paces off now, coming down the west mud as fast as anybody can come through mud. She looked at Kiva and Pru on the bank. It was three seconds, perhaps. Kiva watched her face through all three of them, and saw an officer of thirty years on the coast decide a thing that was not in any book she had ever read.

"It's mine," said Yeo. "On my word. Do it."

Kiva went down off the bank into the channel. Pru came after her.

The thread of slack water was a yard wide and a hand deep. They went across it in one stride and up onto the west mud, and Kiva went to the fold. Not to the near edge. To the far end, the closed end of the sleeve, where it lay heaped over the near edge and over whatever was under the near edge, grey and frosting, with the cold coming off it in a breath that she could feel on her face.

She knelt beside it. Pru knelt beside her.

"It's ten paces long," said Pru. Her voice was very small. "I can't fold ten paces. My ward's the width of a doormat. I fold a rung. I fold a cord."

"Don't fold it," said Kiva.

Pru looked at her.

"Don't lift it," said Kiva. She heard her own voice and made it level. "It folds to the nearest thing. That's all it ever does. On a wharf it folded to a catcher. At Gannet Creek it folded to a man on a line. Up the creek just now it folded to my line and I came off it. It wants to fold. Let it come to you." She pointed at the mud beside the fold's heaped end, a pace off. "Put your ward there. Low. At your hip, the way you catch at a can. Don't put it under anything. Be the nearest thing. And when it comes over onto your tray, finish it. It's already folding. You're only finishing the fold."

Pru looked at the place on the mud. She looked at her own two hands.

"And then nobody," she said.

"And then nobody. I'll say it. You let go on the word."

Pru's ward came up at her hip.

It came up low and small and flat, silver, a tray the width of a doormat, a pace from the heaped grey end of the sleeve. It was the smallest ward Kiva had ever seen lit on a beach. It would not have stopped a child. It was the nearest thing.

Kiva lit.

She lit nothing but her Ember. Declared, single, off her own thimble, by her captain's voice at eight that morning on a causeway. It came up glad under her breastbone, as it always came, as though it had been waiting by the door, and the bar came down her right fist, short and bright, and she knelt on the mud with it at her side and did not lift it.

On the east bank, six paces off across the thread, Trewin had let go of the punt's line. He had come down off the bank into the channel. He stood on the east mud with his boots square and his hands open at his sides and his eyes on Kiva's mouth.

"*Ready,*" he said. His voice was very deep, and quite steady.

The fold moved.

It did it slowly. The heaped closed end of the sleeve, lying across the near edge and across Penhale's back, lifted. It lifted off whatever it lay on, a hand, two hands, grey and streaming, frosting the air. It lifted the way it had lifted off the mud when Seven's near post was cut, in a long grey lip, and it bent, slowly, as a page turns.

It turned toward Pru.

It came off the man under it and turned in the air and came down onto Pru's tray, the whole heavy grey end of it, as a wet sail comes down onto a deck. Kiva saw Pru's arms take it. She saw them go down an inch under the weight and come up again. She saw the cold go into Pru's hands at once, the fingers whitening at the tips.

"*Fold,*" said Kiva.

Pru folded.

She did it as she had done it at the Bench on Monday, and at the gate mast, and on a yard in the second month with Coombe on the step: from the far edge. She tapped the grey once with her right hand and brought the tray in on itself, the far edge first, and the grey came in with it. It came in heavy and slow. It was not a cord. It was not a rung. It was ten paces of live skin folding into itself on a doormat of silver, and it came in, and in, to the size of a loaf, a grey lump on Pru's tray that pulled at it like an anchor and steamed.

The rest of the sleeve went with it. Kiva saw it go. All of it, the whole grey length of it that had swung across the channel on its post, came up off the west mud after its end as a blanket comes up after the corner you lift it by. It came up off the near edge. It came up off the fold over Bridie. It hung in the air from the post in the bend to Pru's tray, a long grey curve, streaming.

Under it, on the mud, there was a man on his knees.

Kiva did not look.

"*Nobody,*" she said.

Short, with the swallow.

Pru let go.

For a quarter of a count, the grey lump hung over the mud a foot above Pru's empty tray, with the long curve of the sleeve going away from it to the post, and nobody had it. It did not fold. There was nobody for it to fold to. It hung in nothing, belonging to nobody, as a coin hangs at the top of a toss.

Kiva took it.

She put her right fist under it, with the bar lit along it, and it came down onto the bar and the Ember took the weight of it. It was heavy. It was heavier than anything she had ever carried in any door, heavier than the cord on the yard, heavier than she had known an Ember could hold. It dragged at her fist toward the post in the bend, where the sleeve was still hung by half its mouth. It pulled like a dog on a lead.

She went.

She went low, six paces east, across the thread of slack water, with the grey lump riding along her fist and the whole long sleeve coming after it through the air. She did not bound high. She could not. The weight would not let her; it held her down like a hand on her shoulder. She went across the channel a foot above the mud, with the struts running out of her calves, and halfway across she felt the lump begin to unfold.

It was opening on her fist. She felt it go round her knuckles, cold, a grey wrap, the way it had gone down onto Pru's tray. It was folding to the nearest thing. The nearest thing was her hand.

*Nobody.*

She had not said it yet. She was not down yet. She was a foot off the east mud with the grey wrapping round her fist and Trewin's boots in front of her, square, planted on nothing yet, waiting, and she said it.

"*Nobody.*"

From the air. Before she landed. She always did.

She let go.

Trewin opened on the end of the word.

He did not wait to see her land. Kiva saw that he did not wait. She saw his face, white, the face of a man stepping off a wall into the dark, and she felt his still door come up out of the east mud under the grey lump in the same breath as her own hand came empty. It came up slow and heavy and very sure, as a post goes into wet ground. The lump came down onto it. It did not hang. It did not have time to hang. It was planted, into the mud of the east side of Mallow Creek, six paces from anybody, with the long grey sleeve stretched taut from Trewin's boots across the channel to the far post in the bend.

It writhed once. Kiva saw it. It tried to fold, the way it had tried on Pru's tray and on her fist. It tried to fold toward Trewin.

He did not move. He stood on it with his still door open into the mud, and it could not fold onto the ground, and it lay still.

Kiva landed on the east mud and went to her knees.

"*Planted,*" said Trewin.

His voice was quite polite.

"*Cut,*" said Yeo.

The red-haired boy in the punt lit his edge and cut the far post, and the top of it came off and went down into the slack water where his purse was, and nobody hauled it. And the stow died.

It died all at once, as Seven's had. The far post went grey and soft and slumped. The long grey sleeve that stretched across the channel from the post to Trewin's boots went grey along its whole length, and thin, and then it was a film of ash lying across the mud and the thread of water like frost on a window. The lump at Trewin's feet went grey with it. It lay on the mud under his boots, and he stood on it, and it was ash.

Trewin shut. He stood for a moment longer, quite still. Then he looked down at his boots.

They were white to the ankle. Frost lay on them like salt.

Kiva was on her knees on the east mud. She looked at her right hand. The knuckles were white, and the backs of the first two fingers, where the grey had gone round them. They did not hurt yet. She knew they would.

She made herself look across the channel.

The west mud was bare now where the fold had been. The sleeve had come up off it and gone across, and there was nothing between the sky and the two people it had been lying on.

Penhale was on his knees. He was bent forward over the girl, with his back to the sky. His ward was still up. It was up over both of them, broad and low and grey, a roof, the way he had held it when he leaned in under the near edge to take her arm. But it was not lit any more. It was grey as the ash on the mud. It was the shape of a ward, standing on nothing, like frost on a window in the shape of a fern.

His back was white. His smock that had been black once was white from the collar to the waist, and his red beard was white, and his hair. He did not move.

Under him, under the grey shape of his ward, on the mud at the edge of the thread of water, Bridie Hebden lay on her side with her face turned up into an inch of air. Her eyes were open. Her plaits had come down. One of her arms was out from under him, the left, and his hand was round it at the wrist, holding it.

"*Down,*" said Yeo, on the spur. "*Everybody. Off the spur. Now.*"

They got Bridie out first.

Afterward Kiva could not have said who did what. She knew she went back across the thread of water on her knees and her hands, because her knees were wet to the thigh afterward and her right hand was screaming. She knew Pru was there, with her own hands held out in front of her as if they belonged to somebody else, the tips of the fingers white. She knew Anwen came down off the bank and Jude after her, and Ivey came the last ten paces through the west mud with her face set, and the red-haired boy jumped out of the punt into water to his waist and waded.

She knew that somebody had to take Penhale's hand off the girl's wrist, and that it was Ivey. Ivey knelt in the mud and took the white fingers one by one and opened them, very gently, as one opens the hand of a sleeping child to take something out of it. They opened. They did not close again.

Anwen and Jude drew Bridie out sideways from under him, along the mud, by her shoulders. She came out stiff and cold and wet. Her legs below the knee, where the sleeve's edge had lain across them, were white through her stockings. Her face was white. Her lips were moving.

"Fathom," she was saying. "One fathom. Two fathom." Her teeth were going so hard that the words came out in pieces. "He was telling me. Under it. He was counting. To keep me still." Her eyes went past Kiva to the man on his knees. "He stopped at three."

Kiva put her left hand on the girl's shoulder. It was the only hand she had that did not hurt. The two fingers lay on the wet brown and buff of the coat and told her nothing.

"You're out," she said. "You're on the mud. You're out."

"Is he—"

"Ivey's with him," said Kiva.

Ivey had turned him over.

She had turned him over onto his back on the mud, quite gently, with her own coat under his head, and she was kneeling beside him with both her hands flat on his chest. They were a catcher's hands, broad and square and dark with the work, and the white light that had been under them at the tear was not under them now. She was not reading anything. She was only holding them there.

His face was white. His eyes were half open. His mouth was a little open in the red-white beard, as if he had been going to say something cheerful and had stopped, as Bridie said, at three.

Ivey took her hands away.

"*Ma'am,*" she said, up the bank, to Yeo. She did not raise her voice either. It was a Company voice. "He's gone."

---

The flood came in at nine minutes to one.

Kiva saw it come. She was on her knees on the west mud beside Bridie, with Pru holding the girl's head out of the thread and Anwen chafing her white legs through the stockings with both hands, and she happened to look at the marsh grass on the west bank. It stirred. It stirred very slightly, all together, inland. And the thread of water in the channel's bottom, which had been lying in its bed like a ribbon since twelve minutes past twelve, began, very slowly, to run the other way.

Seven minutes, she thought. The stow had turned at a quarter to one, or a minute before. The water had turned at nine minutes to.

Seven minutes. And her fingers had said nothing, the whole time, and the grass had stood up straight. The water had not turned. Only the stow.

She did not think anything else about it. There was not room. She put it somewhere, the way she had put Mace's *down a hand* and Penhale's count somewhere, and left it there.

"*The flood,*" said Yeo, from the bank. "*Out of the channel. All of you. Now.*"

They carried them out.

They carried Bridie up the west bank first, on the punt's floorboards, which the boatman had pulled up and passed across. Anwen and Pru and the red-haired boy carried her, with Jude at the head holding the boards level with his good arm. She was talking all the way up the bank, in pieces, about fathoms. At the top of the west bank there was nothing but marsh grass, and they laid her down in it and Anwen took off her own coat and put it over her.

They carried Penhale across the channel to the east side.

The water was coming in now. It was coming up the thread from the sea, a hand deep, two, brown and quick, and it was at their shins by the time they had him on the boards. Ivey took the head. Trewin took the foot, in his white boots, with his face set. Jude went back down into the water to take a side, and the boatman the other, and Kiva took the fourth corner with her left hand, because her right would not close.

They went up the east bank with him. It was steep. It was mud. Halfway up, Jude's bad foot went from under him and the board tipped, and Kiva felt the weight come onto her left arm all at once, and her two fingers said *something's pushing,* and she held.

At the top of the bank, at the edge of the spur, Perrin was waiting.

He was not supposed to lift anything heavier than a cup. Aske had written it on a card. He knelt at the edge of the spur and put both his big hands under the head of the boards as they came up over the lip, and took the weight, and lifted. Kiva saw his face as he did it. It did not change. Only his breath went short and stayed short.

Nobody told him to stop.

They laid Davy Penhale on the stone of the spur, on the punt's floorboards, with his white face to the sky and the wind blowing his white beard sideways. Yeo took off her coat. She knelt and laid it over him, from his chest to his boots, and then she put out her hand and closed his eyes, the left and then the right, with her thumb. She did it as if she had done it before.

She stayed there on her knees with her hand on the coat for a long time.

---

Coombe came at ten past one.

She came along the spur from the causeway at a walk that was nearly a run, in her grey Board coat, with the rope in her hands. Kiva watched her come all the way. She came past the place where Seven's stow had been, a film of grey on the mud under brown water now, and past the place where the Company crew had been kneeling at the upper tear, and past the punt drawn up on the stone. She stopped where Yeo was kneeling.

She looked down at the coat on the boards. She looked at the red-white beard blowing out from under it.

She did not tie a knot. Her hands were quite still on the rope.

"Yeo," she said.

"Dinah." Yeo did not look up. "He went in by the book. To the near edge. He got his ward under it and lifted, and he had her arm." Her voice was perfectly flat. "And then the far end came round. It's a sleeve. It's got two ends. The book's for a sheet."

Coombe did not say anything.

"The girl was on it," said Yeo. "On the skin. Freeing a line. She asked me, from the bank, was it ground. It was lying down. I said what the book says." She took her hand off the coat. "I said it."

"I know," said Coombe.

"It was still on a post. The far post. It turned on it. Before the water." Yeo looked up at last, at the creek, where the flood was coming in over the mud. "Before the water turned, Dinah. I had my watch on it. Seven minutes before."

Coombe looked at the creek too.

Then she turned her head and looked across the spur, at Kiva. Kiva was sitting on the stone with her back against the flag pole and her right hand held in her left on her knee, because it had begun to hurt now, and Pru was beside her with her own hands held out in front of her, the fingertips white and beginning to blister. Coombe looked at Trewin, standing a little way off by himself with his frosted boots on the stone and his hat in his hands. She looked at the film of grey ash across the channel, stretched from the east mud where Trewin had planted to the stump of the far post in the bend, going under now in the brown flood.

She looked at it for a long time.

"Who called it?" she said.

"Rowan," said Yeo. "On my word. Finch caught. Rowan carried. The Tarn's captain planted." She was quiet a moment. "It held. It took the skin off the both of them in the time it takes to say it. And he was dead under it already."

Coombe came across the spur to Kiva.

She did not crouch. She stood over her, with the rope in her hands, and the pale eyes looked down at Kiva's white knuckles and at Pru's white fingertips.

"It held," said Coombe.

"Yes, ma'am."

"Write it." The Marshal's voice did not change at all. "In your seven columns, with every hand in it by name. Who caught. Who carried. Who planted. Who called it. Whose word." She was quiet. "And his. In the first column. Before."

"Yes, ma'am."

Coombe looked at her a moment longer. Then she went back across the spur to where Yeo was kneeling, and she knelt down too, on the stone, on the other side of the boards, in her grey Board coat, with the rope across her knees. The two of them knelt there with Davy Penhale between them and did not say anything to each other at all.

---

The Hunt went on.

Kiva had not thought that it would. She had thought, without thinking it, that when a man died the board would stop, the way the board stops in a lane when the glass is turned over and the marshal lifts his hand. It did not. From the spur she could see the other beats on the Longshore, the flags on their poles standing up out of the marsh at the heads of the creeks, a quarter of a mile apart all the way to the sea. On every one of them there were people, small and dark against the mud, working the flood. A long way off on Brine Reach somebody was hauling a line. Nearer, on the next creek south, a Company punt was going out across the brown water toward a post.

"Mallow Creek's closed," said Yeo.

She had got up. She stood on the spur with her notebook open in her hand, and her face was quite composed, and her voice was flat and brisk, the officer's voice that she had used at nine.

"The lower tear's under the flood," she said. "It'll wait for the bottom of the night's ebb. Ivey's crew will close it near one in the morning, if it's still there, with a guard on it till then." She looked at the creek. "The upper tear's closed. Seven's. They had it shut at twenty to one." She wrote. "Seven's two bags are up the east bank, shut; Talley and my boatman dragged them up on their lines when the flood came. The Tarn's first the same. The Tarn's second is in the creek under the far post, open, and nobody hauled it. That's the creek's." She wrote again. "The net's tin is in the punt."

She wrote for a long time. Kiva watched her. She wrote standing, fast, in the brisk upright hand, and once she stopped and looked at what she had written, and did not cross it out, and went on.

Then she read it aloud. Kiva did not know if she meant to. She read it the way Rhea read a slate, to the four who had stood on the beat, and the Tarn's four, and the Company crew, and the Marshal kneeling on the stone.

"*Closed, Mallow Creek, at one, on the flood. The upper stow, Seven's, cut in the slack, two posts, clean; the near post shed a rung on a surge before, held in part on a wall laid on the water. The lower stow, the Tarn's, cut on one post in the slack. The far post stood. The stow turned on it at a quarter to one, seven minutes before the water, with the Tarn's catcher on the skin by the officer's word, and the skin came over her.*"

She stopped. She went on.

"*Lives: one. D. Penhale, catcher, the Saltmere Company, reserve to the officer, went to the near edge by the book and was taken under the far end. The girl lived under his ward.*" Her voice did not change. "*Hurt: B. Hebden, catcher, the Tarn, both legs below the knee, a burn of cold. P. Finch, catcher, Team Seven, the fingers of both hands at the tips. K. Rowan, catcher, Team Seven, the right hand across the knuckles. Trewin, captain, the Tarn, both feet to the ankle.*"

She turned the page.

"*The skin was taken off them, on the officer's word, by a pass in three. A ward to an Ember to a still door. Called. Nobody holding it twice.*" She looked up. "*That's entered for whoever reads it. Remarks: none, to any four. The remark's the book's.*" She shut the notebook. "And mine. I said it was ground."

Nobody said anything. The flood came in over the mud of Mallow Creek, brown and quick, and covered the place where Bridie had lain, and the place where Penhale had knelt, and the long grey film of the dead stow that stretched across the channel from Trewin's planted end to the stump in the bend.

Jude was standing at the edge of the spur, looking at it.

He had his left arm against his chest and his good hand open at his side. He was looking at the water coming in. Kiva had seen him look at the sea from a boat's rail, and from a causeway at two in the morning, and from the end of the Long Quay last night with his hand on a bollard. He was not looking at it that way now.

"I'd want it said," said Jude, to nobody, very quietly, "that I've seen it come in now. Over somebody."

Nobody answered him. Yeo, behind him, put her notebook in her coat and stood for a moment with her hand still on it. She looked at the back of his head. She did not say anything either.

---

Rhea Sorn went to the Company yard at four, when Gannet Creek was closed and her slate was written and her own four bags were at the gate being combed red.

She had heard it on the causeway at half past one, from a Board clerk running with a paper, and she had not let herself go to Mallow Creek. It was not her beat. Her beat was Gannet Creek, and Old Four and a Westerley four on it, and a kiddle of five posts working the ebb. Her two fours had cut it in the slack on her word, and the Company's crew had closed it at the low, and she had written it on the slate, every line. She had not let her hand shake while she wrote it. *Lives: none.* She had written that at one o'clock, at the very minute, as it turned out, when they were laying him on the spur at Mallow.

The yard was behind the Long Quay, a cobbled square with green vans along one side and the Company's sheds along the other. There was a green van in the middle of it with its doors open and nobody near it. Inside, on a stretcher, under a Company coat, there was a long shape.

Yeo was sitting on the tailboard of the next van with her hands in her lap.

Rhea went and sat down beside her. She did not say anything for a while. Neither did Yeo.

Across the yard, by the shed doors, Pike was sitting on an upturned crate. He had come on Graywater's van to the Longshore at seven to sit on the hard at Gannet Creek and watch, because he could not stay away from a creek, and he had come here after. His hands lay on his knees, closed halfway. He was looking at the open van.

"He had Pike's place," said Rhea at last. "On the coast night. The Monday of last week. At the purses."

"I know. He told me." Yeo did not turn her head. "He said your old man nearly smiled at him once. He was very proud of it."

"Pike nearly smiles at everybody."

"Davy didn't know that." Yeo looked at her hands. "He'd been reserve on four Hunts. He'd never gone onto the mud. He said it was dull, and he was paid for it, and his mother liked it."

Rhea had nothing to say to that, so she did not say anything.

"I told the girl it was ground," said Yeo. "From the spur. She asked. Freeing a line. It was lying on the mud like a hem, and it was on a post, and I didn't look at the post. I looked at the skin. I said what the book says." Her voice did not change. "Coombe's page is at the printer. *A hem that lies down under a thing on its feet is not ground.* I heard her say it at Hollins three seasons gone, over a lock-keeper. I thought it was a good line. I thought, when it's printed, I'll teach it."

"So did I," said Rhea. "When it's printed."

"Who had his far edge?"

Rhea turned her head.

"Davy's," said Yeo. "When he went to the near edge and lifted. Who was watching the far end of the sleeve for him?"

"Nobody," said Rhea.

"Nobody. The corner-hand had it, by the book. The corner-hand was the Tarn's boy, hauling a line with his eyes on the line, as the book tells him to." Yeo was quiet for a moment. "On the Company's crews we give the far edge to the corner-hand. We've done it since before my mother. We don't keep seconds. A second's an inland thing. A Graywater thing."

"It is," said Rhea. "It was. Since the first month."

"Before that?"

"Before that I'd a catcher who watched my heel, and nobody at the far edge at all, for six years. And I'd have told you we didn't need one." Rhea looked across the yard at Pike on his crate. "Then I had a second. A girl of seventeen with two dead fingers. She called the far edge in my van this winter, and on Gannet Creek in the dark, and the line came off it. And on the Tuesday of last week, at dawn, I went short, by my own card, and for one count at dawn there was nobody at the far edge but a man with half a fist doing two things at once." She was quiet. "Half a rung went into Gannet Creek. That's all it cost me."

Yeo did not say anything.

"Her second called it today," said Rhea. "Up your creek. From three paces. I heard that on the causeway too."

"Finch." Yeo nodded. "She called it at twenty past twelve, on Seven's stow, and Rowan came off her line, and the fold lay down where she'd been. I wrote it in my book. I said I'd not seen anybody watching for it." She looked at the open van. "Forty minutes later I'd a man under a fold sixty paces down the same creek, and nobody watching for it, because the book doesn't watch for it. I wrote that too."

They sat a long time.

Pike got up off his crate. He did it slowly, and stood for a moment, and then he walked across the yard to the open van. He did not get into it. He stood at its tailboard with his half-closed hands at his sides and looked in at the long shape under the coat. He stood there for as long as it would take to count three fathoms of line, hand over hand. Then he came back across the yard.

He stopped in front of the two officers on the tailboard.

"He counted it in fathoms," said Pike. "On a drawstring. I'd never heard it before the creek. *One fathom. Two fathom.*" His voice was thick. "I'd my hands half shut on a sack, and I couldn't call it for her, and he called it. He did my place, that night. He did it well." He looked at his hands. "I'd not have had him do it if I could have done it myself."

"I know," said Rhea.

"He had nobody at his far edge," said Pike. "That's all. That's what I came over to say." He looked at Yeo, and his face did the thing it did, which was very nearly a smile, and was not one. "I'm sorry for your man, officer."

"Thank you," said Yeo.

Pike went away across the yard to Graywater's van and sat down on its tailboard by himself.

Rhea took her slate off her belt. She did not know why. It was written; Gannet Creek was closed and entered and there was nothing to add to it. She held it on her knee, slate blue, with her six columns ruled down it. *Before. Closed. Lives. Works. Tinned. Remarks.*

"The girl's columns," said Yeo. "Rowan's. Seven columns, they say. On the far edge, and the second, and what Pike does that he never says."

"Ten sheets," said Rhea. "Pike's craft. She wrote them this winter, in the van, after the coal wharf." She did not look up from the slate. "And a sheet on the pass. The three. She wrote that for Coombe, in the second month."

"I'd want a copy," said Yeo. "For my crew. Ivey first. Before the Lane."

"It's not mine to give. It's the girl's."

"Then I'll ask the girl."

Rhea looked at the slate on her knee for a long time. She had ruled it herself, every line of it, every night for six years, and she had written *Lives: none* on it more times than she could count, and twice she had not. She thought of a card in a kitchen in the autumn, slate blue, read aloud, signed on the back by four children. *Then we look again, and either of us may say no.* She thought of the thirtieth, a fortnight off.

She had spent the spring's advice already. She had spent it at Gannet Creek at one in the morning on a mud hard. She did not have another.

"Ask her tonight," she said. "Before she sleeps. She'll want to have said it to somebody before she sleeps." She hung the slate back on her belt. "That's not advice. That's a thing I know about her."

"Is there a difference?"

"Not much," said Rhea Sorn, and sat on the tailboard beside the other officer until the light began to go.

---

## CHAPTER — manuscript/chapter-32.md

# Chapter 32 — Kiva: The Measure Goes On

Aske greased the knuckles of her right hand in the healers' tent at two, with the flap tied back and the Longshore going under the flood outside.

He did it with two fingers and a pot of yellow grease that smelled of sheep, slowly, as he did everything, and then he wound linen round the hand from the wrist to the first joints and tied it off. He did not look up while he did it. He had done Pru's first, all ten fingertips, each in its own small cap of linen, so that she sat on the next stretcher with her hands held up in front of her like a surgeon waiting to be gloved.

"A burn of cold," he said. "Across the knuckles and the backs of the first two fingers. It'll blister tonight and peel by Sunday." He tied the knot. "It's your right hand. It's not the left. I'd like that understood. It's the one that works, and it'll go on working, and in a week you'll not know it happened."

"And the Ember?"

"The Ember's not in your hand. It's under your breastbone, where it's always been, and it's done nothing wrong today." He looked at her at last. "It carried more than I'd have said it could. I watched you come across that channel from the causeway with a glass, the way you'd watch a horse go at a wall you'd told it not to jump. It held. It's tired. So are you."

"Thursday," said Kiva.

"Thursday's the soonest. I said so this morning, and nothing that happened on that mud has moved it." He put the grease pot in his bag. "The left hand's warm. The right's burned. Neither of them's the hand I'm counting days for." He paused. "I'll say the price on Thursday, if anybody asks for one. I'll say it twice."

On the stretcher at the far end of the tent, Bridie Hebden lay under two blankets with her legs bare to the knee on a folded sheet. They were white to the knee, not grey, and the Company healer was laying wet linen on them, cool, very gently, and changing it. Bridie was not talking about fathoms any more. She was looking at the canvas roof of the tent with her plaits down over the stretcher's edge, and she was not crying. Trewin sat on an upturned crate beside her with his boots off and his feet in a basin of water, white to the ankle, and his hat on his knee.

He had been sitting there for half an hour without saying anything. Now he looked up, across the tent, at Kiva.

"You've plain," he said.

The tent went quiet.

"Two drams," said Trewin. He said it slowly and politely, as he said everything. "Sealed. Ward. You declared it at the gate on the first day, in front of the whole queue. Everybody saw it go silent on the seventh. It was the only one." He looked at Bridie's legs. "Plain ward-trace is for the cold. That's what my grandmother said. A healer can ease the cold with it, if it's plain."

Kiva did not answer at once. She looked at Aske.

Aske had not moved. He sat on the end of her stretcher with his bag on his knees and looked at Trewin, and then at the Company healer, and then at Bridie's white legs on the folded sheet.

"It's for a hand," said Kiva. "That's what's written on it. In two books, under two names. *For a hand. Not for a bank, a floor, a crossing or a sale.*" She made herself go on. "It's Seven's word. It's my captain's name and mine. If it were wanted, I'd have to go to my four and to the Order, and say it was wanted, and ask."

"I know," said Trewin. "I'm not asking you to break your word. I'm asking whether it's wanted."

"It's not," said Aske.

They both looked at him.

"Plain ward-trace eases a starved hand," said Aske. "A hand that's had the ash drawn out of it through a door, so that the door's hungry and the flesh round it's cold from inside. I've done it. Her aunt's hand was starved once, in the autumn, and plain eased it inside the hour." He nodded at Bridie. "That's not starved. That's burned. It's a burn of cold from outside, off a skin, the same as frost off a gate. It wants grease and wet linen and a fortnight. Plain ash on that would do nothing that grease won't do better, and you'd have spent the last plain ward-trace on the road south for a pot of grease." He looked at Trewin. "I'm sorry. I'd say so if it were otherwise. I'd say so to her four and to the girl's own face."

Trewin looked at him for a long time. Then he nodded, slowly, as though nodding were a thing that had to be done properly.

"Thank you," he said. "I'd sooner know."

"You'd sooner know," said Aske. "So would I. So would she." He got up. "Miss Hebden. The linen's to be changed every quarter of an hour till dark, and you're not to stand on them till Friday, and you're to eat something. I'll come at six."

Bridie looked at the tent roof.

"He stopped at three," she said, to nobody.

---

Jude said it on the cart going back along the causeway, with the book open on his knee and the Longshore going under brown water on both sides.

"If it had been her hands," he said. "Starved. If Aske had said yes."

Nobody answered him for a while. Pru sat with her capped fingers held up. Anwen sat with her hands in her sleeves. Perrin sat on the cart's floor with his back against the side and his hand on his ribs and his face grey, and he did not open his eyes.

"Then we'd have had to talk about it," said Kiva.

"We'd have had to talk about it." Jude looked at the page. "It's bound in our word. It's bound for your hand. I'd want it said that I thought about it, all the way back from Mallow, what I'd have said if Aske had said yes." He shut the book on his finger. "I don't know what I'd have said. I'd want that said too."

"I know what I'd have said," said Perrin, without opening his eyes.

"What?"

"I'd have said I'm captain, and it's my name under hers in two books, and I'd go to the Order myself and tell them why, and I'd break it." He breathed carefully. "And then I'd have minded it for the rest of my life. I'd like it known I'm glad I don't have to."

---

The Board combed the Hunt at the gate on the timber pond from four until after six.

Every four came in off the Longshore with its bags, and every bag went on the long table under the mast, and a clerk in grey held a comb over it, and another clerk wrote down what the comb said. The queue went back along the boards and round the end of the Graving's wall. Nobody talked much in it. The word had gone along the causeway and along the Mole and into the town long before any of them came in, and the queue had heard it, and it stood with its bags at its feet and waited.

Seven came to the table at a quarter to six.

There were three things to comb. The two bags off Mallow Creek, tied at their necks with the purses' own drawstrings, grey canvas, wet, with the mud of the east bank still on them. And the tin from the punt, where Ivey had netted the shed rung off Anwen's wall in the morning.

The clerk held the comb over the tin first.

Kiva heard the third reed sing, high and thin, the way it sang for ward-trace. Then she heard the seventh. It was not a hair. It was a voice. It sang over the third and went on singing after the clerk had taken the comb away.

"Red," said the clerk. "I above trace. Nine drams and a tenth." He did not look up. "Wet on the ebb?"

"Wet on the ebb," said Anwen.

"They all are. I've not had a wet one under all day." He tied a red tag on the tin's handle and wrote. "Next."

The first bag. The rung off the near post, the short one, cut at fourteen minutes past twelve and shut in the slack. Kiva had hauled it. *One fathom. Two fathom. Three.*

The clerk held the comb over it. The third reed. Then the seventh, a voice, over the third.

"Red. Thirty-eight drams." He tagged it. "Next."

The second bag. The far post's, the tall one, cut at twenty-four minutes past and hauled by Pru. The clerk held the comb over it.

The third reed sang.

The seventh did not sing. It hummed. It was a hair, a thread of sound, so faint that Kiva would not have heard it if she had not been listening for it with her whole body. It was the sound that she had heard from every can in Meridian since the thaw, and every tin on the road south, and every bag from Cowl Edge. It was the hair.

"Trace," said the clerk. He looked up for the first time. "Under trace on the seventh. Clean." He looked at the bag, and at the comb, and at them. "Twelve drams and six-tenths. Clean."

"Clean," said Jude.

"Clean." The clerk wrote it. "Don't ask me why one's clean and the one off the next post isn't. Off the same stow, in the same slack, in the same purse, by the same catcher. I've had it all day. One in four that's dry, perhaps, combs clean. Nobody's had a wet one under." He tied a white tag on the second bag. "Twelve and six, clean, Team Seven. The Board's half, the Company's quarter, your quarter. Three drams and fifteen hundredths, to your book." He looked down the queue. "Next."

They stood back from the table. Jude had the book open on his arm and was writing.

"*Wednesday the 16th,*" he said aloud. "*The Hunt, Mallow Creek. Pryce, a wall on water, seventeen. Talley, two cuts, ten. Finch, a fold, twelve. Rowan, an Ember, her own thimble. Spent, thirty-nine.*" He wrote. "*Combed: fifty-nine drams and seven-tenths; forty-seven and a tenth red, and tagged. Twelve and six clean. The four's quarter, three and fifteen.*" He did the sum in the margin, twice, as he always did. "*Held, six and twenty-six.*"

"Six and twenty-six," said Anwen.

"The most we've had since the hills." Jude did not look up. "I'd want it said that I'd give it all back."

Nobody said anything to that.

At the next table along the boards, a man in Halden's black and gold was sitting with a ledger.

Kiva had seen him from the queue. She had not thought anything of it. Halden's technical office had a clerk at every board; Mr. Adlam had said so. He sat with his ledger open and wrote, and every time a bag on the comb table got a red tag, he leaned over and read the tag and wrote a line.

Kellan was standing beside him.

Kellan was not in the queue. Halden had been combed at five. He stood by the clerk's table in his black and gold with his hands in his pockets, reading the ledger upside down over the clerk's shoulder, and his face had the look it had when a sum would not come out.

He saw Kiva. He came over.

"Skerry Pill," he said.

"I heard. On the causeway. Lyle."

"A door." Kellan looked at the boards. "Her ward. Gone, not shut. She held a boom against a shed rung on Carrow's word, and the lodger came out of the pill's mouth like an eel, and Treen called run, and she didn't. I cut it on the mud. I was too late." He said it flatly, the way he did sums. "Bray wrote a remark against me. A dram off Halden on the mark. And our second line was half set when I left it, and at the slack a part of our second rung went past the purse. Bray put that one down to the pill, not to me. I'd have put it to me." He shrugged. "We were combed at five. Eight and two, after shares and the remark. Pettit says he'd have gone himself."

"I'm sorry," said Kiva. "About Lyle."

"So am I." Kellan looked at her bandaged hand, and at Pru's capped fingers, and away. "I heard about Mallow. Everybody on the causeway heard. A Company catcher, and a girl of the Tarn under him." He was quiet. "And a pass in three. Nobody holding it twice."

"It held."

"I know it held. Coombe said so, on the causeway, to the Clerk, at two. In front of everybody." He took his hands out of his pockets. "I wanted to tell you something else. That's not why I came over."

He nodded back at Halden's clerk with his ledger.

"He's writing every red tag," said Kellan. "Every bag in the Hunt that combs over trace on the seventh. He writes the four, the beat, the weight, and the tag number, and then a line under each. I read the line." He did not lower his voice. "*Claimed, under the patent of 2391. All ash exhibiting the Step, however produced.*"

Kiva looked at the clerk. He was a young man, neat, with ink on his second finger, writing carefully.

"It's not a crime," said Kellan. "It's the patent. The house has claimed it since 2391, and the Board's never said it can't. He's doing his work. He'd be doing it badly if he didn't." He did the sum aloud, as he always did. "But I've been standing here an hour. I've watched every bag. One in four that's dry combs clean, the comb clerk says. Nothing that's been wet combs clean. So call it nine-tenths of what twenty-four fours killed on the Longshore today combs red." He looked at her. "The house has a claim on nine-tenths of the Hunt. On paper. Tonight."

"What does it do with it?"

"Nothing, tonight. It's red; it's evidence; it's tagged. Nobody can use it." Kellan put his hands back in his pockets. "I don't know what it does with it next year. I don't know what it does with it if next year it's ten-tenths." He was quiet. "I'm not saying anything's wrong. I'm saying I did the sum, and it came out, and I didn't like the number. I'd expect nothing else of a sum. I thought somebody should know it besides me."

He went back to the clerk's table and stood there, reading upside down.

---

Noa was at the mast.

The mast stood up black above the timber pond as it had stood every day for a fortnight, forty feet, with its drum and its eight pens. Nobody had stood on its crosses since Monday morning. Noa's desk was at its foot, and Polglase's beside it, and the two readers were standing at them in the last of the light with their tapes unrolled.

Kiva went over. She had not meant to. She found that she had.

"Rowan," said Noa. He had his pencil behind his ear. He looked at her bandaged hand and then, carefully, not at it. "I heard."

"Everybody's heard."

"Yes." He looked down at his tape. "I'd like to show you something. I don't know what it is. I want to show it to somebody who was on the Longshore."

He put his finger on the tape.

It was a day's tape off the mast, a long white strip, with eight lines running along it from the eight pens, flat, because nobody had been on the crosses. The lines ran flat all along it, hour after hour. And at one place, near the middle, there was a mark across them. It was no taller than a hair is wide. It went across all eight lines, top to bottom.

"Twelve forty-four," said Noa. "By the mast clock. All eight pens. A hair." He took his finger away. "Nobody was on the mast. Nobody's been on it since Monday. There's no cliff for it to come after. It's just there."

"There's a hair on this mast most days at the springs," said Polglase, beside him, without looking up from his own tape, which had the same mark at the same place. "I've said so. It's the harbour."

"It's the neap," said Noa.

Polglase did not answer that. He took his spectacles off and polished them.

Kiva looked at the hair on the tape. Twelve forty-four.

"The stow turned at a quarter to one," she said. "On Mallow Creek. The lower one. The Tarn's. Perrin had the glass and the watch, and he wrote it down. A quarter to one, or a minute before." She heard her own voice, quite level. "It turned before the water. The water turned at nine minutes to one. Seven minutes after. My fingers had said nothing the whole time, and the grass stood up straight."

Noa looked at her. Then he took his pencil from behind his ear and wrote in the ledger on his desk's ledge, in his narrow upright hand. Kiva watched him write.

*The 16th. A hair on all pens at 12.44 by the mast clock. Nobody on the mast. At Mallow Creek, by T7's captain's watch, a stow turned at 12.44 or 12.45, seven minutes before the water (Rowan, K., fingers on a pole; the grass). N.B.*

He did not draw a line between them. He did not write *because.* He left a space under it.

"I don't know what that is," he said. "I'm not going to say I do."

"I know you're not."

"Mr. Polglase says it's the harbour."

"Mr. Polglase has been reading this mast for forty years," said Polglase, putting his spectacles back on. "He's begun to look." He rolled his tape. "He'd like that noted as well."
