It was a quarter to seven. Kiva took her left hand out of her pocket, the warm one, the unbandaged one, and laid the first two fingers on the iron leg of Noa's reading desk where it stood on the boards over the black water of the timber pond. Lightly. Without leaning.

*It keeps time.*

She wrote it in the back of the book with the pencil in her left hand, badly, because her right was in linen. *Wednesday the 16th. A quarter to seven. The leg of the reader's desk, the gate. Yes.*

---

Anwen Pryce climbed Tar Lane at seven with tar on her boots and her hands in her sleeves, and did not know what she was going to say.

The Ropewalk was lit. Its windows went away along the ridge in a long line of yellow squares, as they had the evening she had first come up, and the doors at the near end stood open. Inside, the boards were bare. There were no children tonight. There was one lamp hung from the beam over the near end, and under it, on the bench against the shed's wall, Maud Lyle was sitting by herself with a towel in her hands.

On the boards in front of her, Juniper Carrow was walking.

Anwen stopped at the door.

Carrow was walking up and down the near end of the shed, slowly, ten paces and back, in her shirtsleeves, with her edge lit on her right fist. Only her edge. Anwen could feel it. There was no post going into the ground under her, no stance; her feet came up off the boards at every step like anybody's feet. She walked ten paces, and turned, and walked back, and at every step her edge wavered on her fist the way a candle wavers when somebody opens a door.

"It's loud," said Lyle, from the bench, without looking round. "That's what she's finding. I told her." She had seen Anwen in the doorway. "Come in, Pryce. She'll not stop for you."

Anwen came in. She sat down on the bench, a little way from Lyle, and did not say anything.

They watched Carrow walk.

"She shut her stance at five," said Lyle. "In front of the whole school, at the gate, after they combed us. Treen asked her what she was doing and she said, *I'm learning to declare.*" Lyle's square careful face did not change. "She'll declare edge at the Lane. Only edge. On a twelve-pace floor, against people who've watched her lay since the first month. Treen says she'll be nothing. Treen's probably right."

"Why?"

"She called boom." Lyle turned the towel over in her hands. "She says she's called boom a hundred times and never once thought about what she was calling. She says she called *laid,* without saying it, because she'd never once called anything else. She says she'll not stand on a floor again with two doors open together until she knows what it's calling." She was quiet for a moment. "She says it's not because it was wrong. She says she doesn't know whether it was wrong. She says that's worse."

Carrow turned at the end of her ten paces. Her edge wavered and steadied. She did not look at the bench.

Anwen looked at Lyle's hands on the towel.

"Can I see?" she said. "I'm not asking to be kind. I'd want to know. If it were me."

Lyle looked at her. Then she put the towel down on the bench and stood up.

She opened her stance. Anwen felt it go into the boards, a post driven into the ridge under the Ropewalk, solid, sure, the same as it had been in the lane and at the lock. Then Lyle lifted her right hand to her hip, where a ward comes up.

Nothing came up.

She held her hand there. Anwen watched it. It was a Hold's hand, broad, steady, held at the hip with the palm up, the way Anwen held her own a hundred times a day. There was nothing on it. There was nothing under it. There was no tray, no wall, no silver, not even the hesitation of a door that was shut and might open. There was a hand, at a hip, in the lamplight.

"There," said Lyle. "That's all."

She shut her stance. She sat down again and picked up the towel.

"It's quiet," she said. "I always said laid was the quietest thing you'd ever feel. Two hands on a rope. You stop having to think about either." She folded the towel. "It isn't. This is. There's one hand on the rope now, and I can't stop thinking about the other one."

Anwen sat with her own hands in her sleeves.

She had thought, all the way up Tar Lane, that she would not say *I told you.* She had decided it on the causeway, and again at the bottom of the lane, and again at the door. It had been easy to decide. It was harder to sit on a bench beside the girl she had told, twice, and not have it in her mouth.

It was not in her mouth. She found that it was not there at all.

"I want it said that I'm sorry," said Anwen. "That's all I came to say. I'm sorry."

"I know you are." Lyle did not look at her. "You told me. Twice. I heard you both times. I'd have done the same tomorrow." She was quiet. "Forty drams going down the pill onto the flats, wet, red. They'd have gone anyway. Nobody's going to net them. I held a boom for forty drams of red ash that nobody can use, and I'd do it again, and I don't know why."

"Because it was coming down the pill," said Anwen. "And you were the boom."

Lyle looked at her then.

"Carrow said I was the best wall on the coast," she said. "I said I wasn't. I said I was the best laid wall on the coast, and if you took my feet out of the floor I'd be a wall like anybody's." She almost smiled. It did not get as far as her mouth. "They took the wall. I've got my feet. I'm a floor now, like anybody's. Less, maybe."

Anwen looked at the boards. At Carrow, walking. At her own heels.

"You leaned well," said Lyle. "In the first lane, against us. You leaned from the floor; your heels were right. I said so." She put the towel round her neck. "Will you show me? How you lean from the floor. When you've nothing on your hands. I've a floor left. I'd like to be good with it."

"Yes," said Anwen. "Tomorrow. Before the Lane."

"Tomorrow." Lyle nodded. "Thank you, Pryce."

At the end of her ten paces Carrow stopped, and turned, and shut her edge. She stood on the bare boards in the lamplight with her hands at her sides and nothing open at all, and she looked at the bench for the first time.

"Gapper," she said to Anwen. Not unkindly. As a fact about the weather. "How long did it take you? To stop hearing it. One door."

"I've never stopped," said Anwen. "That's how you can hear it. That's how you know it's there."

Carrow considered that for a long moment, with her quick blunt face giving nothing away. Then she lit her edge again, alone, on her right fist, and began to walk.

---

The board went up on the tent pole at the timber pond at nine, under the lamp, with the Board's seal on it.

The whole of the Measure was on the boards to read it. Kiva had never seen so many people on the timber pond, not on the first day at the gate, not at the seeding board on Saturday. They stood in the dark in their colours round the pole and along the edge of the black water, and the lamp hung over the sheets, and nobody pushed. It was the quietest crowd she had ever stood in.

There were two sheets. The first was the Hunt.

*The Hunt. Twenty-four fours on twelve beats. Marked on each four's quarter of its clean yield, after the Board's half and the officer's company's quarter, less a dram on the mark for every remark.*

*1. Team Twenty-Eight, Meridian. Eleven drams and eight-tenths. No remarks.*

*2. Old Four, Meridian. Ten and four-tenths.*

*3. Westerley, second four. Nine and ninety-five hundredths.*

She read down. *6. Halden License, Meridian. Eight and two-tenths; one remark.* *12. The Ropewalk School, first four. Four and a tenth; two remarks.* *15. Team Seven, Meridian. Three and fifteen hundredths. No remarks.* And at the bottom, by itself, *24. The Tarn, first four. Nought.*

Under the Hunt, in the clerk's hand, there was a line that had never been on any board.

*Mallow Creek closed at one, on the flood. D. Penhale, catcher, the Saltmere Company, reserve to the officer, lost. The Board records it.*

That was all. Kiva read it three times. *The Board records it.* She thought of a man on a spur in his thigh boots with mud on his fingers, touching his forehead to her. *When it's printed, I'll read it. I'm not one for reading.*

The second sheet was the standings.

*After three boards. Marked by place, twenty-four to the first and one to the last, and added.*

*1. Halden License, Meridian. Sixty-five.*

*2. Old Four, Meridian. Sixty-four.*

*3. Holloway, first four. Fifty-nine.*

*4. The Ropewalk School, first four. Fifty-eight.*

*5. Team Twenty-Eight, Meridian. Fifty-seven.*

*6. Westerley, first four. Fifty-six.*

*7. Westerley, second four. Fifty-five.*

*8. Holloway, second four. Fifty-four.*

*9. Team Seven, Meridian. Fifty-three.*

*10. The Ropewalk School, second four. Forty-six.*

*The national license roll takes the first eight after four boards. The Lane, in brackets, from Thursday.*

"Ninth," said Perrin.

He was standing beside her. Aske had let him come on condition that he did not stand for long, and he had already stood for longer than that. He had his coat buttoned over the linen and his hand pressed to his side.

"Ninth," said Kiva.

"One mark off eighth. One mark off the roll." He looked at the sheet. "Third last night. Ninth tonight. Fifteenth on the Hunt, with no remarks, with clean work on our stow and a fold called and a pass that held." He was quiet for a moment. "With a dry bag that combed red and a dry bag that combed clean, off the same stow in the same slack, and nobody can say why."

"It's honest," said Jude.

He had come up on her other side with the book under his good arm. He was not looking at the standings. He was looking at the line under the Hunt.

"It's honest," said Jude again. "I'd want it said that I know it's honest. That nobody gave us anything and nobody took anything." He did not look away from the line. "I'd want it said that I don't care about the mark. Tonight. I'll care tomorrow. Tonight I don't."

Perrin did not answer him. He stood with his hand on his ribs and read the standings again, slowly, all ten lines.

"The Lane," he said. "Thursday. We need two places in a bracket. Against fours that have watched us lay a ladder and drain a jacket and stand on a far edge." He breathed, carefully. "I'll be at the hurdle with my hands in my pockets."

"Declaring," said Kiva.

"Declaring." He looked at her bandaged hand. "And you've your seventh from tomorrow. If Aske says. If it's wanted."

"If it's wanted."

He looked at her a moment longer, and then nodded, and went away slowly toward the loft with Jude beside him, to lie on his bed till Aske came.

---

"Your three people held."

Kiva turned round. Selka Dray was standing behind her in the dark, on two feet, without a stick, in Twenty-Eight's colours, with her face like a shut door.

"I watched it," said Dray. "Through Marten's glass, from Brine Reach. Half a mile. I saw your officer stand on the spur, and the girl come across the channel, and the other girl's ward come up on the mud, small, and the thing come down onto it." Her face did not change. "I saw you go across low with something on your fist that was dragging you down, and the Tarn's captain stand on the far bank with his boots square. I saw it go into the mud." She was quiet. "I didn't see what was under it. Marten did. He took the glass away from me."

Kiva did not say anything.

"That's three people," said Dray. "One door each. Declared. On an officer's word. I've no rule against three people. I've no rule against an Ember carrying a thing six paces." She looked at the board, at the top line of the Hunt, where her own four stood first. "We won it. Eleven and eight-tenths. Clean bags off a creek where nothing went wrong, on a day when a man died a mile off. I'll take it. I'd not want anybody to think I was pleased."

"Nobody thinks that."

"Somebody will." Dray turned her shut face back to Kiva. "I've not changed my mind about you. I want you to know that. I'll see you in a bracket, on a declared count, with a needle on you." She paused. "That was three people. I'll say so to anybody who asks. That's all."

She went away into the crowd, on two feet, with a limp so slight that nobody who had not seen her in a chair would have known it was a limp.

---

The Mercury's late sheet was being sold on the Long Quay by a boy with a bag, under the white lamps, at a penny.

Kiva did not buy one. She read it over a man's shoulder, under the lamp outside the Counting House, where a crowd had stopped to read it together. The headline went right across the top of the page in letters as big as her thumb.

*THE WORST HUNT IN MEMORY.*

And under it, smaller: *A Company Catcher Lost on Mallow Creek. Nine Bags in Ten Comb Red. A Ropewalk Hold Loses a Door on Skerry Pill.* And under that, smaller still, in a column with a black line round it: *"A Bad Rift Season," Says the Board. "We Have a Gifted Generation in a Bad Season. The Measure Goes On."*

"*A gifted generation,*" said the man she was reading over. He said it to the woman beside him, low. "Tell that to his mother."

The woman did not answer. She took his arm, and they went away along the quay toward the low town, where the lamps were yellow.

Kiva stood under the white lamp and looked up at the Counting House. Every window was lit. Somewhere behind them there was a long room and a glass case and a man with his left hand lying flat on it, who had written to the Chair on Saturday in his own hand. He would have the Mercury by now. He would have read the line about the red bags. He would have read the line in the black box.

She did not know what he thought. She found that she wanted to, and that she was not going to go up the steps and ask.

She went on along the quay.

---

Orla Dane sat on a hard chair in the corner of the Board's printer's shop on Quay Street at a quarter past ten, with her stick across her knees, and watched Dinah Coombe take a page off a hook.

The printer had come down in his shirt, with a candle, grumbling. He had opened his door to the Board's Marshal because she had knocked on it as if it were on fire. Now he stood behind his counter in the smell of ink and oil and paper with the candle in one hand and his other hand flat on the counter, and he said, for the third time, "The Board said after the Measure, ma'am. In writing. The Clerk's own hand."

"I know what the Board said," said Coombe. "I'm the Board's Marshal. I'm saying now."

"The Clerk—"

"I've seen the Clerk. At eight, at her tent." Coombe laid the page on the counter. "She said the Board's printer takes the Marshal's word for the Marshal's page. She said it took her thirty years in a great many rooms to say a thing like that, and she said it." She smoothed the page with the flat of her hand. "I want it set tonight. I want it on every officer's beat on the Longshore by six in the morning, and on every van in the Company's yard, and in every catcher's pocket in this town who can read."

Orla had walked with her from the causeway. She had not been asked. She had come out of the healers' tent at half past seven and found the Marshal standing on the causeway with her rope in her hands, looking at Mallow Creek under the flood, and she had stood beside her, and when Coombe turned to walk back to the town she had walked with her, a step at a time, on her stick. Neither of them had said anything for a mile.

The printer looked at the page. He held the candle to it.

"*On a Thing That Folds from the Far Edge,*" he read. "*The Marshal's page.*" He looked up. "It's only one page, ma'am."

"It's one page. It's been one page since the second month." Coombe took a pencil out of her coat. "I'm changing one line."

She bent over the counter and wrote. Orla could not see what. She saw Coombe's hand move across the head of the page, slowly, in the candlelight, and stop, and write again.

"Read it back," said Coombe.

The printer read it. "*After D. Penhale, catcher, the Saltmere Company, at Mallow Creek, the 16th of the third month, 2681, who went in by the book.*" He stopped. He put the candle down. "Davy Penhale?"

"Davy Penhale."

"I knew his father." The printer looked at the page for a long time. "I printed his father's notice. The Company's. Ten years gone." He took the page off the counter. "I'll set it tonight, ma'am. I'll set it myself. You'll have it by five."

He went into the back with the candle and the page, and they heard him begin to move type in the dark.

Coombe stood at the counter with her hands on it. She did not sit down. Orla watched her from the hard chair. She had known Dinah Coombe by name for twenty years and by sight for a fortnight, and she had never once seen her hands still on her rope. They were still now, flat on the counter.

"You held it back," said Orla. "The page. In case it was wrong."

"I did."

"It wasn't wrong."

"I didn't know that." Coombe did not turn round. "I'd one man on the Longshore who called a hem ground and laid on it. He's a lock-keeper. I wrote a page. It said make the thing carry its sheet uphill, and if that fails, weight on the far edge, and the far edge to the second, as a place. I'd seen it work twice. Once on a wharf, from a slate, and once on a frozen yard in Meridian with a bedsheet." She was quiet. "Twice is not a page. I thought. I gave it to the printer and said hold it till the Measure's done, and if it's wrong, nobody's read it."

Orla looked at her stick.

"I held a card a season," she said. "From a boy I put on a Bench twenty years ago, and never wrote to after. I didn't show it to anybody, in case I was wrong about him, and in case I was right." She did not look up. "The girl found him in a week. By walking down a quay."

Coombe turned her head.

"It's not the same," said Orla. "I'm not saying it's the same. I'm saying that holding a thing back in case it's wrong is a thing I know about. That's all." She put her hand on the stick. "Your page wasn't wrong. You didn't know. Now you've put his name at the top, and it's going out at five, and somebody will read it on a creek tomorrow who'd not have read it otherwise."

"He'd have read it," said Coombe. "He said so on the causeway this morning, in the queue, to anybody who'd listen. *When it's printed, I'll read it. I'm not one for reading.*" Her hands were still on the counter. "That's what I can't get past. He was going to read it."

Orla did not answer that. There was no answer to it. She sat on the hard chair in the printer's shop with her bad knee out straight and listened to type being set in the dark, a letter at a time, small hard clicks, like somebody counting.

After a while Coombe took her hands off the counter and picked up her rope. She began, slowly, to tie a knot in it.

---

Mara Rowan stood on the bank gallery of the harbour pump house at half past ten, with Peverell beside her and Hart behind, and took her reading hand out of her pocket.

The thirty-six cells stood along the two galleries, eighteen a side, in their tall glass cases. Their lights turned. They turned together, every one of them, at the same pace, four and four, fifty-seven to the minute, and the six iron pumps below went up and down on the same stroke, and the whole hall hummed with it like a held note. Mara had stood in this hall every day for a fortnight. She had not once put her hand on a case.

She held it out now. She held it an inch from the glass of the first cell on the north gallery, where the light inside turned slow and yellow-white, and she did not lay it on.

Peverell watched her. She had her spectacles on their cord and her apron on, and her small crabbed hands folded on the rail. Hart stood a pace behind with his watch on the inside of his wrist and his bad hand in his jacket.

"A Company man," said Peverell. "On the Longshore. Davy Penhale." She said it quite flatly. "I knew his mother. She takes in washing in Ropery Row, below the high-water line. Her kitchen's dry because of these." She did not look at the cells. "His mother's kitchen."

"I know," said Mara.

She held her hand an inch from the glass. She could feel the count in it, through the air, faint. Four and four. She could feel what her hand would tell her if she laid it on. She had felt it on twenty-four cells on a hill and seven drums on another, and a trunk of drums under a city, and she knew what it would cost and how long it would take to come back.

"I'll not," she said.

Hart did not say anything. Peverell turned her head.

"I've been deciding for a fortnight," said Mara. "Whether to touch one. Rusk has asked me twice. You've asked me with your face every morning. Jessamy said on Monday night that somebody would have to, before the 23rd, or we'd be standing on the landing watching a ring we'd written down." She did not take her hand away from the glass. "He's right. Somebody will have to. Somebody will have to touch all thirty-six of them at once, on one word, at nine minutes past six, with the water still. If anybody's going to do it at all."

"Then touch one," said Peverell. "Now. And tell me what it says."

"If I touch one," said Mara, "then I'm the one who reads them. Then on the 23rd there are thirty-six cells and one hand, and the hand's mine, and it's been starved once already, and if it goes on the third cell, or the tenth, there's nobody." She looked at her hand, an inch from the glass. "That's what this bank is. One engine. Thirty-six cells and nobody can do anything alone. I'll not make the thing that takes it out of step another one engine, with my hand for the count."

"Then who reads it?"

"Thirty-six hands," said Mara. "Not mine. Your fitters. Six of the Works'. Rusk's men, if he'll send them, and he will, because I'll ask him tonight. The Company's closing crews, if Yeo will lend them, and she'll lend them now." She took her hand away from the glass at last and put it, not in her pocket, but on the gallery rail, flat. "I'll teach them. Every one, a cell each, by the 22nd. Jessamy will teach them when the water's still; his hand knows it and mine doesn't. You'll teach them your cells. And on the 23rd I'll stand on that landing with a watch and say *now,* and thirty-six people who aren't me will put their hands on thirty-six cases."

Peverell looked at her for a long time.

"I'll not be the keeper who stopped the pumps," she said.

"You'll be the keeper whose fitters read them," said Mara. "That's a different line in your book."

Peverell said nothing. Then she took a pencil out of her apron pocket and a fold of the pump house's fault paper, and she began, slowly, in her small crabbed hand, to write names.

Hart had not moved. Mara turned her head and looked at him.

"Well," she said. "You've been quiet."

He turned his watch on his wrist, once.

"Thirty-six," he said. "On one word. With the water still." He looked at the cells. "That's the right number. I'd not have said it. I'd have said one good hand." He was quiet a moment. "I've one hand that reads and one that doesn't, and the one that reads is the one that's ruined. I'll teach your thirty-six when the water's still. I'll teach them what quiet feels like." He looked at her. "You've decided."

"I've decided."

"Then I'll write it down," said Hart, and took his book of slacks out of his jacket with his left hand.

---

Ivey came up Quay Street at a quarter to eleven, with a lantern, and knocked at the door of Quiller's chandlery under the sail loft, and asked for Rowan.

Quiller sent the boy up. Kiva came down the outside stair in her coat with her right hand in linen and her book in her left, and found the Company catcher standing in the shut shop among the coils of rope and the tins of tar, with the lantern on the counter. She was short and broad, with square dark hands and a face that was tired all the way through. She had not taken her coat off. There was mud on it to the waist.

"The officer sent me," said Ivey. "Yeo. She said she'd asked the Graywater officer, and the Graywater officer said it wasn't hers to give. It was yours." She looked at Kiva's bandaged hand. "Your columns. On the far edge, and the second, and the pass in three. She wants them for the crew. She wants me to read them first. Before the Lane. Before the next Hunt." She stopped. "I'd sooner read them tonight. If you'll let me. I'll not sleep anyway."

Kiva did not ask anything. She went back up the stair, and into the loft, past Perrin asleep with Aske sitting on the end of his bed, past Jude at the window, past Pru lying on her back with her capped hands on her chest and her eyes open. She opened her box. She took out the ten sheets of Pike's craft in their seven columns, tied with tape, and the sheet on the handed crossing that she had written for Coombe in the second month with every hand in it by name. She took them down the stair and laid them on Quiller's counter under the lantern.

Ivey untied the tape. She read.

She read standing, with her square dark hands flat on either side of the sheets, the way Orla read, with her lips shut. She read the first sheet, and the second. She read Pike's heel, and his *not yet* and *now,* and the place where he never looked at the post. She read the far edge. She stopped at it and read it twice.

*The far edge: the second's. To say. Two words, once.*

"We give it the corner-hand," said Ivey.

"I know. He told me. Penhale. This morning, in the mud." Kiva stood on the other side of the counter. "He said when the Marshal's page was printed, he'd read it."

Ivey did not look up. She went on to the next sheet.

She read for half an hour. Kiva did not sit down. She stood on the other side of Quiller's counter in the smell of tar and rope, with the lantern between them, and when Ivey came to the sheet on the handed crossing and read it slowly, all of it, Kiva waited until she had finished.

Then she said what she had been waiting to say since the spur.

"It turned before the water," she said.

Ivey looked up.

"The stow. The Tarn's. It turned on its post at a quarter to one. The water turned at nine minutes to. Seven minutes after." Kiva made her voice plain. She did not want it written. She wanted it said, once, to somebody who could use it. "I had my fingers on a purse pole. They tell me the slack; they tell me nothing at the slack and something when it ends. They said nothing the whole seven minutes. The grass stood up straight. The water was still. And the stow turned anyway, as if it had heard the turn coming before the water had."

"Things like that don't hear," said Ivey.

"That's what Penhale said. At half past ten, when the upper one leaned before a gust." Kiva looked at the sheets on the counter. "I don't know that it heard. I know it moved first. So on the coast the far edge isn't when the tide turns. It's when the thing decides to. Whoever has the far edge can't watch the water for it. They have to watch the thing. The far end of it. All the way through the slack, even when the slack's holding." She was quiet for a moment. "And a stow's a sleeve. It's got two ends. The book's for a sheet."

Ivey did not say anything for a long time. She looked at the sheet on the handed crossing, and at Kiva's bandaged hand on the counter, and at the lantern.

"I turned him over," she said at last. "On the mud. I'd my hands on him for the count of three fathoms. I wasn't reading anything. I just had my hands on him." She put one square dark finger on the column headed *Before.* "He'd have been in this column. If anybody had written him. *A Company catcher, reserve, who goes to the near edge by the book.*"

"Coombe said to put him there," said Kiva. "In the first column. On the spur."

"Then put him there." Ivey squared the sheets together. She did it carefully, the way Penhale had squared a purse's iron ring into the mud. "I'll copy these tonight, if you'll let me. All of them. And I'll read them to Yeo's crew at six, before the printer's boy comes round with the Marshal's page. And at the next Hunt, I'll have a second." She tied the tape. "I've never had one. Nobody on this coast has. I'll have one. She'll have the far end of the thing, and she'll say it once."

"It's a place," said Kiva. "Not a person."

"I know. It's in your column." Ivey picked up the lantern. "He'd have liked that. He'd have laughed. *Nobody,* he said to me this morning on the spur. *She's got a thing called nobody. Nobody passes anything to nobody on this coast; she'd put half the Company out of a job.*" Her face did something, and stopped. "He laughed so hard a curlew got up."

"I heard it."

"I'll bring them back by the morning," said Ivey. "Thank you, Rowan."

She went out into Quay Street with the sheets under her coat and the lantern swinging, and Kiva stood in the dark shop and listened to her boots go away toward the low town, until there was nothing to hear but the gratings in the gutters rattling on the count.

---

Kiva went up the stair at half past eleven.

The loft was dark except for the candle on the sill by the north window. Aske had gone to his own bed. Perrin was asleep on his back with his hand on his ribs, breathing short. Pru was asleep at last, with her capped hands on the blanket. Anwen's bed was empty; she had not come back from Tar Lane. Jude was at the window.

He was standing where he always stood, with his good arm on the frame, looking out at the harbour. Kiva went and stood beside him. Below them the Long Quay's white lamps burned in a row along the black water without a flicker, all the way to the Counting House. Beyond the Mole, the sea was breathing, every count at once.

"Yeo was at the board," said Jude. "At nine. Behind us. I saw her." He did not turn his head. "She didn't say anything. She looked at me and she didn't say anything."

"She will."

"I know she will. I'd want it said that I know." He was quiet a long time. "I asked everybody since the autumn what it's for. The ash. I asked a Marshal and a foreman and my own four. I asked Yeo on the Ropewalk steps. I asked the sea last night, from the quay." He looked at the dark water. "Today I carried a man up a bank on a board who'd counted fathoms to a girl under a skin to keep her still. That's what it's for. I think. Not the ash. The bank. Carrying it up the bank." He stopped. "I don't know. I'd want it said I don't know. I'll stay anyway. When it's done. If she asks."

Kiva did not answer. There was nothing to say that would not have been smaller than what he had said. She stood beside him at the window.

After a while he went to bed.

She took her book out with her left hand and sat on the edge of her own bed by the candle. She opened it to the back, where the clean pages were, past the stub where she had torn one out on Monday for Pru. She ruled a fresh sheet into seven columns, badly, with the pencil in her left hand, because her right was in linen. At the head of the first she wrote the word that went there.

*Before.*

Under it, in the first line, she wrote what Coombe had told her to write, and what Ivey had told her to put there, and what she would have put there herself without being told.

*D. Penhale, catcher, the Saltmere Company. Reserve to the officer. Went to the near edge by the book. Counted fathoms under it to keep a girl still. Stopped at three.*

She looked at it for a long time. She did not cross anything out.

Then, though there had been no crossing and the fourth column was owed nothing, she did a thing she had no need to do. She took the disk out of her coat on its cord and laid it on the page, on the line she had just written, and looked at it in the candlelight.

It was cold.

She had known it would be. It had been cold through every crossing since the first, and through three doors and a fever and a Glory master's table, and it was cold now. She did not write that down. There was no column for it tonight. She put the disk back inside her coat.

Down the room, Aske turned over in his bed and said, without waking, or perhaps not, "Thursday. Soonest."

Kiva blew out the candle.

Outside the window, under the white lamps and the yellow ones and the dark, the town went on keeping its count. Four and four. Fifty-seven to the minute, or faster. On the Longshore the flood was at the top of its neap, quiet, over the flats and the creeks and Mallow Creek, over the place where a stow had stood and a man had knelt. In a shop on Quay Street a printer was setting a page, a letter at a time, with a dead man's name at the top of it. In a pump house a keeper was writing thirty-six names on fault paper. In a long tarred shed on the ridge, a girl with one door was walking up and down by lamplight, ten paces and back, learning to hear it.

The Measure went on. In the morning there would be the Lane.

She lay down in the dark with her bandaged hand on her chest and her warm hand at her side, and the first two fingers of it, without her meaning them to, came to rest on the iron rail of the bed.

*It keeps time.*

It was not a quarter to seven. It was not on purpose. She did not write it down.

---

# O'Connor 1.3.0 — numerical author formula

# Applying the owner's numerical craft formula

The owner explicitly selected
`/Users/drive/penname/research/ironprince-craft-formula.md` to govern this book.
Use its craft instructions more or less exactly, including the numbers. These
are active design targets for the planning model and Opus/Fable author, not merely
editorial observations. The compiler embeds sections 2–6 and 8–9 verbatim below
these instructions and records the source hash so runs can be compared.

Let the sentence-length distribution, short/long sentence shares, paragraph
rhythm and Flesch reading targets influence the actual prose. Let the POV shares,
front-loaded progression vocabulary and distributed character-development rates
influence how the book is built. Preserve section 8's secondary/use-to-taste
status; do not turn its explicitly excluded filler/repetition tics into quotas.

Write original scenes in open-range movements of six or more chapters. The formula constrains the intended
shape and cadence; the author still chooses conversations, tactics, discoveries
and how events unfold. Do not reduce this brief to “clear writing with progression.”
The owner names the cast. No source novel quotations, character substitutions or
scene-by-scene replication belong in the manuscript.

Aim at the supplied distributions across a movement and the cumulative book.
Book-third teaching proportions and book-level POV proportions need not be met
independently by each chapter. Do not stop after each sentence or chapter to run a
gate. Write the full movement, then compare target versus observed results in the
existing editorial pass and make a focused same-author repair for material drift.
Do not silently broaden tolerances or drop inconvenient targets.

Keep the source's own caveats: pause-derived paragraph/scene measures are
directional proxies, and development counts are heuristic. Their adoption as
design targets does not establish that they accurately measure the printed book.
The full source-book audit remains unfinished. Keep source values such as ~1.7x
and ~950 words unchanged; the known arithmetic/denominator questions belong in
the audit, not a silent rewrite of the owner's chosen prompt. If competing targets
cannot be reconciled, report the conflict rather than fabricating a passing score.

This prompt guides output; it does not train model weights or guarantee exact
statistical compliance. Compliance must be measured after prose exists.

Source: /Users/drive/penname/research/ironprince-craft-formula.md
Source SHA-256: 9f97e2f225c0bcf61d2e922719153fcf00e3b029f7b1552f213888d33130c0b8

The following craft sections (2–6, 8–9) are included verbatim. Sections 1 and 7's legal discussion is not drafting guidance. Use original names, world, systems, events and phrasing; the owner names the cast.

## 2. Sentence rhythm

| Metric | Target |
|---|---|
| Mean sentence length | 14.6 words |
| Median sentence length | 11 words |
| Spread (population stdev) | ~26 words — wide, meaning short declaratives sit right next to long compound-complex sentences, not clustered near the mean |
| Share of very short sentences (≤5 words) | 27.7% |
| Share of very long sentences (≥40 words) | 3.3% |

**Read:** roughly 1 sentence in 4 is a hard, short beat (often a single clause landing a
punch after a longer setup) — this is the "short sentence for impact" technique, not
uniform mid-length prose. Target this ratio, not just the mean; a manuscript that hits mean
14.6 but has no short-sentence spikes will not read the same.

## 3. Paragraph and scene rhythm (ASR-inferred proxy)

Whisper transcripts carry no typographic paragraph marks — this section is inferred from
pause gaps between narrated speech segments, not a count of the print manuscript's actual
paragraph breaks. Treat as directional, not exact.

| Metric | Target |
|---|---|
| Inferred paragraph length (pause ≥0.6s) | median 18 words, mean 26.8 words — short, frequent beats |
| Inferred scene/section length (pause ≥1.6s) | mean ~950 words between scene-level breaks, ~8.7 scene breaks per 10k words |

**Read:** short paragraphs are the default texture, with scenes running roughly 900–1000
words before a beat change (POV settle, location change, or time-skip). Avoid long
unbroken expository blocks; break early and often.

## 4. Progression-mechanic ("change frequency") callout rate

| Metric | Target |
|---|---|
| Progression/stat-vocabulary density | ~58 hits per 10k words (rank, growth-analog, spec, level-up, stat-block language) |
| Placement | front-loaded — the opening third of the book carries roughly 1.7x the progression-callout density of the middle and closing thirds (900 vs. 471 vs. 514 hits, by third) |

**Read:** the system gets *established* early and hard, then recedes into the background
as character and plot take over — it does not stay evenly dense through the whole book.
Build your own progression system's vocabulary (do not reuse his axis names — see §7), but
match this front-loaded shape: teach the mechanic aggressively in the first third, then let
it surface only at genuine turning points afterward.

## 5. Character-development beat cadence

Methodology: named-character mention within ~120 characters of a reflection/interiority
marker ("realized," "decided," "for the first time," "swore," "no longer," etc.) — a
heuristic count, not a scene-by-scene read.

| Metric | Target |
|---|---|
| Protagonist development-moment rate | 4.5 per 10k words |
| Protagonist distribution across thirds | roughly even (41 / 56 / 50 — a slight middle-third bump, not backloaded) |
| Supporting-cast development-moment rate | an order of magnitude lower per character (0.1–0.7 per 10k words each), spread across 8–10 named characters rather than concentrated in one deuteragonist |

**Read:** growth beats for the lead land steadily throughout, not saved up for a finale —
and the supporting cast gets *many* small beats each rather than one character getting a
big secondary arc. If your story gives one best-friend character 80% of the secondary
development budget, that's a different shape than this book's.

## 6. POV structure

| Metric | Target |
|---|---|
| Protagonist POV share | ~87% of chapter-word-volume (combining his given-name and surname references) |
| Secondary-character POV/focus share | ~13%, spread across 4–5 named characters at 2.5–3.7% each — brief cutaways, not sustained alternating-POV chapters |

**Read:** this is single-POV-dominant with occasional short cutaway scenes to a handful of
other named characters, not a true multi-POV structure. If you want to signal "feels like
Iron Prince," don't build alternating-chapter dual POV — keep the lead's POV overwhelming
and let other characters get glimpses, not chapters.

## 8. Supporting craft signals (secondary, use to taste)

- **Filler-word discipline:** "that" runs hot (92.1 per 10k words) — a known, common genre
  tic, not a target to imitate; if anything, tighten below this. Other fillers ("just,"
  "almost," "felt," "seemed") sit in the 5–25-per-10k band — moderate, not aggressively
  self-edited, not sloppy either.
- **Adverb density:** ~161 -ly adverbs per 10k words — fairly high; this is a book that
  lets adverbs carry tone rather than insisting on pure show-don't-tell. Not a craft flaw
  to correct, a genre-register signal to optionally match.
- **Dialogue-tag (reporting-verb) density:** ~41 per 10k words — moderate dialogue presence,
  consistent with an action/interiority-forward book rather than a dialogue-driven one.
- **Combat-vocabulary density:** ~22 per 10k words (strike/block/dodge/blade/impact/etc.) —
  present throughout, not just in set-piece fight chapters.
- **Readability:** Flesch Reading Ease 72.3, Flesch-Kincaid grade 6.8 — accessible YA/adult
  crossover register, short-to-medium sentence construction, low jargon-density outside the
  progression-vocabulary spikes.
- **Repetition discipline:** common connective 3-grams ("out of the," "one of the," "for a
  moment") sit at 2–4 per 10k words — normal English-prose background rate, not a
  distinctive tic to replicate. Don't chase these; they're noise, not signature.

---

## 9. One-paragraph brief for the drafting agent

> Write single-POV-dominant (~85–90% lead-character screen time), short-paragraph,
> pause-heavy prose (median paragraph ~18 words, scenes breaking roughly every 900–1000
> words). Mix short punch sentences (~28% of all sentences ≤5 words) against longer
> compound-complex ones (mean 14.6 words, wide spread) rather than settling into uniform
> mid-length sentences. Establish your progression system hard and often in the first
> third of the book, then let it recede to occasional high-stakes surfacing afterward. Land
> a protagonist growth/realization beat roughly every 2,200 words, spread evenly across the
> book rather than saved for the climax, and give 8–10 supporting characters small
> development beats each rather than concentrating growth in one deuteragonist. Moderate
> dialogue-tag and adverb use; accessible ~7th-grade reading level; combat vocabulary
> present in the background throughout, not confined to fight scenes. Every name, every
> system-label, every scene: yours, invented, original.

## NUMERICAL REVIEW

# Numerical formula alignment — after the movement

This is an informed editorial pass, not a blind cold read. Read the supplied
formula and full movement, then report target, observed result, method, coverage
and uncertainty. Keep numerical alignment separate from reader scores.

- Sentence mean, median, population standard deviation; shares ≤5 and ≥40 words.
- Written paragraph mean/median and actual scene/section boundaries. Compare with
  the requested targets, but do not equate typography with ASR pause segmentation.
- Flesch Reading Ease and Flesch-Kincaid grade. Identify the sentence tokenizer and
  syllable estimator; proper names/system terms can affect estimates.
- Progression vocabulary hits per 10,000 words, and placement by equal word-count
  book thirds once available. Define a project-specific lexicon before counting;
  do not borrow the source novel's names. An incomplete book cannot confirm the
  full front-loading ratio. Report provisional cumulative coverage instead.
- Lead development moments and supporting-cast distribution: cite actual choices
  or realizations and distinguish manually identified beats from regex proxies.
- POV word share: identify viewpoint segments, not the most frequently mentioned
  name. Track the lead and secondary viewpoints against the book-level allocation.
- Secondary signals: -ly words, reporting verbs, combat terms, fillers and repeated
  phrases. Publish lexicons and counting definitions. A suffix count is not proof
  that every matched word is an adverb. Preserve optional/non-target distinctions.

Use reproducible counts when tooling exists. Otherwise mark a metric UNMEASURED;
never estimate a precise score by intuition. Save the method or command with the
report so later runs use the same definitions. Inspect ASR reference limitations
without discarding the owner's chosen drafting targets.

Locate meaningful departures, especially uniform sentence lengths, missing short
beats, reading level drift, dense paragraphs, late teaching or a coequal second
lead replacing the requested POV shape. Consolidate the most important repairs
with continuity findings into the existing bounded author repair. Protect exact
owner text and successful scenes. Do not iterate indefinitely to hit decimals or
hide irreconcilable targets. Bring consequential formula conflicts to the owner.

# Kindling 2680, Book Two — state ledger

> Built by TAC

Status: **Book Two entry state only. No Book Two prose exists.** Everything in sections 1–9
is the accepted Book One exit state (evening of Sunday the 16th of the tenth month, 2680),
carried from `books/book-2680-opening/STATE_LEDGER.md`,
`books/book-2680-opening/provenance/MOVEMENT-006-CONTINUITY.md`, and chapters 47–48. Where
this file and the Book One manuscript disagree, the Book One manuscript governs. Section 10
records the checks made before this file was saved. Section 11 lists what was reconciled.
Movement blocks are appended below section 11 only from surviving Book Two prose.

## 1. Calendar

Every month has thirty days and twelve months make the year (Aske's "eleven weeks and four
days" from Monday the 10th of the tenth month to the new year confirms that no day stands
between the twelfth month and the first). A month therefore begins two weekdays after the
one before it.

| Month | 1st falls on | 30th falls on | Fixed points |
|---|---|---|---|
| Tenth, 2680 | Saturday | Sunday | Book One ends Sunday the 16th. First free day of Book Two: **Monday the 17th.** |
| Eleventh, 2680 | Monday | Tuesday | **The 1st:** Rhea Sorn's card goes back to her, signed or torn. Mara's hand is on a card "until the eleventh month." |
| Twelfth, 2680 | Wednesday | Thursday | Nothing fixed. |
| First, 2681 | **Friday** | Saturday | **The 1st:** Aske's ban on anything lit ends, if he signs the card up. The night van offer begins. Perrin joins his Hall's second team. |
| Second, 2681 | Sunday | Monday | Nothing fixed. |
| Third, 2681 | **Tuesday** | **Wednesday** | **The National Measure, at Saltmere.** Selka Dray "on her feet by the third month." Rhea Sorn's card ends on the 30th. The 11th (a Friday) is seventy-seven years to the day after the Ferren keeper's fever entry of 2604. |
| Fourth, 2681 | Thursday | Friday | Nothing fixed. |

Earlier anchors that prove the chain: the fifth month's 29th was a Wednesday (the Pit); the
sixth began on a Friday; the seventh on a Sunday; the ninth on a Thursday (the Regional, Friday
the 16th and Saturday the 17th); the tenth on a Saturday.

## 2. Kiva Rowan

- **Age and household.** Seventeen. Lives over Rowan Instruments with her aunt and guardian,
  Mara Rowan. A guardian's hand is still wanted beside her own wherever a rule says "under
  eighteen." No birthday is fixed in the supplied record; Book Two does not move her age
  unless the Book One manuscript gives a date.
- **Place.** *Rowan, K., Third Division, the Crooked Line: seated. No longer provisional.
  Catcher.* (the Chair, the 14th of the tenth month). Crooked Line third team; Team Seven.
  "Her sponsor's line and every condition on her certificate stand as written. She is not
  thereby classified." There is still no column for her; there is a page headed *Witnessed*
  with two lines on it.
- **The Home Line.** *Rowan, K. Entered on the Line's roll as catcher, autumn 2680. Hand as
  before.* Not a seat. No wage but a line-hand's day rate when called. Called by a Line
  foreman, with her sponsor's leave while she has a sponsor, "to set a mark, to read ground,
  to call a count that is hers and name the ones that are not." She has not yet been called.
- **Certificate, as it stands.**
  - Orla Dane's line: recovery only, dark hands; **and one crossing, Ember to still, low, to a
    mark, declared to a count, on a sanctioned mast read by the Chair's reader, one in seven
    days.** Not certified: any stack, any third door, anything Orla has not seen six times.
  - The Chair's conditions of the spring: one door declared to the referee before every
    sanctioned event, and any change declared; her book to her sponsor weekly; no live-cell
    events until her sponsor certifies in writing; one sanctioned-event incident ends her
    season. (The 2680 season is read. Whether that last line is re-issued for 2681 is the
    Chair's to say and is not on any page.)
  - The Chair's conditions of the 2nd of the seventh month: no second door on any Circuit
    floor except a crossing declared to a count and read at the time from a sanctioned mast
    by the office's reader; a stack so read is an incident; not at the Bench; recovery only on
    any floor with a contained rift. No crossing counts unless the tape shows the cliff. Noa
    Bexley is reader to the office.
  - **Selka Dray's mast rule** (any candidate with more than one door is read from a
    sanctioned mast by a reader of the Meridian office) has gone to Saltmere under seal.
- **Crossings on the record.** Ember to still: one under a field direction (the 29th of the
  fifth month), then Orla's six on sanctioned masts, and one begun and not landed in a lane
  at the Regional. Hand to Ember: landed once (the eighth month, 0.30). A crossing costs
  about 0.30 to 0.31 of a dram, of which about 0.26 is the gap. An unlanded crossing still
  spends the hand and the week's go. A stack is a miss, forbidden, and an incident if read.
- **Three doors.** Order: hand, Ember, still ("catch, carry, plant"); 0.59 whole. **Three
  clean in all: a yard (the 29th of the eighth month), the Pit (the 17th of the ninth), the
  Old Yard (the 5th of the tenth).** Uncertified, unclassified, and by the Chair's written
  entry "not to be asked of her." The last two were done under a recorded Home Line direction
  and an officer's hand, with the price said first by her healer; the last was offered and not
  asked. Never twice inside a fortnight. Aske does not know that the arm would come back a
  fourth time.
- **Readings.** Nothing has been measured since Classification Day, 2680: Draw 3.1; Hold,
  Edge 3.0, Ward 2.6, Stance 6.2; rank unsettled (the frame flickers); first door, none; the
  Call column dark. **No rank, Hold, Draw or bench figure has been re-measured.**
- **Body.**
  - **The first two fingers of the left hand have permanently lost fine sensation.** They
    bend, grip, and report pressure ("something's pushing"). They do not tell edge from flat,
    hot from cold, or one thread from two. Ward-trace ash and rest are ruled out as remedies.
  - The left arm lost feeling to a hand above the elbow on the 5th of the tenth month and came
    back over four days "to where it was, and not a hair less."
  - **Aske's card of the 10th: nothing lit until the new year. "Not a step. Not a ward for a
    tap." Dark hands and her voice.** His standing rule before that: one crossing in seven
    days, with the hand seen the next morning; the three-door is not on his card at all.
  - The left knee is still strapped and complained once, running. The three heat-lines run
    from the breastbone over the collarbone toward the left shoulder and upper arm.
- **Handoffs she names** (the list of the 30th of the ninth month): the cord, all three turns
  (Pru Finch); the slack of a hem on her left side (she takes a right-hand corner or is called
  to); tin lids with a lip; the pin of a seal and whether wax has gone off (Anwen has the
  wax); a button and a cuff; **the count of a thing that keeps time (somebody else's, named
  before).**
- **The disk.** Cold. It warmed once, on the 29th of the fifth month, after her first correct
  deliberate crossing, and has been cold through every crossing and all three three-door
  sequences since. Recognized by the Compact Library's Reader as the plate of nine rings
  carried by the first teachers and *released* by appointment. Held of the Line; borne by K.
  Rowan; not equipment, an instrument, or evidence; no hand but the bearer's without a vote of
  the watch; the bearer writes in the fourth column within the week after any crossing. What
  it is for is unknown. The first bearer's book is lost.
- **The dream.** No recurrence since Classification Day, 2680. No fever on any night of the
  autumn. She has Annick's phrase *the sky unroofed* and the Ferren keeper's line of 2604.
  **The name "the Unroofed Sky" has never appeared on the page.**
- **Her book.** Green cover, soft at the corners; a spring, a summer, and an autumn; no page
  ever torn out. It goes to Orla weekly and lies on the kitchen table nightly for Mara.

## 3. Team Seven

- **The four:** Kiva Rowan (catcher), Anwen Pryce (Hold), Perrin Cade (carry; calls *off*
  and keeps the brake), Kellan Renn (blade). **Jude Talley** stands as Kill by name, on the
  line for a head's share and not on the book. **Pru Finch** is a catcher with a sheet of her
  own and the standing reserve.
- **Standing.** Fourth at the Regional Measure. **Second step, in its own right;** the
  brackets came off on the 14th of the tenth month. It goes forward to the National Measure.
- **The book, signed by four on Saturday the 15th of the tenth month:**

| Line | Drams |
|---|---:|
| **Held** | **25.53** |
| **Sealed medical (W, plain ward-trace)** | **0.8** |
| By name to J. Talley, in one autumn | 7.8 |

  Check: 23.62 − 3.0 (second purse sheet and poles, for Pru) − 0.70 (call drams spent) − 0.59
  (three-door line) + 7.2 (to the book from Stopgate's pot) = 26.53; less 1.0 returned at par
  to the wardens of Stopgate = **25.53.** The loan of 4.0 to the casualty reserve went out and
  came back. Sealed: 2.0 − 1.2 (Mara Rowan's reading hand, entered as medical) = **0.8.**
- **The reserve cannot be made up.** There is no plain ward-trace to be bought in Meridian;
  every can has "had the hair" since the thaw. It "stays eight-tenths until somebody kills
  something quiet."
- **Not on the team's book:** Kiva's own weekly half dram; Pru's pay, which is off the
  Circuit's reserve card.
- **Red, and untouched:** Seven's own rung from the Pit, stepped, on Kiva's tag; Pru's bag
  from Stopgate on her own tag. Across the district 111 and 503.7 drams stand red-tagged from
  before, with 560.4 more from Stopgate. Where the red row goes is not said. The patent claim
  on the Weighbridge bag is before the Compact's court, unruled.
- **Saltmere.** **No warrant has been posted or entered.** Kellan reckons two drams a head at
  the least, five heads, ten drams. Nobody from the Circuit has been sent to the coast in
  eleven years.
- **Open inside the four:** which four Kellan stands with at Saltmere. He cut for the Halden
  license four at the Regional and declared for Seven under rule nineteen once the floor was
  struck. He counts himself among "five heads" on the 15th. No page settles it.

## 4. The offer

Rhea Sorn's card, slate blue, read in full to the four and signed on its back by all four on
Saturday the 15th of the tenth month:

> *Rowan, K. Catcher's second, to Pike. The night van, two nights in the month, from the first
> of the new year, when her healer's card is up. Dark hands. No door on my floor that is not
> on her certificate. Finch with her, for the cord, if Finch will. If T7 is called on a night
> of mine, she is T7's and I go short. Until the last of the third month. Then we look again,
> and either of us may say no. No seat is promised. R.S.*

- Kiva's two conditions, accepted: the *before* column is read out each night to all; her four
  have the first word.
- Pru Finch has said yes, on being asked by Kiva as well.
- A catcher's second "sets nothing. She calls nothing." She kneels at Pike's other hem and
  tells him afterward what he did that he did not say aloud. She is not to open her mouth
  until the bag is sealed.
- At most **six nights:** two in each of the first, second, and third months of 2681.
- The card is to go back to Rhea on **Monday the 1st of the eleventh month.**
- Rhea's slate has six columns: *Before. Closed. Lives. Works. Tinned. Remarks.*

## 5. Other people, as they stand

| Person | Body, place, and open thread |
|---|---|
| **Mara Rowan** | Grade Two, restored. Reader of the trunk for Examiner Rusk; has the hill, the bank, and the stopping of it. **Left (reading) hand starved to the wrist on the 6th and eased inside the hour: a week before she reads a drum, a month before she reads a bank.** The right hand has told her nothing since Linden Row (2677). Eleven sheets in seven columns, *How the Ferren bank was taken out of step, with what was got wrong first,* are tied and waiting on the dresser for the coast boat on Monday the 17th. Her page of the 29th (Kiva's dream as told) is sealed and timed in the Library's press. The Order's six questions are in her hands; two are to be answered. |
| **Orla Dane** | Accountable sponsor; her standing rides on Kiva's record. Holds **Jessamy Hart's card** and has shown it to nobody. Moved her crate a pace north. Bad left knee, stick. |
| **Anwen Pryce** | Has the wax and the sealed reserve. A relief's place on Doran Mace's ward line (the night van) for next summer; Mace left her name where it was (*Said so as she went*). Has told her mother. "Done minding." |
| **Perrin Cade** | **Two ribs sprung, not broken; strapped.** 380 kilos for thirty counts, cut in the stone. His Hall's second team from the new year. Minds, and says so. |
| **Kellan Renn** | Has the Halden yard, Tuesdays and Thursdays, nothing owed. On Seven's book "as a blade who's told where he's wanted." His mother signs "A. Renn, grader." |
| **Jude Talley** | Kill by name; 7.8 drams by name. Will stand at Saltmere, asked by name. Wants to see the sea. |
| **Pru Finch** | Her own sheet, her own red tag, the cord. Nipped to the first joint once. Goes to the night van with Kiva. |
| **Noa Bexley** | Reader to the Chair's office. His page reads *Not yet weighed* and *Not one thing.* Three columns before the Charter Board, Mr. Adlam second. |
| **Selka Dray** | Left leg broken above the knee and burned with cold on the 17th of the ninth month; in a chair; "on my feet by the third month." Unpersuaded, on the record twice. Means to beat Kiva in a lane at Saltmere, on a declared count, with a needle on her. Not a friend. |
| **Rhea Sorn** | Graywater. Wrote to the board; the brackets came off. Burned a card that said *mine.* |
| **Pike** | Graywater's catcher, six-and-twenty years. Will have Kiva as his second. |
| **Nairn** | Graywater's second blade; the leg healed; walking without the stick. |
| **Hollis Vane** | Home Line foreman ("the Sag"). Three directions to Kiva in his ledger, two with a cost column. |
| **Ysra Crest** | Placement Chair. Posted "seated." Keeps the *Witnessed* page. |
| **Healer Aske** | Keeps Kiva's card. Would not round the price down. |
| **Lowen Brask** | Old Four; named the hold and the paid-out string; two to the bar. |
| **Examiner Rusk, Mr. Adlam, Lumb, Ansty, A. Renn, Officer Yeo** | As in the Movement Six checkpoint. Yeo went south on the 8th and will ask for the Works' reader again in the spring. |

## 6. The grid and the rifts

- **The Ferren bank** (twenty-four cells, the Municipal Works') **is out of step.** The method:
  nothing in the water that keeps time; pumps stopped; every cell quenched on one word; fresh
  cans, each salted with a pinch of plain ash that "has never heard a beat"; a card with its
  own count under each handle, "not two alike in this room"; a reader between each cell and
  the next; each pump restarted on its own eight cells. It needs the main to be **still.**
- **Seven drums remain in step** on the Brickfields trunk: **19, 20, 21, 25, 26, 27, 28,** each
  alone on an open link, to be done one a day with the wardens' old winter stock. None has been
  done on the page.
- **Drum twenty-nine is fouled** (about thirty drams through a split plank). Its four streets
  are on boxes for about a month from the 6th of the tenth month.
- The Stopgate sluice works. The Crooked Line School's core house never fell in.
- **Saltmere's harbour bank, thirty-six cells, went into step at twenty past two on the 17th of
  the ninth month and has not come out.** Its pumps have not dropped a stroke. Nobody at
  Saltmere knows how to take a bank out.
- **Nobody has said** that water can step a cell, why a tear throws stepped ash, why husks
  stood a hold, or why the bank fell in on the 17th.
- The Home Line's suspension of held tears has not been lifted on the page.
- A can of civic stock in the Works' own store was cut with soot. Noticed; nobody has taken
  it up.
- **Evidence.** The notch on Kiva's pen falls eleven seconds after her second cliff (three
  occasions). The lamps' T-0 falls when a hold comes down. They are two things. T-0 entries
  on record: 2446, 2604, 2676, and three in 2680 (the Pit twice, Stopgate once). Noa's three
  columns (people; rifts and spawn; Saltmere) all rise, faster since the fifth month. One
  unvouched line says an overseas port's bank fell in at the same hour.

## 7. Knowledge boundaries

| Fact | Who has it | Who does not |
|---|---|---|
| **Kiva has dreamed, and what she saw** | Kiva; **Mara** (told in full; her page is sealed) | Everyone else |
| That a dream came on Classification Day, with no content | **Ysra Crest** | — |
| Annick's fever entry, read in the room | Kiva (the sixth month); Orla (2664). Neither has told the other what it says. Orla has not been told that Kiva dreams. | Mara has not read it |
| That Kiva's lips moved, counting, as she read Annick | The sister of the Even Hand; the Halden endowment's observer | — |
| The Ferren keeper's entry of 2604 | Kiva, Mara, Lumb (as a page in his book) | Aske, A. Renn, Lumb, and Mr. Adlam were out of earshot and **do not know that Kiva has dreamed** |
| The four T-0 entries, the 2446 margin, the disputed box, Orla's 2664 reading | Anwen, Kellan, Perrin, Pru, Noa | **None of them knows of the dream** |
| **Jessamy Hart's card and his request to read Kiva's work** | **Orla Dane only** | **Kiva.** Also Mara, the four, the Chair |
| That Hart is a pump-fitter on the south coast whose harbour bank stepped | Orla | Kiva |
| A color in the dream with no word for it | Kiva alone; on no page | Mara |
| What the bearer's book records of Mara's refusal in 2669 | Kiva, Vane | Mara, as far as the page shows |
| What the disk is for; the first book | Nobody | — |

## 8. Institutions

- The Regional result stands without brackets: **1. Halden License. 2. Old Four. 3. Team
  Twenty-Eight. 4. T7.** "The first company goes forward to the National Measure, at Saltmere,
  in the third month, 2681. Entries under each team's own hand."
- Circuit rules made in 2680 and still in force: a gate shall be left lit; any catcher may
  red-tag a bag on stated grounds; no ash above I-trace on any Circuit floor; every Measure
  dram combed at the gate.
- The Charter Board has three sheets under the Chair's seal. It has not answered.
- The Order of the Even Hand's offer of a year as a guest, one door, is "on the latch."
- The Crooked Line School's tenth edition restores the lane in seven columns, with Kiva's
  lines in the fourth and sixth.

## 9. Unresolved threads carried in

The cause of the Step. Why a hold stood. What the notch and the lamps are. Who else has
dreamed. The filter. The disk's purpose and first book. Kiva's classification. Whether three
doors can be made safe, taught, or done a fourth time. Saltmere's bank. The seven drums. The
red bags and the court claim. The cut can. The Charter Board. Hart. Dray. Rhea's "we look
again." Kellan's four. The warrant. The reserve that cannot be made up. Mara's sealed page.
The overseas line.

## 10. Checks made before saving (planning session, 2026-10-12)

| Item | Source checked | Result |
|---|---|---|
| Month chain | Checkpoint §1 and §9 item 21; ledger blocks for Movements Three to Six; Aske's count in chapter 47 | Consistent. The first month of 2681 begins on a Friday; the third on a Tuesday; the 30th of the third month is a Wednesday. |
| Certification | Ledger, Movement Five block; checkpoint §2; the board in chapter 48 | Seated, unclassified; one certified crossing (Ember to still); hand to Ember not certified; three-door not certified and "not to be asked." |
| Body limits | Chapter 47 (Aske, Monday the 10th); checkpoint §2 | Two fingers permanent; arm returned; nothing lit until the new year. |
| Team Seven's book | Checkpoint §6 table and check line; chapter 48 | Held 25.53; sealed 0.8; Talley 7.8. Arithmetic re-run and correct. |
| Rhea Sorn's offer | Chapter 48, card quoted in full | Quoted above verbatim. Six nights at most. No seat. |
| Hart's knowledge boundary | Chapter 48 (Orla's cutaway and the closing survey); checkpoint §0 and §4 | Private to Orla. Kiva has not been told. |
| National Measure timing | The board in chapter 48 | Saltmere, the third month, 2681. No day is given; Book Two sets the days. |

## 11. Reconciled for Book Two

1. **Hart's hand.** Orla says in chapter 8 that he "lost a hand" and left "with one hand."
   His card says "the hand, the bad one." Both stand: Orla never saw him afterward. He has one
   working hand and one ruined one that is still on his arm.
2. **Who dreams.** The series bible says that "a small number of high-rank practitioners"
   record the fragments. Book One's second account is a pump keeper's. The accepted book
   governs: the fever reaches ordinary people who sit beside banks and lamps; high-rank
   accounts are fuller. Book Two's plan builds on both.
3. **The notch.** The Movement Three block's "about forty seconds" was the keeper's guess and
   is superseded: eleven seconds.
4. **Kellan's "five heads."** Left open by Book One and settled inside Book Two (see
   `BOOK_MAP.md`).
5. **The seven drums** are 19, 20, 21, 25, 26, 27, and 28. Twenty-nine is fouled, not stepped.

## Movement One block — chapters 1–8 (first draft, 2026-10-13)

Status: **from first-draft prose, unreviewed.** Everything here is on the page of
`manuscript/chapter-01.md` to `chapter-08.md`. The full record is
`provenance/MOVEMENT-001-CONTINUITY.md`. If review or repair changes the prose, this block is
corrected from the surviving text. Span: Monday the 17th of the tenth month, 2680, to Saturday
the 30th of the first month, 2681.

### M1.1 Kiva Rowan

- Seventeen; seated; **unclassified.** On the Home Line's roll; not yet called.
- Dark for eighty-one days. **Each door relit singly under Aske's eye on Friday the 1st of
  the first month, 2681.**
- **Aske's card:** single doors, declared, one at a time; a crossing, one in seven days *at
  the most,* hand seen the next morning; the three-door "not on my card."
- **Re-read on the Circuit's instruments, Monday the 4th:** Draw **1.6** (was 3.1). Hold:
  Edge **3.1** (3.0), Ward **4.5** (2.6), Stance **6.5** (6.2). Rank, one door declared and
  two held shut: **Flame.** First door: none. With two doors used in sequence (one certified
  crossing, Thursday the 7th, read by Noa): **dashes;** "none shown." Not a classification.
- **Crossings:** one in this movement, Ember to still, 0.30 (0.26 the gap), off her own
  thimble. Hand to Ember: none; not certified. **Three doors: three clean; not asked; not
  done.**
- **Body:** first two left fingers as before; arm as it was; knee strapped.
- **The two fingers, proved (4th of the twelfth month):** through something solid, at a light
  touch, they say that a thing keeps time (six in seven); never what time; they read her own
  pulse if she leans or is frightened. "A reason to look." Not healing.
- **The disk:** cold. One fourth-column line (the 8th of the first month).
- **The dream:** no recurrence. Known in full to Mara alone, still.
- **The Chair's conditions for 2681:** as for 2680, re-entered line by line; at Saltmere her
  door is declared "by her captain's voice"; **one incident at a sanctioned board ends her
  season.**
- **Writing:** a copy of her own method sent to J. Hart (six sheets; three squares ruled off;
  one line left out and declared); **ten sheets of Pike's craft in seven columns;** a drill
  sheet begun.

### M1.2 Team Seven

- **Entry for the National Measure signed and the warrant paid, Friday the 29th of the first
  month.** *Captain: Cade, P., carry. Catcher: Rowan, K. Hold: Pryce, A. Kill: Talley, J.
  Reserve: Finch, P., catcher.*
- **Kellan Renn stands for Halden License** (*Blade: Renn, K.;* captain Pettit). His name
  stays on Seven's book for calls in Meridian.
- **Jude Talley is on the team book from the entry.**
- **The book:**

| Line | Drams |
|---|---:|
| Carried in | 25.53 |
| Floor, the 22nd of the tenth | −0.32 |
| Floor, the 16th of the first | −0.57 |
| Warrant, five heads at 3.0 | −15.00 |
| **Held** | **9.64** |
| Spoken for: the Marshal's floor (4.0); hand to Ember, six goes (1.8) | 5.80 |
| **Uncommitted** | **3.84** |
| **Sealed medical (W, plain)** | **0.8, unopened** |

- No plain ward-trace is to be had in Meridian or from Saltmere's shelf.
- **License:** second step in its own right; **no Flask license in its own right.** By the
  Board's notice a four without one hunts attached.
- Seven lost a yard contest to Old Four, six to five (the 16th). Correction in hand: a mark
  is held first and tested under the holder; *mine* before *yours.*

### M1.3 The night van

- The card went back signed on the 1st of the eleventh month and **stands unaltered.**
- **Two nights done:** the 9th (a string of three; three clean bags) and the 22nd–23rd of the
  first month.
- **The 23rd, the coal wharf below the Hythe:** a string of four with its skin laid on the
  ground for a cloth, which folds from the far edge and takes a rung home (**"a purse
  string"**). **Pike's hands were taken to above both wrists;** six weeks. Kiva said two words
  before a bag was sealed, a count late. Pru caught three rungs under Rhea's slate. Nothing
  tinned clean; 198 drams and a tin of skin red-tagged.
- **Standing line on Rhea Sorn's slate:** *The far edge: the second's. To say. Two words,
  once.*
- **Four nights remain** (two in the second month, two in the third): Varley, of Imrie's van,
  for the cloth; Pike rides as a voice; Kiva is second to Pike.

### M1.4 Other people

| Person | State |
|---|---|
| **Mara Rowan** | Seven drums out by the 25th of the tenth. Twelfth sheet: no count shared with anything on the same water, pumps included. Left hand whole; reads banks from the 1st of the eleventh. Has Saltmere's failure, six minutes in a fitter's hand, and a tide table she cannot yet read. Has not answered Yeo. Knows of Hart. |
| **Orla Dane** | Told Kiva of Hart on the 24th of the tenth. Has not read the copy. Hart has written to Kiva and not to her. |
| **Jessamy Hart** | Two cards and a list. The fitter with the watch at the Saltmere harbour pump house. Asked what Kiva's two fingers tell her; has been asked what his hand tells him. Not met. |
| **Pike** | Fifty-four. Hands in linen to the elbow. Right thumb slow since the autumn before last, in his own writing. |
| **Perrin Cade** | Ribs mended; his Hall's second team; captain, unwilling. |
| **Anwen Pryce** | Walks a wall forty paces. Keeps the wax. |
| **Pru Finch** | Second pin; caught on a company's floor; reserve. |
| **Selka Dray** | On two sticks. Unpersuaded. Marten captains Twenty-Eight. |
| **Rhea Sorn** | Card kept; slate changed. |
| **Dinah Coombe** | Hunt Marshal, Blaze; comes to Meridian in the second month; one floor a four at a dram a head. Not met. |

### M1.5 The grid

- **No drum on the Brickfields trunk is in step.** Drum twenty-nine's streets came off their
  boxes on the 6th of the eleventh month.
- A drum given a pump's count fell in with the pump through the water (drum nineteen, twice).
- **Saltmere tried the twelve sheets on the 20th and 21st of the eleventh month and failed:**
  the harbour's water is never still. Six cells went back in, at one to forty-one minutes;
  the long holds were all on the second day's hour. Six jars of plain spent; no more given.

### M1.6 Knowledge boundaries, changed

| Fact | Now also known to |
|---|---|
| Hart's first card and his request | Kiva (from Orla); Mara (from Kiva) |
| That Hart is a fitter at the Saltmere harbour pump house, by his hand on the list | Kiva, Mara |
| Hart's second card | Kiva, Mara, Orla (in the book) |
| That one line was left out of the copy | Hart, Orla (not which line, nor why) |
| **That Kiva dreams** | **Nobody new.** |
| Pike's thumb | Pike, Kiva, Rhea, Stroud |
| Nine red tags on the Halden license yard | The Rowan table of the 25th (Kiva, Anwen, Perrin, Jude, Pru, Mara). Not Birch. |

### M1.7 Dates ahead

Road south: inside thirty days of the 30th of the first month. The gate: Tuesday the 1st of
the third month. The Bench: Monday the 14th, **forty-four days** on. Rhea Sorn's card ends on
the 30th of the third month.

### M2.1 Kiva Rowan

- Seventeen; seated; **unclassified;** one door declared at every board.
- **Hand to Ember: four crossings,** Circuit's mast, read by Noa, Orla present: the 1st (0.31), the
  8th (0.30), the 15th (0.31), the 22nd (0.30). **Four of Orla's six; not certified.** The Chair's
  ledger: the fifth and sixth at the Saltmere gate mast, read by N. Bexley, sponsor present, "one in
  seven days and not faster. Not certified until the sixth."
- **The hand sulks longer each time** (Aske, Monday at seven): knuckle/warm by noon; wrist/warm by
  four; an inch above the wrist/warm next morning; middle of the forearm/warm Wednesday the 25th at
  seven ("two nights"). After the fourth the card was held until warm. The two fingers as before.
- **Aske's card:** one crossing in seven days at the most; hand seen the next morning; "four of six."
  **Aske travels south with Seven by road.**
- **No Ember-to-still crossing, no stack, no three-door.** Three clean; not asked.
- **The two fingers** said *nothing* (and were believed, pulse 68–72) on a stone by the old kiln on
  Cowl Edge and on the Order's door jamb. The kiln husk sang the seventh reed.
- **The disk:** cold every time, including beside Clemency Thrale and through the fever.
- **The dream: recurred** on the Sag ferry, Friday the 27th, with a fever (112 by Pru) that broke at
  about four on Saturday. As on the page: no roof; nothing over her, enormous, paying attention;
  roads of unlike lights, some going out, some white; she was "not so high as she had been." **She
  told nobody.** Aske knows only that she has had such a fever before. **The dream is not in her
  book;** the fever is. "Unroofed Sky" has not appeared.
- **First time near a Glory master:** her three doors "lay down" in Thrale's presence.

### M2.2 Team Seven

- **Flask license in its own right,** the Marshal's floor, Thursday the 12th, one remark (a bag at
  sixty-two). *Under its captain's name.*
- **Lost the Marshal's lane to Halden License six to two** (the 10th); correction: the tap, the hand
  on the knee, the Kill guarding the pass. Kellan read it twice in six on the 16th.
- **The handed crossing** (Jude's name): Finch catch → "nobody" → Rowan carry (declared Ember) →
  "nobody" → Cade plant. Clean at a quarter count at both gaps on the 11th and before Coombe on the
  13th, on the Low Lane's unsanctioned mast. *Mine before yours* between unlike doors reads as one
  line (a stack) and is not used. **Not done live.**
- **Hunt at Cowl Edge** (19th–20th), own entry, under Cade, witnessed by Vane: a pack at Garside's mill
  (keeping the wheel's time; the hatch shut) and a lone husk in the old kiln. **59.2 clean; nothing
  plain;** the four's eighth, 7.40.
- **Injuries:** Talley, left shin burned (the 2nd) and left arm frosted to the elbow (the 20th, not
  taken; sling). Cade's ribs complain when he lifts.
- **Equipment:** Finch's own purse sheet (her name on the pole) frosted and replaced (2.40, no pole).
  Kiva had carried it to the mill as her second cloth, and it went over the race lip. Finch keeps the
  frosted sheet. Mrs. Pryce's grey double went over the third heap, and it is still the drill sheet.
  Perrin keeps Coombe's knotted rope.
- **The book:**

| Line | Drams |
|---|---:|

| **Uncommitted** | **about 5.63** |
| **Sealed medical (W, plain)** | **2.0** (0.8 + 1.2 given by the Order) |

  Check: 9.64 − 10.81 + 7.40 = 6.23. **The sealed 2.0 is bound in both books: *For a hand. Not for a
  bank, a floor, a crossing or a sale.*** Signed Cade and Rowan. The Order's 1.2 combed full on the
  third and silent on the seventh. That is the first comb since the thaw over *whole* plain ash. The
  soot-cut 2669 jar refused on the 24th was silent on the seventh and thin on the third.

### M2.3 The night van

- **Two nights, as voice and cloth:** Friday the 6th (a pack of three husks at the Brickfields tram
  sheds; three bags, 155 of 162) and Tuesday the 17th (a purse string in the level Corn Exchange yard;

- **Pike:** dressings to a band at each wrist by the 17th; fingers move a quarter; hands "by the fourth

### M3.1 Kiva Rowan

- Seventeen; seated; **unclassified**; one door declared at every board **by her captain's voice**.
- **Hand to Ember: six of six, certified** by Orla Dane on Thursday the 10th in the Chair's ledger (four at
  Meridian, two at the Saltmere gate mast, read by N. Bexley). **Two crossings certified**: Ember to still;
  hand to Ember. One in seven days at most.
- **Fifth** (Thu 3rd, 0.31) — cold to a hand below the elbow; warm Sun 6th at seven: **three nights.**
  **Sixth** (Thu 10th, 0.30) — cold to **the elbow, an ache in the joint** (noon and four; a hand below at
  ten; the noon check is told after Adlam's 11.30 visit and the wire *(repair)*); Aske on Friday at seven:
  "three nights to go" *(repair)*; warm **Mon 14th at seven: four nights.** Aske: the reach is at the elbow; he wants it warm on more than one morning before a seventh;
  **Thursday the 17th is the soonest.** Aske says the price before each crossing.
- **The notch** at the Saltmere gate mast follows a **single** cliff: **seven seconds** (fifth), **six**
  (sixth), on every pen. Meridian: eleven, after a second cliff only. Unexplained; "a coast thing."
- **The two fingers:** say *keeps time* everywhere in Saltmere; say **nothing at slack** (Gannet Creek; the
  basin grating) and report the slack's end before the water moves. **Hart's method begun:** same question,
  same hour (a quarter to seven), yes or no; first entry Sun 13th: yes.
- **The disk:** cold every time, including on the Proprietor's table. Fourth-column lines for both crossings.
- **Fever, Friday the 11th** after the Counting House: to 112, broke about three; **no dream.** Aske's card:
  *Fever, the second this year. Both near a Glory master, within the day.* Kiva said aloud, "Nothing came."
  Aske wrote a line on the card that she did not see.
- **Near the Proprietor her pulse falls to his count (57)** and her Ember and still door want to fall in; she
  held them shut.
- **The dream:** not recurred; **known to nobody new.** The ferry detail of lights "gone white" is **Kiva's
  alone** (not told to Mara). "Unroofed Sky" and "Breach" do not appear.
- **Refused** the Proprietor's request for the three-door on a Halden mast (price: forty drams to Seven's
  book; a seat on his roll at a Torch's wage). **Three doors: three clean; not done.**
- **Not done:** Ember-to-still crossing; stack; the handed crossing live (one yard rehearsal only).

- Dray's rule read the Ropewalk's lays as one line; Noa entered "a stack… an incident." **The Clerk's ruling
